Nested Nonparametric Instrumental Variable Regression
Isaac Meza, Rahul Singh

Abstract
Several causal parameters in short panel data models are functionals of a nested nonparametric instrumental variable regression (nested NPIV). Recent examples include mediated, time varying, and long term treatment effects identified using proxy variables. In econometrics, examples arise in triangular simultaneous equations and hedonic price systems. However, it appears that explicit mean square convergence rates for nested NPIV are unknown, preventing inference on some of these parameters with generic machine learning. A major challenge is compounding ill posedness due to the nested inverse problems. To limit how ill posedness compounds, we introduce two techniques: relative well posedness, and multiple robustness to ill posedness. With these techniques, we provide explicit mean square rates for nested NPIV and efficient inference for recently identified causal parameters. Our nonasymptotic analysis accommodates neural networks, random forests, and reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces. It extends to causal functions, e.g. heterogeneous long term treatment effects.



Keywords:
heterogeneous treatment effect, ill posed inverse problem, proxy variable, semiparametric efficiency, short panel data

1
Introduction and related work

Mediated, time varying, and long term treatment effects are causal parameters defined in short panel data models. In the presence of unobserved confounding, e.g. latent ability, several recent works have proposed nonparametric identification strategies for these causal parameters using auxiliary variables called proxies that satisfy relevance and exclusion conditions
(
Miao et al., 2018
;
Deaner, 2018
;
Dukes et al., 2023
;
Ying et al., 2023
;
Ghassami et al., 2022a
;
Imbens et al., 2025
)
. Across settings, each causal parameter
\theta_{0}
turns out to be a scalar summary of a
nested
nonparametric instrumental variable regression (nested NPIV) function
h_{0}
.
Structural parameters in several econometric models have a similar form, e.g. parameters in triangular simultaneous equations and hedonic price systems
(
Hausman, 1977
;
Wooldridge, 1996
;
Newey et al., 1999
;
Ekeland et al., 2004
;
Ai and Chen, 2007
)
.

A nested NPIV function
h_{0}
is a solution to an inverse problem of the form
\mathbb{E}\{h(B)|C\}=\mathbb{E}\{g_{0}(A)|C\}
, where
g_{0}
is itself an NPIV function that solves an inverse problem.
For example,
g_{0}
is a solution to
\mathbb{E}\{g(A)|C^{\prime}\}=\mathbb{E}(Y|C^{\prime})
. In general,
C\not\subset C^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime}\not\subset C
.
Solving each equation requires inverting a conditional expectation operator, which is ill posed.
The nested NPIV
h_{0}
is more challenging than the NPIV
g_{0}
because ill posedness of the nested inverse problems may compound, in potentially complex ways.
Our research question is how to derive mean square rates for nested NPIV, and how to conduct inference on its functionals, with machine learning.

Contributions
. Our primary contribution is to derive mean square rates for the nested NPIV function
h_{0}
over general function spaces, e.g. neural networks, random forests, and reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces (RKHSs).
It appears that previous mean square consistency results for the nested NPIV function
h_{0}
are specific to series estimation, and do not give an explicit mean square rate of convergence
(
Ai and Chen, 2007
)
; they could arbitrarily slow.
Stronger results, namely mean square rates, are necessary to conduct semiparametric inference for the causal parameter
\theta_{0}
using machine learning.

We derive increasingly strong rate results under increasingly strong assumptions. For mean square rates, we assume that (i) the function space
\mathcal{H}
used in estimation is not too complex, satisfying a critical radius condition that is standard in the
M
estimation literature; and (ii)
h_{0}
is smooth, satisfying a source condition that is standard in the NPIV literature. For faster mean square rates, we formulate what appears to be a new condition: (iii) the nested inverse problems have a well behaved
relative
measure of ill posedness.

As a secondary contribution, we translate our nested NPIV mean square convergence rates into guarantees for causal inference in short panel models, with sharper dependence on ill posedness.
Similar to previous works on targeted and debiased machine learning, we combine
(\hat{h},\hat{g})
and their dual analogues into an estimator
\hat{\theta}

(
Zheng and van der Laan, 2011
;
Chernozhukov et al., 2018
;
Chernozhukov et al., 2022a
)
. Unlike previous works, we prove
\hat{\theta}

is
multiple
robust to ill posedness:
it tolerates moderate ill posedness of multiple inverse problems, as long as other inverse problems are mildly ill posed, generalizing techniques previously developed in cross sectional models
(
Chernozhukov et al., 2023
)
.

Our results apply to not only causal scalars but also causal functions in panel settings, e.g. heterogeneous long term treatment effects. Our class of causal parameters includes several for which machine learning estimation and inference were not previously given; see Section
2
.

Our techniques uncover new insights in economic data. We extend the program evaluation of the US Job Corps, which randomly assigned eligibility for job training. A previous, parametric approach corrects for latent motivation with proxy variables, and finds a zero or imprecise change in arrests directly due to job training.
1
1

1

This direct effect is the component of the total effect that is not mediated by employment.
Our RKHS approach finds a small and precise decrease in arrests directly due to job training. We also extend the program evaluation of the Tennessee Student Teacher Achievement Ratio experiment (Project STAR), which randomly assigned kindergarten students to small or large class sizes. We document heterogeneity in the long term effects of enrollment in a small class, with the strongest long term effects for students with the lowest prior scores.

Related work.
We contribute new estimation and inference results to the literature on simultaneous equation models with different instruments for different equations
(
Wooldridge, 1996
;
Ai and Chen, 2007
)
. Much early work focused on parametric three stage least squares
(
Amemiya, 1977
)
or optimal instruments when
C^{\prime}\subset C

(
Chamberlain, 1992
;
Brown and Newey, 1998
;
Ai and Chen, 2012
)
. We study a version of the problem motivated by short panel proxy models: an NPIV
g_{0}
with instrument
C^{\prime}
enters the conditional moment for the nested NPIV
h_{0}
with instrument
C
, where possibly
C\not\subset C^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime}\not\subset C

(
Dukes et al., 2023
;
Ying et al., 2023
;
Ghassami et al., 2022a
;
Imbens et al., 2025
)
. Its essential feature is that ill posedness compounds. This key challenge is the reason why prior works on proxies define the nested NPIV, but pose as a question how to derive its mean square rate.
The key challenge is assumed away in panel models with Markov or linear factor structure
(
Deaner, 2018
;
Imbens et al., 2021
)
.
Compared to
Ai and Chen, 2007
’s series analysis, we study a different machine learning estimator and provide different main results: explicit mean square rates, and inference beyond Donsker spaces.

Our critical radius and source assumptions generalize those of classical NPIV
(
Newey and Powell, 2003
;
Ai and Chen, 2003
;
Hall and Horowitz, 2005
;
Blundell et al., 2007
;
Darolles et al., 2011
;
Chen and Reiss, 2011
;
Chen and Pouzo, 2012
;
Santos, 2012
;
Severini and Tripathi, 2012
)
, while our relative well posedness assumption appears to be new.
Similar to many NPIV papers, we use the source condition to control the bias of Tikhonov regularization
(
Darolles et al., 2011
;
Hall and Horowitz, 2005
;
Horowitz and Lee, 2005
;
Carrasco et al., 2007
;
Chen and Pouzo, 2012
;
Gagliardini and Scaillet, 2012
;
Singh et al., 2019
)
.

Whereas we provide adversarial estimation and inference results for nested NPIV and panel models, previous works provide adversarial estimation and inference results for NPIV and cross sectional models. These earlier results do not face the key challenge of compounding ill posedness, and do not apply to our setting. For NPIV, several works prove projected mean square error
(
Dikkala et al., 2020
)
and mean square error
(
Liao et al., 2020
;
Bennett et al., 2023a
;
Bennett et al., 2023b
;
Bennett et al., 2023c
)
rates under critical radius and source conditions.
2
2

2

See references therein for the vast literature on NPIV with non-adversarial machine learning, as well as adversarial approaches to NPIV without rate guarantees.

By contrast, we prove such rates for nested NPIV and introduce relative well posedness.
For treatment effects in cross sections, several works prove Gaussian approximation
(
Hirshberg and Wager, 2021
;
Chernozhukov et al., 2020
;
Kallus et al., 2021
;
Ghassami et al., 2022b
)
. By contrast, we prove Gaussian approximation for treatment effects in short panels, which involve nested inverse problems.

An earlier draft circulated under a different title
(
Singh, 2021
)
.
This paper appears to be the first to:
(i) propose nested NPIV estimators over general machine function spaces with mean square rates;
(ii) define a notion of relative well posedness across inverse problems;
(iii) prove inference for machine learning estimators of proxy mediation analysis, heterogeneous long term effects, and other important causal parameters;
(iv) characterize multiple robustness to ill posedness.
None of (i), (ii), (iii), or (iv) appear to be contained in previous works.

Structure.
Section
2
demonstrates that several important causal parameters are functionals of a nested NPIV.
Section
3
proposes our machine learning procedure.
Section
4
proves new mean square rate guarantees, with and without the technique of relative well posedness.
Section
5
translates the rates into semiparametric inference on causal parameters, with multiple robustness to ill posedness.
Section
6
presents real world applications: the proxy direct treatment effect of the US Job Corps, and heterogeneous long term treatment effects of Project STAR.

2
Examples in statistics and econometrics

The nested NPIV is a recurring challenge in several areas of statistics and econometrics, which motivates our investigation. In this section, we list some salient examples from the modern causal inference literature and the traditional economic literature on supply and demand estimation. To the best of our knowledge, this paper provides the first explicit estimation and inference guarantees with machine learning for Examples
1
,
2
, and
4
below.

The initial examples use proxy variables, i.e. auxiliary variables that satisfy relevance and exclusion conditions, to adjust for unobserved confounding in short panel models. Throughout these examples, we denote covariates, treatments, and outcomes by
(X,D,Y)
. Treatment and outcome proxies are
(Z,W)
. When applicable, the mediators are
M
. The potential outcomes are
Y^{(d)}
or
Y^{(d,m)}
, and the potential mediators are
M^{(d)}
.

Example 1

(Proxy mediation analysis)
.

Let
h_{0}
be an outcome confounding bridge that solves the inverse problem
\mathbb{E}\{h(X,D,W)|X,Z,D=0\}=\mathbb{E}\{g_{0}(X,D=1,M,W)|X,Z,D=0\}
, where
g_{0}
solves the inverse problem

\mathbb{E}\{g(X,D,M,W)|X,Z,D=1,M\}=\mathbb{E}(Y|X,Z,D=1,M)
. Under proxy variable assumptions
(
Dukes et al., 2023
)
, the pure direct effect
\textsc{direct}=\mathbb{E}[Y^{\{1,M^{(0)}\}}]
is a functional of
h_{0}
, and
h_{0}
is a term in its efficient influence function.
3
3

3

This definition follows
Robins and Greenland, 1992
. See
Richardson and Robins, 2013
for alternatives.

Concretely, we may wish to measure the direct effect of US Job Corps job training
D
on subsequent arrests
Y
that is not mediated by employment
M
. Unobserved motivation
U
may confound employment and arrests. Researchers have used the time spent with a Job Corps recruiter as an auxiliary variable
Z
that reflects motivation yet does not directly cause employment or arrests. Researchers have used pre-training expectations as an auxiliary variable
W
that reflects motivation yet cannot be caused by training or employment. We replicate and extend this empirical strategy in Section
6
, relaxing previous parametric assumptions.

In the next example, we index variables by the time period when they are observed.

Example 2

(Proxy time varying treatment effect)
.

Let
h_{0}
be an outcome confounding bridge that solves the inverse problem

\mathbb{E}\{h(X_{1},D_{1},W_{1},D_{2}=d_{2})|X_{1},Z_{1},D_{1}=d_{1}\}=\mathbb{E}\{g_{0}(X_{1},D_{1},W_{1},X_{2},D_{2}=d_{2},W_{2})|X_{1},Z_{1},D_{1}=d_{1}\}
,
where
g_{0}
solves the inverse problem

\mathbb{E}\{g(X_{1},D_{1},W_{1},X_{2},D_{2},W_{2})|X_{1},Z_{1},D_{1}=d_{1},X_{2},Z_{2},D_{2}=d_{2}\}=\mathbb{E}(Y|X_{1},Z_{1},D_{1}=d_{1},X_{2},Z_{2},D_{2}=d_{2}).

Under proxy variable assumptions
(
Ying et al., 2023
)
, the time varying treatment effect
\textsc{time}(d_{1},d_{2})=\mathbb{E}\{Y^{(d_{1},d_{2})}\}
is a functional of
h_{0}
, and
h_{0}
is a term in its efficient influence function.

When combining short term experimental data
(G=\textsc{exp})
with long term observational data
(G=\textsc{obs})
for long term causal inference, a similar statistical problem arises.

Example 3

(Proxy long term effect)
.

Let
h_{0}
be an outcome confounding bridge that solves the problem

h_{0}(X,D,G=\textsc{exp})=\mathbb{E}\{g_{0}(X,D,M,W)|X,D,G=\textsc{exp}\},

where

g_{0}
solves the inverse problem

\mathbb{E}\{g(X,D,M,W)|X,D,Z,M,G=\textsc{obs}\}=\mathbb{E}(Y|X,D,Z,M,G=\textsc{obs})
.
Under proxy variable assumptions
(
Ghassami et al., 2022b
;
Imbens et al., 2025
)
, the long term effect

\textsc{long}=\mathbb{E}\{Y^{(d)}|G=\textsc{obs}\}
is a functional of
h_{0}
, and
h_{0}
is a term in its efficient influence function.

Similar to Example
1
, Examples
2
and
3
use auxiliary variables to detect and correct for unobserved confounding.
While Examples
1
and
2
each have two inverse problems, Example
3
has one.
It is a special case of our framework, since a nonparametric regression is a special case of an NPIV.
4
4

4

In the notation of Section
1
,
B=C
in Example
3
, so
h(C)=\mathbb{E}\{h(C)|C\}=\mathbb{E}\{g_{0}(A)|C\}
.

Example
3
was proposed subsequently to the initial draft of this paper, highlighting how proxy panel models are an active area of research, generating new nested NPIV examples.
5
5

5

Some additional, subsequent examples include
Park et al., 2024
;
Bai et al., 2025
. Neither work provides a mean square rate for the nested NPIV, highlighting the importance of our main result.

The examples so far are causal scalars. Our results extend to causal functions, e.g. heterogeneous treatment effects. For clarity, we set aside proxy variables and let
X_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
be the baseline covariate of interest used to index heterogeneity. Note that a nested regression is another special case of a nested NPIV.
6
6

6

In the notation of Section
1
,
A=C^{\prime}
and
B=C
in Example
4
.

Example 4

(Heterogeneous long term effects)
.

Let
h_{0}
be an outcome bridge that solves the problem

h_{0}(X_{1},X_{2},D)=\mathbb{E}\{g_{0}(X_{1},X_{2},M,G=\textsc{obs})|X_{1},X_{2},D,G=\textsc{exp}\}
,
where
g_{0}
is the outcome mechanism

g_{0}(X_{1},X_{2},M,G)=\mathbb{E}(Y|X_{1},X_{2},M,G).

Under surrogate variable assumptions
(
Athey et al., 2019
)
, the heterogeneous long term effects
\textsc{long}(x_{1})=\mathbb{E}\{Y^{(d)}|X_{1}=x_{1}\}=\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow 0}\mathbb{E}\{\ell_{\lambda}(X_{1})Y^{(d)}\}
are functionals of
\ell_{\lambda}h_{0}
, and
\ell_{\lambda}h_{0}
is a term in their influence functions.

Example
4
generalizes from average to heterogeneous effects by introducing
a local weighting
\ell_{\lambda}(X_{1})
around the value
x_{1}
with bandwidth
\lambda
. Formally,

\ell_{\lambda}(X_{1})=\omega^{-1}K\{(X_{1}-x_{1})/\lambda\}
, where
K
is a bounded symmetric kernel that integrates to one, and
\omega=\mathbb{E}[K\{(X_{1}-x_{1})/\lambda\}]
is its normalization. Our results complement those of
Kallus et al., 2018
, who study a different problem. Our results also apply to heterogeneous direct effects and heterogeneous time varying effects.

Concretely, we may wish to extrapolate long term effects of kindergarten class size
D
on middle school test scores
Y
. The experimental group
(G=\textsc{exp})
are students in Project STAR, for whom we see elementary school test scores
M
rather than middle school test scores
Y
. The observational group
(G=\textsc{exp})
are students in New York City public schools, for whom we see elementary and middle school test scores but not kindergarten class size. In Section
6
, we ask whether the long term effects of kindergarten class size vary based on baseline aptitude
X_{1}
.

By taking
\ell_{\lambda}(X_{1})=1
, we provide estimation and inference for the average long term effect with machine learning. This modest yet practical contribution builds on the efficient influence function of
Chen and Ritzwoller, 2021
.
7
7

7

Contemporaneously to
Singh, 2021
,
Chen and Ritzwoller, 2022
provide a similar result for average long term effects, but not for heterogeneous long term effects.

Finally, we turn to a classic example in economics. Consider the equations
Q=\textsc{demand}(P,W_{Q})+U_{Q}
and
P=\textsc{supply}(Q,W_{P})+U_{P}
, where
Q
is quantity sold,
P
is price,
W_{Q}
is consumer income,
W_{P}
is input price, and
(U_{Q},U_{P})
are unobservable demand and supply shocks
(
Matzkin, 2008
)
. A standard simultaneous equation model would consider
(W_{Q},W_{P})
to be exogenous:
\mathbb{E}(U_{Q}|W_{Q},W_{P})=0
and
\mathbb{E}(U_{P}|W_{Q},W_{P})=0
.
A generalization that tolerates omitted variables is
\mathbb{E}(U_{Q}|Z_{Q},W_{P})=0
and
\mathbb{E}(U_{P}|W_{Q},Z_{P})=0
; in the terminology of econometrics,
Z_{Q}
instruments for
W_{Q}
, and
Z_{P}
instruments for
W_{P}

(
Hausman, 1977
)
.

Example 5

(Simultaneous equations with omitted variables)
.

Let
h_{0}
solve the inverse problem
\mathbb{E}\{h(P,Q,W_{P},W_{Q})|W_{Q},Z_{P}\}=\mathbb{E}\{g_{0}(P,W_{Q})|W_{Q},Z_{P}\}
, where
g_{0}
solves the inverse problem
\mathbb{E}\{g(P,W_{Q})|Z_{Q},W_{P}\}=\mathbb{E}(Q|Z_{Q},W_{P})
. Under instrumental variable assumptions
(
Hausman, 1977
)
, the demand curve
\textsc{demand}(P,W_{Q})
is
g_{0}(P,W_{Q})
and the supply curve
\textsc{supply}(Q,W_{P})
is
h_{0}(P,Q,W_{P},W_{Q})-\textsc{demand}(P,W_{Q})+P
. Elasticities are their average derivatives.

3
Nested NPIV estimators over general spaces

While there are several causal and structural models which give rise to nested NPIVs, they are all unified by the following problem:
h_{0}
solves
\mathbb{E}\{h(B)|C\}=\mathbb{E}\{g_{0}(A)|C\}
, where
g_{0}
solves
\mathbb{E}\{g(A)|C^{\prime}\}=\mathbb{E}(Y|C^{\prime})
, and in general
C\not\subset C^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime}\not\subset C
.
8
8

8

In general, the solutions are not unique; our algorithms will target the minimal norms solutions.

Conditions for
h_{0}
to exist are well documented in prior work, using Picard’s criterion
(
Kress, 1989
, Theorem 15.18)
.
In this section, we define two natural estimators of the nested NPIV. Each estimator is defined over a general space
\mathcal{H}
, e.g. a neural network, random forest, or RKHS.

Since the nested NPIV reduces to NPIV when
g_{0}(A)=Y
, a
sequential
approach replaces
Y
with an initial NPIV estimator
\hat{g}(A)
to estimate
\hat{h}(B)
. Building on an extensive literature for adversarial NPIV estimation,
(
Dikkala et al., 2020
;
Liao et al., 2020
;
Bennett et al., 2023b
;
Bennett et al., 2023c
, e.g.)
, we define a sequential nested NPIV estimator as follows.

Algorithm 1

(Sequential nested NPIV)
.

Given observations
(A_{i},B_{i},C_{i})
, an initial estimator
\hat{g}
that may be estimated on the same data, and a hyperparameter
\mu>0
,

\hat{h}=\argmin_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\left[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\left\{2\cdot\textsc{loss}(f,\hat{g},h)-\textsc{penalty}(f)\right\}+\mu\cdot\textsc{penalty}(h)\right],

where
\textsc{loss}(f,g,h)=\mathbb{E}_{n}[\{h(B)-g(A)\}f(C)]
,
\textsc{penalty}(f)=\mathbb{E}_{n}\{f(C)^{2}\}
,
\textsc{penalty}(h)=\mathbb{E}_{n}\{h(B)^{2}\}
, and
\mathbb{E}_{n}(\cdot)
averages over observations.

To interpret the estimator, recall that the
inverse problem that defines
h_{0}
may be viewed as the conditional moment restriction
\mathbb{E}\{h_{0}(B)-g_{0}(A)|C\}=0
, which implies a continuum of unconditional moment restrictions
\mathbb{E}[\{h_{0}(B)-g_{0}(A)\}f(C)]=0
for all square integrable
f\in\mathbb{L}_{2}
. The adversary attempts to violate the empirical moments by searching for the worst counterexample
f
. Our estimator attempts to preserve the empirical moments, by searching for an estimate
h
that is the most robust to the adversary’s worst counterexample. The following corollary shows that the penalty terms normalize the game in the right way.

Corollary 1

(Sequential limit)
.

Consider the population limit of Algorithm
1
where
n\rightarrow\infty
,
\mu\rightarrow 0
, and
\hat{g}
converges to
g_{0}
:
\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\mathbb{E}[2\{h(B)-g_{0}(A)\}f(C)-f(C)^{2}]
. If
\mathbb{E}\{h(B)-h_{0}(B)|C=(\cdot)\}\in\mathcal{F}
, then this limit equals
\mathbb{E}([\mathbb{E}\{h(B)-h_{0}(B)|C\}]^{2})
.

In the final expression, the limit is the mean square error of the projection of
h(B)-h_{0}(B)
onto the instrument
C
. It is the well known weak metric in the NPIV literature
(
Ai and Chen, 2003
, eq. 14)
. Thus Algorithm
1
’s empirical criterion converges to the weak metric, even though an analyst does not have access to unbiased samples from
h_{0}(B)
.

The sequential approach is largely agnostic about the initial estimator
\hat{g}
. On the one hand, its analysis will be simpler. On the other hand, it presupposes that
\hat{g}
converges to
g_{0}
in a mean square sense, so ill posedness in estimating
g_{0}
may sequentially compound when estimating
h_{0}
. Towards a better dependence on ill posedness, we define a simultaneous approach that jointly estimates
\hat{g}(A)
and
\hat{h}(B)
, in the spirit of
Ai and Chen, 2007
.

Algorithm 2

(Simultaneous nested NPIV)
.

Given observations
(A_{i},B_{i},C_{i},C_{i}^{\prime})
and hyperparameter values
(\mu^{\prime},\mu)
,

\displaystyle(\hat{g},\hat{h})

\displaystyle=\argmin_{g\in\mathcal{G},h\in\mathcal{H}}\bigg[\sup_{f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}\left\{2\cdot\textsc{loss}(f^{\prime},Y,g)-\textsc{penalty}(f^{\prime})\right\}+\mu^{\prime}\cdot\textsc{penalty}(g)

\displaystyle\quad+\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\left\{2\cdot\textsc{loss}(f,g,h)-\textsc{penalty}(f)\right\}+\mu\cdot\textsc{penalty}(h)\bigg],

where
\textsc{loss}(f^{\prime},Y,g)=\mathbb{E}_{n}[\{g(A)-Y\}f^{\prime}(C^{\prime})]
. The remaining terms are defined as before.

As before, the inverse problem that defines
h_{0}
implies a continuum of unconditional moment restrictions
\mathbb{E}[\{h_{0}(B)-g_{0}(A)\}f(C)]=0
. Now,
the inverse problem that defines
g_{0}
implies another continuum of unconditional moment restrictions
\mathbb{E}[\{g_{0}(A)-Y\}f^{\prime}(C^{\prime})]=0
. The adversary attempts to violate the empirical moments by searching for the worst counterexamples
(f^{\prime},f)
, while our estimator attempts to preserve them by searching for robust estimates
(g,h)
. Crucially,
g
must be chosen to balance the two aspects of the game.

Corollary 2

(Simultaneous limit)
.

Consider the population limit of Algorithm
2
where
n\rightarrow\infty
,
\mu,\mu^{\prime}\rightarrow 0
:

\sup_{f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}}\mathbb{E}[2\{g(A)-Y\}f^{\prime}(C^{\prime})-f^{\prime}(C^{\prime})^{2}]+\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\mathbb{E}[2\{h(B)-g(A)\}f(C)-f(C)^{2}].

If
\mathbb{E}\{g(A)-g_{0}(A)|C^{\prime}=(\cdot)\}\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
,
\mathbb{E}\{h(B)-h_{0}(B)|C=(\cdot)\}\in\mathcal{F}
, and
\mathbb{E}\{g(B)-g_{0}(B)|C=(\cdot)\}\in\mathcal{F}
,
then it equals
\mathbb{E}([\mathbb{E}\{g(A)-g_{0}(A)|C^{\prime}\}]^{2})+\mathbb{E}\{(\mathbb{E}[\{h(B)-h_{0}(B)\}-\{g(A)-g_{0}(A)\}|C])^{2}\}
.

The limit contains two terms: (i) the bias of
g
projected onto the initial instrument
C^{\prime}
; (ii) the gap between the biases of
h
and
g
projected onto the nested instrument
C
. The limit coincides with the generalized weak metric of
Ai and Chen, 2003
and
Ai and Chen, 2007
, i.e. the metric in which those authors provide rates for series estimators. By contrast, we provide rates in mean square error. Moreover, we show that if
(C^{\prime},C)
are compatible in some way, then these various biases can be small and compounding ill posedness can be avoided.

4
Main result: Mean square rates

Our main contribution is explicit mean square rates of convergence for machine learning estimators of nested NPIV, i.e.
\mathbb{E}[\{\hat{h}(B)-h_{0}(B)\}^{2}]
. Such a result is a crucial building block for efficient inference on the functionals in Section
2
. We provide rates for Algorithm
1
using critical radius and source conditions, then faster rates for Algorithm
2
under critical radius, source, and relative well posedness conditions. Relative well posedness appears to be a novel concept.

We denote the conditional expectation operators
T(h,g)=\mathbb{E}\{h(B)-g(A)|C=(\cdot)\}
and
S(g)=\mathbb{E}\{g(A)|C^{\prime}=(\cdot)\}
.
Since
(g_{0},h_{0})
may be non-unique, we take
(g_{0},h_{0})
to be solutions to
T(h,g)=0
and
S(g)=\mathbb{E}(Y|C^{\prime}=\cdot)
with the smallest second moments.
We assume they exist. Explicit conditions for their existence are given in the various identification papers listed in Section
2
.
To lighten notation, we also write
T_{h}(h)=T(h,0)
and
T_{g}(g)=T(0,g)
. Finally, we write the
\mathbb{L}_{2}
norm as
\|\tilde{f}\|^{2}=\mathbb{E}\{\tilde{f}(\tilde{A})^{2}\}
.

Throughout, we assume that the data are independent and identically distributed. Future work may relax this simplifying assumption.

4.1
Rates with compounding ill posedness

Permissive assumption: Critical radius.
We assume that our estimator
\hat{h}
is not too complex in a familiar sense.
Consider a generic function space
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
that contains functions
f:\tilde{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
which are uniformly and absolutely bounded by one.
9
9

9

This can be relaxed to functions that have some finite bound by a rescaling argument.
Let
\epsilon_{i}
be independent random variables taking values on
\{-1,1\}
equiprobably. The local Rademacher complexity of
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
over a neighborhood of radius
\delta
is
\mathcal{R}_{n}(\tilde{\mathcal{F}},\delta)=\mathbb{E}\left\{\sup_{f\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}:\|f\|\leq\delta}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}f(\tilde{A}_{i})\right\}
. Building on a mature
M
estimation literature, we bound
\|\hat{h}-h_{0}\|
in terms of local Rademacher complexities.

We optimize these bounds into fast rates by an appropriate choice of the radius
\delta
, called the critical radius. The critical radius
\delta_{n}
is the smallest possible solution to the inequality
\mathcal{R}_{n}\{\textsc{star}(\tilde{\mathcal{F}}),\delta\}\leq\delta^{2}
, where
\textsc{star}(\tilde{\mathcal{F}})=\{cf:f\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}},c\in[0,1]\}
is the star hull of
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
. For many spaces, it is a converging sequence as
n\uparrow\infty
.

Assumption 1

(Critical radius)
.

For the space
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
, which we will instantiate in various ways below, its critical radius converges:
\delta_{n}=\tilde{O}(n^{-\alpha})
for some
\alpha\in(0,1/2]
, where
\tilde{O}(\cdot)
means
O(\cdot)
up to logarithmic factors.

Example 6

(Gaussian kernel)
.

Let
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
be the unit ball in the RKHS with the Gaussian kernel, with uniform data over
[0,1]
. Then
\delta_{n}=\sqrt{\frac{\ln(n)}{n}}
and
\alpha=1/2
.

Example 7

(Neural network)
.

Let
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
be a rectified linear unit (ReLU) neural network with depth
L
and a total of
W
parameters.
10
10

10

For a multilayer perceptron with depth
L
and width
H
,
W=O(H^{2}L)
.
Then
\delta_{n}=\sqrt{\frac{LW\ln(W)\ln(n)}{n}}
and
\alpha<1/2
.

See e.g.
Foster and Syrgkanis, 2023
for the critical radii of other RKHSs, other neural networks, random forests, and sparse linear spaces, demonstrating that the condition is quite permissive. See e.g.
Wainwright, 2019
for an equivalent entropy integral condition. See
Chernozhukov et al., 2020
for a detailed exposition of how the critical radius condition relaxes the Donsker condition to accommodate “simple” machine learning.

Appendix
\thechapter.B
gives projected mean squares rates using only critical radius conditions.

Familiar assumption: Source condition.
To pass from a projected mean square rate to a mean square rate, i.e. from
\|T_{h}(\hat{h}-h_{0})\|^{2}=\mathbb{E}([\mathbb{E}\{\hat{h}(B)-h_{0}(B)|C\}]^{2})
to
\|\hat{h}-h_{0}\|^{2}=\mathbb{E}[\{\hat{h}(B)-h_{0}(B)\}^{2}]
,
we assume that the nested NPIV
h_{0}
is smooth in a familiar sense. In particular, we assume it well approximated by the top of the spectrum of the conditional expectation operator
T_{h}(h)=\mathbb{E}\{h(B)|C=(\cdot)\}
. To state the condition, we denote the adjoint operator by
T_{h}^{*}
.

Assumption 2

(Source)
.

h_{0}=(T_{h}^{*}T_{h})^{\beta_{h}/2}w_{h}
for some
w_{h}\in\mathcal{H}
and some
\beta_{h}\in(0,\infty)
.

To interpret Assumption
2
, suppose
T_{h}
admits a singular value decomposition
(\sigma_{j},u_{j},v_{j})
, so that
T_{h}h=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\sigma_{j}\langle h,v_{i}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}_{2}}u_{j}
. Assumption
2
is equivalent to imposing that
h_{0}\in\mathcal{H}^{\beta_{h}}=\{h\in\mathbb{L}_{2}:\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}1_{\sigma_{j}\neq 0}\cdot\sigma_{j}^{-2\beta_{h}}\langle h,v_{j}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}_{2}}^{2}<\infty\}
, where
\mathbb{L}_{2}
is the space of square integrable functions. Clearly,
\mathcal{H}^{0}=\mathbb{L}_{2}
when each
\sigma_{j}\neq 0
. For
\beta_{h}>0
,
\mathcal{H}^{\beta_{h}}
is a subset of
\mathcal{H}^{0}
. Due to the singular value penalty
\sigma_{j}^{-2\beta_{h}}
, it consists of functions that are not too aligned with the higher order right singular functions
(v_{j})
. Intuitively, it rules out “rough” functions primarily supported on the tail of the spectrum of
T_{h}
. In this way, it limits the ill posedness of the inverse problem.

To lighten notation, let
\textsc{well}(\beta)=\frac{\min(\beta,1)}{\min(\beta,1)+1}
be a measure of well posedness. At best,
\beta\geq 1
and
\textsc{well}(\beta)=1/2
. At worst,
\beta\rightarrow 0
and
\textsc{well}(\beta)\rightarrow 0
. Our final rates will depend on the well posedness
\textsc{well}(\beta_{h})
.

See e.g.
Chen and Reiss, 2011

for comparisons of source conditions employed in the NPIV
literature.
In their series analysis,
Ai and Chen, 2007
place an assumption equivalent to
\beta_{h}=2

(
Bennett et al., 2023a
, Proposition 5)
and prove mean square consistency, whereas we consider
\beta_{h}<2
and prove mean square rates.

Compounding ill posedness throttles rates.
Under Assumptions
1
and
2
, we derive rates for
\|\hat{h}-h_{0}\|^{2}
that are throttled twice: by the ill posedness of inverting
S
and then
T
. To simplify exposition, we assume that the conditional expectation operator
T
is correctly specified by the adversary’s function space
\mathcal{F}
in Algorithm
1
. This simplifying condition can be relaxed, incurring an additive approximation error; see our earlier draft.

Assumption 3

(Closedness)
.

T(h-h_{0},g-g_{0})\in\mathcal{F}
for all
h\in\mathcal{H}
and
g\in\mathcal{G}
.

Theorem 1

(Bound for Algorithm
1
)
.

Suppose Assumption
1
holds for
\mathcal{F}
,
\mathcal{G}
,
\mathcal{H}
, and
\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{F}
;
Assumption
2
holds; and Assumption
3
holds. Then with probability
1-\zeta
, when
\mu=O(1)
and
\delta_{n}=\Omega[\{\ln\ln(n)+\ln(1/\zeta)\}^{1/2}n^{-1/2}]
, we have

\|T_{h}(\hat{h}-h_{0})\|^{2}=O(R_{n})
and
\|\hat{h}-h_{0}\|^{2}=O(\mu^{-1}R_{n})
, where

R_{n}=\mu^{\min(\beta_{h}+1,2)}\|w_{h}\|^{2}+\delta_{n}^{2}+\|\hat{g}-g_{0}\|^{2}.
The former conclusion,
\|T_{h}(\hat{h}-h_{0})\|^{2}=O(R_{n})
, also holds without Assumption
1
applied to
\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{F}
.

Theorem
1
is our first main result: a mean square rate for nested NPIV.
The rate
R_{n}
contains bias
\mu^{\min(\beta_{h}+1,2)}\|w_{h}\|
, variance
\delta_{n}^{2}
, and initial estimation error
\|\hat{g}-g_{0}\|^{2}
.
It generalizes known results for NPIV: without the initial estimation error term, it recovers NPIV rates in mean square error
(
Liao et al., 2020
;
Bennett et al., 2023b
;
Bennett et al., 2023c
)
and projected mean square error
(
Dikkala et al., 2020
)
. It seems to strengthen known projected mean square results for NPIV since it relaxes Assumption
1
from
\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{F}
to
\mathcal{H}
, which may be of independent interest.

Theorem
1
cleanly separates the ill posedness of
\hat{g}
from
\hat{h}
. The ill posedness of
\hat{g}
appears via
\|\hat{g}-g_{0}\|^{2}
, which is additively separable from the other terms in
R_{n}
. The projected mean square rate
R_{n}
is not affected by the ill posedness of
\hat{h}
. However, the rate for mean square error is slower than the rate for projected mean square error by a factor of
\mu^{-1}
, encoding the ill posedness of inverting
T_{h}
to isolate the nested NPIV.

The rates may be optimized by choosing
\mu
to balance the bias term with the other terms.

Corollary 3

(Rate for Algorithm
1
)
.

Suppose the conditions of Theorem
1
hold. Take
\mu=\max(\delta_{n},\|\hat{g}-g_{0}\|)^{\frac{2}{\min(\beta_{h},1)+1}}
. Then with probability
1-\zeta
,
\|T_{h}(\hat{h}-h_{0})\|^{2}=O\left\{\max(\delta_{n}^{2},\|\hat{g}-g_{0}\|^{2})\right\}
and
\|\hat{h}-h_{0}\|^{2}=O\left\{\max(\delta_{n}^{2},\|\hat{g}-g_{0}\|^{2})^{\textsc{well}(\beta_{h})}\right\}
.

For concreteness, we instantiate a well known NPIV estimator for
g_{0}
, namely the NPIV special case of Algorithm
1
. We place assumptions for
g_{0}
analogous to those for
h_{0}
.

Assumption 4

(Source)
.

g_{0}=(S^{*}S)^{\beta^{\prime}_{g}/2}w^{\prime}_{g}
for some
w_{g}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}
and some
\beta^{\prime}_{g}\in(0,\infty)
.

Assumption 5

(Closedness)
.

S(g-g_{0})\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
for all
g\in\mathcal{G}
.

Corollary 4

(Compounding ill posedness)
.

Suppose the conditions of Theorem
1
hold, extending Assumption
1
to
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{G}\times\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
; and
Assumptions
4
and
5
hold. Write the regularizations as
(\mu_{g},\mu_{h})
. Set
\mu_{g}=\delta_{n}^{\frac{2}{\min(\beta^{\prime}_{g},1)+1}}
and
\mu_{h}=\delta_{n}^{\frac{2}{\min(\beta_{h},1)+1}\textsc{well}(\beta^{\prime}_{g})}
. Then with probability
1-\zeta
,
\|T_{h}(\hat{h}-h_{0})\|^{2}=O\left\{\delta_{n}^{2\textsc{well}(\beta^{\prime}_{g})}\right\}
and
\|\hat{h}-h_{0}\|^{2}=O\left\{\delta_{n}^{2\textsc{well}(\beta_{h})\textsc{well}(\beta^{\prime}_{g})}\right\}
.

Example 6

(Gaussian kernel)
.

By Corollary
4
,

\|\hat{h}-h_{0}\|^{2}=O_{p}\left[\left\{\frac{\ln(n)}{n}\right\}^{\textsc{well}(\beta_{h})\textsc{well}(\beta^{\prime}_{g})}\right]
.

Example 7

(Neural network)
.

By Corollary
4
,

\|\hat{h}-h_{0}\|^{2}=O_{p}\left[\left\{\frac{LW\ln(W)\ln(n)}{n}\right\}^{\textsc{well}(\beta_{h})\textsc{well}(\beta^{\prime}_{g})}\right]
.

Ill posedness compounds in a simple way for Algorithm
1
: our mean square rate is a “base rate”
\delta_{n}^{2}
slowed by the ill posedness of each inverse problem via the product
\textsc{well}(\beta^{\prime}_{g})\cdot\textsc{well}(\beta_{h})
. Recall that
\delta_{n}^{2}
is
\tilde{O}(n^{-1})
for parametric classes, and it converges at well known, often optimal regression rates for many nonparametric classes. At best,
\beta^{\prime}_{g},\beta_{h}\geq 1
and Algorithm
1
’s mean square rate is
O(\delta_{n}^{1/2})
. Our mean square rates do not further improve for stronger source conditions, echoing the saturation effect of ridge regression
(
Bauer et al., 2007
)
. By contrast, Algorithm
2
’s mean square rate will be
O(\delta_{n})
at best, under a new assumption.

4.2
Rates without compounding ill posedness

New assumption: Relative well posedness.
For faster rates of convergence, we place what appears to be a new assumption: the
g_{0}
inverse problem is relatively well posed compared to the
h_{0}
inverse problem. For readability, we focus on the main conditions below and defer an additional, technical condition to Appendix
\thechapter.A
. Denote the singular values of the conditional expectation operator
S
by
(\sigma_{j}^{\prime})
and those of
T_{g}
by
(\sigma_{j})
.

Assumption 6

(Relative well posedness)
.

\|(S^{*}S+\mu^{\prime}I)^{-1}T_{g}^{*}T_{g}\|_{\text{op}}=O(1)
for all
\mu^{\prime}>0
.

If
S
and
T_{g}
have the same right singular functions, then
\|(S^{*}S+\mu^{\prime}I)^{-1}T_{g}^{*}T_{g}\|_{\text{op}}=\sup_{j}\frac{\sigma_{j}^{2}}{(\sigma_{j}^{\prime})^{2}+\mu^{\prime}}
is clearly recognizable as a relative measure of well posedness for the conditional expectation operators
S
and
T_{g}
, up to a tolerance
\mu^{\prime}
. Assumption
6
imposes that the singular values of
S
, aided by
\mu^{\prime}>0
, are not too small relative to the singular values of
T_{g}
.
It essentially requires that
C^{\prime}
is a strong enough instrument relative to
C
.

Proposition 1

(Linear models with scaled data)
.

Suppose that
(g_{0},h_{0})
are linear and
(A,B,C,C^{\prime})
are scalars with mean zero, unit variance,
\textsc{corr}(A,C)=\rho_{A}
,
\textsc{corr}(B,C)=\rho_{B}
, and

\textsc{corr}(A,C^{\prime})=\rho^{\prime}
, where the correlations may be sequences decreasing in
n
. Assumption
6
holds when

|\rho_{A}/\rho^{\prime}|
is
O(1)
.

Remark 1

(Intuition)
.

Here,
g_{0}=\frac{\mathbb{E}(YC^{\prime})}{\rho^{\prime}}
and
h_{0}=\frac{\mathbb{E}(YC^{\prime})}{\rho^{\prime}}\frac{\rho_{A}}{\rho_{B}}
. Without our condition, ill posedness manifests as the
product
of instrument strength
|\rho^{\prime}|\cdot|\rho_{B}|
. With our condition, it manifests as the
minimum
of
|\rho^{\prime}|
and
|\rho_{B}|
, since
|g_{0}|\leq\frac{|\mathbb{E}(YC^{\prime})|}{|\rho^{\prime}|}
and
|h_{0}|\lesssim\frac{|\mathbb{E}(YC^{\prime})|}{|\rho_{B}|}
.

Proposition 2

(Nonlinear models with Gaussian data)
.

Suppose that
(A,B,C,C^{\prime})
are jointly normal scalars with mean zero, unit variance,
\textsc{corr}(A,C)=\rho_{A}
,
and
\textsc{corr}(A,C^{\prime})=\rho^{\prime}
.
Assumption
6
holds when
|\rho_{A}/\rho^{\prime}|
is
O(1)
.

When
(A,B,C,C^{\prime})
are standard normal random vectors, then relative well posedness imposes that the canonical correlations between elements of
A
and
C^{\prime}

are at least the same order as the canonical correlations between elements of
A
and
C
. See Appendix
\thechapter.H
for details.

In addition, we impose what appears to be a new notion of completeness.

Assumption 7

(Relative completeness)
.

If
Sg=0
then
T_{g}g=0
. The range of
S
is closed.

Under Assumption
7
, if a function is indistinguishable to the initial conditional expectation operator
S:g(\cdot)\mapsto\mathbb{E}\{g(A)|C^{\prime}=(\cdot)\}
, then it is also indistinguishable to the nested conditional expectation operator
T(0,g):g(\cdot)\mapsto\mathbb{E}\{0-g(A)|C=(\cdot)\}
. Intuitively, Assumption
7
ensures that solving the nested NPIV operator equation does not require information that cannot be learned from the initial NPIV operator equation.

Assumption
7
naturally extends the standard completeness condition for NPIV:
Sg=0
implies
g=0

(
Newey and Powell, 2003
)
. Our condition is weaker. It requires injectivity only on a restricted subspace defined relative to the next operator, rather than on the entire function space.

Faster rates via relative well posedness.
Assumption
6
improves convergence rates by limiting how the ill posedness of
g_{0}
compounds the ill posedness of
h_{0}
. We arrive at a somewhat surprising result: under this auxiliary condition, the final rate for
h_{0}
depends on the
minimum
of
\textsc{well}(\beta_{h})
and
\textsc{well}(\beta_{g}^{\prime})
rather than their product, which is a dramatic improvement. In other words, rates are throttled only once. Such a result requires that
g_{0}
is smooth with respect to not only
S
but also
T
, i.e. another source condition.

Assumption 8

(Source)
.

g_{0}=(T_{g}^{*}T_{g})^{\beta_{g}/2}w_{g}
for some
w_{g}\in\mathcal{G}
and some
\beta_{g}\in(0,\infty)
.

Theorem 2

(Bound for Algorithm
2
)
.

Suppose Assumption
1
holds for
\mathcal{F}
,
\mathcal{G}
,
\mathcal{H}
,
\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{F}
,
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
, and
\mathcal{G}\times\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
;
Assumptions
2
through
8
hold; and an additional regularity condition holds given in Appendix
\thechapter.A
. Then with probability
1-\zeta
, when
\mu=\mu^{\prime}=O(1)
and
\delta_{n}=\Omega[\{\ln\ln(n)+\ln(1/\zeta)\}^{1/2}n^{-1/2}]
(a simplifying lower bound), we have
\|S(\hat{g}-g_{0})\|^{2}=O(R_{n})
,
\|T(\hat{h}-h_{0},\hat{g}-g_{0})\|^{2}=O(R_{n})
,
\|\hat{g}-g_{0}\|^{2}=O(\mu^{-1}R_{n})
, and
\|\hat{h}-h_{0}\|^{2}=O(\mu^{-1}R_{n})
, where

R_{n}=\mu^{\min(\beta_{h}+1,2)}\|w_{h}\|^{2}+\mu^{\min(\beta_{g}^{\prime}+1,2)}\|w_{g}^{\prime}\|^{2}+\mu^{\min(\beta_{g}+1,2)}\|w_{g}\|^{2}+\delta_{n}^{2}
.

Theorem
2
is a new result for the simultaneous nested NPIV. The rate
R_{n}
contains the bias terms
\mu^{\min(\beta_{h}+1,2)}\|w_{h}\|^{2}+\mu^{\min(\beta_{g}^{\prime}+1,2)}\|w_{g}^{\prime}\|^{2}+\mu^{\min(\beta_{g}+1,2)}\|w_{g}\|^{2}
and a variance term
\delta_{n}^{2}

Theorem
2
cleanly separates the ill posedness of
\hat{g}
from
\hat{h}
, and in a different way than Theorem
1
. The ill posedness of
\hat{g}
appears via the second and third bias terms, which are additively separable from the other terms in
R_{n}
.

The rate for mean square error is slower than the rate for projected mean square error by a factor of
\mu^{-1}
. Rates may be optimized by choosing
\mu
to balance the bias and variance. Recall that
\textsc{well}(\beta)=\frac{\min(\beta,1)}{\min(\beta,1)+1}\in(0,1/2]
measures well posedness.

Corollary 5

(Better rates due to relative well posedness)
.

Suppose the conditions of Theorem
2
hold. Take
\mu=\delta_{n}^{\frac{2}{\min(\underline{\beta},1)+1}}
where
\underline{\beta}=\min(\beta_{h},\beta_{g}^{\prime},\beta_{g})
. Then with probability
1-\zeta
,
\|T_{h}(\hat{h}-h_{0})\|^{2}=O(\delta_{n}^{2})
and
\|\hat{h}-h_{0}\|^{2}=O\left\{\delta_{n}^{2\textsc{well}(\underline{\beta})}\right\}
.

Example 6

(Gaussian kernel)
.

By Corollary
5
,

\|\hat{h}-h_{0}\|^{2}=O_{p}\left[\left\{\frac{\ln(n)}{n}\right\}^{\textsc{well}(\underline{\beta})}\right]
.

Example 7

(Neural network)
.

By Corollary
5
,

\|\hat{h}-h_{0}\|^{2}=O_{p}\left[\left\{\frac{LW\ln(W)\ln(n)}{n}\right\}^{\textsc{well}(\underline{\beta})}\right]
.

Ill posedness compounds in a simple way for Algorithm
2
: our mean square rate is a “base rate”
\delta_{n}^{2}
slowed by the minimal well posedness
\textsc{well}(\beta_{h}\wedge\beta_{g}^{\prime}\wedge\beta_{g})
. At best,
\beta_{h},\beta^{\prime}_{g},\beta_{g}\geq 1
and the mean square rate is
O(\delta_{n})
. Meanwhile, the projected mean square rate is
O(\delta_{n}^{2})
.

Comparing Corollary
5
with Corollary
4
, we see that Assumption
6
improves rates by restricting how ill posedness may compound. Whereas Corollary
4
gives
\|\hat{h}-h_{0}\|^{2}=O\left\{\delta_{n}^{2\textsc{well}(\beta_{h})\textsc{well}(\beta^{\prime}_{g})}\right\}
, Corollary
5
gives
\|\hat{h}-h_{0}\|^{2}=O\left\{\delta_{n}^{2\textsc{well}(\beta_{h}\wedge\beta_{g}^{\prime}\wedge\beta_{g})}\right\}
. The base rate
\delta_{n}^{2}
is throttled less in the latter. By placing an assumption on the relative measure of well posedness, we ensure that the ill posedness compounds in a much more benign way. Remarkably, it is the
minimum
rather than the
product
. Remark
1
gives intuition.

Comparing Corollary
5
with previous series analysis, we see the main assumptions generally align, yet Corollary
5
strengthens asymptotic mean square consistency
(
Ai and Chen, 2007
, Lemma 3.1)
to a nonasymptotic mean square rate. It also sharpens the projected mean square rate from
o_{p}(n^{-1/2})

(
Ai and Chen, 2007
, Theorem 3.1)
to
O(\delta_{n}^{2})
. Assumption
1
is similar to series complexity, as measured by covering numbers
(
Ai and Chen, 2007
, Assumption 3.7)
. Assumption
2
relaxes previous source conditions from
\beta=2
to
\beta<2

(
Ai and Chen, 2007
, Assumption 3.8.3)
.
Assumption
6
is not necessary for the mean square rate in Theorem
1
. We conjecture that it may verify previous high level conditions
(
Ai and Chen, 2007
, Assumption 4.1)
.

5
Semiparametric inference

Theorem
2
alleviates some of the compounding ill posedness of nested NPIV that appears in Theorem
1
. In this section, we further alleviate how compounding ill posedness affects inference on causal parameters that are functionals of the nested NPIV. In particular, we characterize a multiple robustness to ill posedness: multiple inverse problems may be moderately ill posedness, as long as other inverse problems are mildly ill posed.

It seems that multiple robustness to ill posedness has not been characterized in previous work on generic functionals of machine learning nuisances
(
Zheng and van der Laan, 2011
;
Chernozhukov et al., 2018
;
Chernozhukov et al., 2022a
)
.
Our multiple robustness to ill posedness generalizes the double robustness to ill posedness known for linear functionals of NPIV
(
Chernozhukov et al., 2023
)
such as proxy treatment effects in cross sectional data
(
Kallus et al., 2021
;
Ghassami et al., 2022b
)
.

Bilinear influence functions.
We study causal parameters with bilinear influence functions, e.g. mediated, time varying, and long term effects, since these motivate our interest in nested NPIV. This class of parameters includes causal scalars, e.g. the proxy direct effect (Example
1
), and causal functions, e.g. heterogeneous long term effects (Example
4
). We develop both of these examples to make our general results concrete.

For this class of causal parameters, four nuisances appear in the influence function, which we denote by
(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4})
. Below, we will see that
(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4})
are simple transformations of
(h_{0},g_{0})
and their dual analogues. For simplicity, let the argument for
h_{j}
be
B_{j}
, and let its conditional expectation operator be
T_{j}
.
11
11

11

If
\mathbb{E}\{h_{0}(B)|C\}=\mathbb{E}\{g_{0}(A)|C\}
and
\mathbb{E}\{g_{0}(A)|C^{\prime}\}=\mathbb{E}(Y|C^{\prime})
, then
(B_{1},B_{2},B_{3},B_{4})=(B,A,C^{\prime},C)
.
We study parameters whose influence functions take the form

\psi(B_{1},B_{2},B_{3},B_{4})=h_{1}(B_{1})+h_{3}(B_{3})\{Y-h_{2}(B_{2})\}+h_{4}(B_{4})\{h_{2}(B_{2})-h_{1}(B_{1})\}-\theta_{0}.

Example 1

(Proxy mediation analysis)
.

Recall the definitions of
(h_{0},g_{0})
from Section
2
.
Now let
h_{0}^{\prime}
be a treatment confounding bridge that solves

\mathbb{E}\{h^{\prime}(X,Z,D,M)|X,D=1,M,W\}=\mathbb{E}\left\{g_{0}^{\prime}(X,Z,D=0)\frac{\mathbb{P}(D=0|X,M,W)}{\mathbb{P}(D=1|X,M,W)}|X,D=1,M,W\right\}
,
where
g_{0}^{\prime}
solves

\mathbb{E}\{g^{\prime}(X,Z,D)|X,D=0,W\}=\mathbb{E}\left\{\frac{1}{\mathbb{P}(D=0|X,W)}|X,D=0,W\right\}
.
Then

h_{1}(X,W)=h_{0}(X,D=0,W)
,

h_{2}(X,M,W)=g_{0}(X,D=1,M,W)
,

h_{3}(X,Z,D,M)=1_{D=1}h_{0}^{\prime}(X,Z,D=1,M)
, and

h_{4}(X,Z,D)=1_{D=0}g_{0}^{\prime}(X,Z,D=0)
.

Example 4

(Heterogeneous long term effects)
.

Recall the definitions of
(h_{0},g_{0})
from Section
2
.
Then

h_{1}(X_{1},X_{2})=\ell_{\lambda}(X_{1})h_{0}(X_{1},X_{2},D=d)
and

h_{2}(X_{1},X_{2},M)=\ell_{\lambda}(X_{1})g_{0}(X_{1},X_{2},M,G=\textsc{obs})
localize the outcome bridge and outcome mechanism. Now define

h_{3}(X_{1},X_{2},M,G)=\frac{1_{G=\textsc{obs}}}{\mathbb{P}(G=\textsc{obs}|X_{1},X_{2},M)}\frac{\mathbb{P}(D=d|X_{1},X_{2},M,G=\textsc{exp})\mathbb{P}(G=\textsc{exp}|X_{1},X_{2},M)}{\mathbb{P}(D=d|X_{1},X_{2},G=\textsc{exp})\mathbb{P}(G=\textsc{exp}|X_{1},X_{2})}
and

h_{4}(X_{1},X_{2},D,G)=)\frac{1_{G=\textsc{exp}}1_{D=d}}{\mathbb{P}(D=d|X_{1},X_{2},G=\textsc{exp})\mathbb{P}(G=\textsc{exp}|X_{1},X_{2})}
, which contain the treatment and selection mechanisms.

For such parameters, we estimate the causal parameter according to a standard procedure.

Algorithm 3

(Causal parameter)
.

Split the sample into
train
and
test
folds. Given nested NPIV estimators
(\hat{h}_{1},\hat{h}_{2},\hat{h}_{3},\hat{h}_{4})
estimated from observations in
train
, calculate the empirical influence of observation
i\in\textsc{test}
as

\hat{\psi}_{i}=\hat{h}_{1}(B_{1i})+\hat{h}_{3}(B_{3i})\{Y_{i}-\hat{h}_{2}(B_{2i})\}+\hat{h}_{4}(B_{4i})\{\hat{h}_{2}(B_{2i})-\hat{h}_{1}(B_{1i})\}.

This process generates a vector
\hat{\psi}\in\mathbb{R}^{n/2}
. Reversing the roles of
train
and
test
, we generate another such vector. Slightly abusing notation, we concatenate the two to obtain
\hat{\psi}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Estimate
\hat{\theta}=\textsc{mean}(\hat{\psi})
and
\hat{\sigma}^{2}=\textsc{var}(\hat{\psi})
, and return the confidence interval
\textsc{CI}=\hat{\theta}\pm 1.96\hat{\sigma}n^{-1/2}
.

Multiple robustness to ill posedness.
Algorithm
3
is well known in semiparametric theory. Our secondary contribution is to demonstrate that it has stronger properties than previously documented, which help to alleviate the compounding ill posedness of nested NPIV. Similar to many previous works, we leverage Neyman orthogonality.

Assumption 9

(Neyman orthogonality)
.

(i) For all
\tilde{h}_{1}\in\mathcal{H}_{1}
,
\mathbb{E}[\tilde{h}_{1}(B_{1})\{1-h_{4}(B_{4})\}]=0
.
(ii) For all
\tilde{h}_{2}\in\mathcal{H}_{2}
,
\mathbb{E}[\tilde{h}_{2}(B_{2})\{h_{4}(B_{4})-h_{3}(B_{3})\}]=0
.
(iii) For all
\tilde{h}_{3}\in\mathcal{H}_{3}
,
\mathbb{E}[\tilde{h}_{3}(B_{3})\{Y-h_{2}(B_{2})\}]=0
.
(iv) For all
\tilde{h}_{4}\in\mathcal{H}_{4}
,
\mathbb{E}[\tilde{h}_{4}(B_{4})\{h_{2}(B_{2})-h_{1}(B_{1})\}]=0
.

Assumption
9
implies Neyman orthogonality in our bilinear setting. It is straightforward to verify Assumption
9
by the law of iterated expectations, for all of the motivating examples, e.g. mediated, time varying, and long term treatment effects with or without proxies. Finally, we place some weak regularity conditions.

Assumption 10

(Regularity conditions)
.

(i) The residual variances are bounded:
\mathbb{E}[\{Y-h_{2}(B_{2})\}^{2}|B_{2}]\leq\bar{\sigma}_{y}^{2}
and
\mathbb{E}[\{h_{2}(B_{2})-h_{1}(B_{1})\}^{2}|B_{1}]\leq\bar{\sigma}_{2}^{2}
. (ii) The generalized balancing weights are bounded:
\|h_{3}\|_{\infty}\leq\bar{h}_{3}
and
\|h_{4}\|_{\infty}\leq\bar{h}_{4}
. (iii) The generalized balancing weights are censored:
\|\hat{h}_{3}\|_{\infty}\leq\bar{h}^{\prime}_{3}
and
\|\hat{h}_{4}\|_{\infty}\leq\bar{h}^{\prime}_{4}
. Here,
(\bar{\sigma}_{y}^{2},\bar{\sigma}_{2}^{2},\bar{h}_{3},\bar{h}_{4},\bar{h}^{\prime}_{3},\bar{h}^{\prime}_{4})
may be diverging sequences.

Assumption
10
(i) is weak and standard. Assumption
10
(ii) encodes a familiar condition necessary for regular estimation. In Example
4
, it imposes that the treatment and selection propensity scores are bounded away from zero and one. Assumption
10
(iii) can be achieved by censoring extreme values in way that is asymptotically negligible. For causal functions,
(\bar{\sigma}_{y}^{2},\bar{\sigma}_{2}^{2},\bar{h}_{3},\bar{h}_{4},\bar{h}^{\prime}_{3},\bar{h}^{\prime}_{4})
diverge as the bandwidth
\lambda
vanishes, which our inference result tolerates.

To state our final result, let
\sigma^{2}
,
\kappa^{3}
, and
\chi^{4}
be the second, third, and fourth moments of
\psi(B_{1},B_{2},B_{3},B_{4})
defined above. Like Assumption
10
, these quantities are fixed for causal scalars and diverging for causal functions, which our inference result tolerates.

Theorem 3

(Multiple robustness to ill posedness)
.

Suppose Assumptions
9
and
10
hold, as well as the moment regularity
\{\left(\kappa/\sigma\right)^{3}+\chi^{2}\}n^{-1/2}\rightarrow 0.
Suppose the following quantities are
o_{p}(1)
: the individual rates
\left(1+\bar{h}_{4}/\sigma+\bar{h}_{4}^{\prime}/\sigma\right)\|\hat{h}_{1}-h_{1}\|
,

\left(\bar{h}_{3}/\sigma+\bar{h}_{3}^{\prime}+\bar{h}_{4}/\sigma+\bar{h}_{4}^{\prime}\right)\|\hat{h}_{2}-h_{2}\|
,

(\bar{h}_{4}^{\prime}+\bar{\sigma}_{y})\|\hat{h}_{3}-h_{3}\|
,

\bar{\sigma}_{2}\|\hat{h}_{4}-h_{4}\|
; and the product rates

1.

n^{1/2}\sigma^{-1}\{\|T_{1}(\hat{h}_{1}-h_{1})\|\|\hat{h}_{4}-h_{4}\|\wedge\|\hat{h}_{1}-h_{1}\|\|T_{4}(\hat{h}_{4}-h_{4})\|\}
,

2.

n^{1/2}\sigma^{-1}\{\|T_{2}(\hat{h}_{2}-h_{2})\|\|\hat{h}_{3}-h_{3}\|\wedge\|\hat{h}_{2}-h_{2}\|\|T_{3}(\hat{h}_{3}-h_{3})\|\}
,

3.

n^{1/2}\sigma^{-1}\{\|T_{2}(\hat{h}_{2}-h_{2})\|\|\hat{h}_{4}-h_{4}\|\wedge\|\hat{h}_{2}-h_{2}\|\|T_{4}(\hat{h}_{4}-h_{4})\|\}
.

Then
\hat{\theta}\overset{p}{\rightarrow}\theta_{0},

\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\sigma}(\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0})\overset{d}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{N}(0,1),
and

\mathbb{P}\{\theta_{0}\in(\hat{\theta}\pm 1.96\hat{\sigma}n^{-1/2})\}\rightarrow 0.95.

Theorem
3
summarizes nonasymptotic Gaussian approximation and variance estimation for mediated, time varying, and long term effects with generic machine learning in Appendix
\thechapter.F
. Whereas previous nonasymptotic results are limited to cross sectional parameters and NPIV
(
Chernozhukov et al., 2023
)
, we study short panel parameters and nested NPIV. Theorem
3
holds with or without proxy variables, and applies to nonparametric causal functions.
Compared to previous asymptotic results, we handle new cases, e.g. the proxy direct effect (Example
1
), heterogeneous
long term treatment effects (Example
4
), and many more; see Section
2
.

See Appendix
\thechapter.G
for a more explicit version of Theorem
3
for causal functions, including conditions on how the bandwidth
\lambda
vanishes. When
\lambda\asymp n^{-1/5}
,
\hat{\theta}(x_{1})
converges to
\theta_{0}(x_{1})
at the rate
n^{-2/5}
, which is familiar in Nadaraya-Watson estimation.

Our results confer a multiple robustness to ill posedness for functionals of nested NPIV.
In particular, Theorem
3
’s product rate conditions partly ameliorate how the ill posedness of the nested NPIV
h_{0}
affects inference for the causal parameter
\theta_{0}
. Each rate condition multiplies a projected mean square rate with a mean square rate. The former sidesteps the ill posedness of
\hat{h}
, extending classic results for functionals of NPIV
(
Blundell et al., 2007
)
.

Comparing Theorem
3
with previous series analysis, the estimating equation is different, the complexity assumption is more lax, and the rate conditions are more strict. Algorithm
3
is constructed from a Neyman orthogonal moment, rather than a plug-in moment
(
Ai and Chen, 2007
, eq. 7)
. Theorem
3
does not impose the restrictive Donsker condition
(
Ai and Chen, 2007
, Assumption 4.5)
. It requires product conditions involving not only projected mean square rates
(
Ai and Chen, 2007
, Assumption 4.2)
but also mean square rates.

Ill posedness saved is flexibility earned.

Corollary
5
improves rates of convergence for nested NPIV estimators. Theorem
3
refines the nested NPIV rate conditions for inference on the causal parameter. We combine these results to concretely illustrate how some inverse problems may be moderately ill posed, as long as others are sufficiently well posed. By sharpening the dependence on ill posedness, we allow for estimation over more complex function spaces.

Given the compounding ill posedness, it is not obvious that machine learning estimation of nested NPIV could culminate in
n^{-1/2}
inference for the causal parameter. Indeed, previous machine learning inference results for causal scalars may be pessimistic.

Proposition 3

(A negative result)
.

Some previous rate conditions for causal inference are very general, yet in terms of mean square error only, e.g.
Zheng and van der Laan, 2011
;
Chernozhukov et al., 2018
and various works that build on them. The mean square rates of Corollary
5

fail to satisfy such rate conditions.

By contrast, we provide a positive end-to-end result, which is useful for short panel data models with proxies and compounding ill posedness. In full generality,
(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4})
may each be a nested NPIV.
12
12

12

For several examples,
(h_{1},h_{3})
are nested NPIVs while
(h_{2},h_{4})
are NPIVs. Proposition
4
remains the same, replacing
(\vec{\beta}_{2},\vec{\beta}_{4})
with
(\beta_{2},\beta_{4})
and using fewer source conditions.

To lighten notation, write
\vec{\beta}_{j}=(\beta_{jg},\beta^{\prime}_{jg},\beta_{jh})
with smallest entry
\underline{\beta}_{j}
.

Proposition 4

(A positive result)
.

Suppose the conditions of Corollary
5
hold for
(\hat{h}_{1},\hat{h}_{2},\hat{h}_{3},\hat{h}_{4})
. Write the largest critical radius as
\bar{\delta}_{n}=\tilde{O}(n^{-\alpha})
, and the source conditions as
(\vec{\beta}_{1},\vec{\beta}_{2},\vec{\beta}_{3},\vec{\beta}_{4})
. Set regularizations as in Corollary
5
. Suppose
\sigma\asymp n^{\gamma}
. Then product rate conditions of Theorem
3
are satisfied if
(i)

\gamma+\alpha\{\textsc{well}(\underline{\beta}_{1})\vee\textsc{well}(\underline{\beta}_{4})+1\}>1/2;

(ii)

\gamma+\alpha\{\textsc{well}(\underline{\beta}_{2})\vee\textsc{well}(\underline{\beta}_{3})+1\}>1/2;

(iii)

\gamma+\alpha\{\textsc{well}(\underline{\beta}_{2})\vee\textsc{well}(\underline{\beta}_{4})+1\}>1/2.

We interpret these inequalities, and confirm that the set of values
(\alpha,\vec{\beta},\gamma)
satisfying them is nonempty.
Each right hand side is a constant, so each inequality is a joint requirement on the critical radius via
\alpha
, the source conditions via
\vec{\beta}
, and the asymptotic variance via
\gamma
.

The quantity
\alpha
measures the complexity of the function classes. At best,
\alpha=1/2
for parametric function classes. For nonparametric classes,
\alpha<1/2
.

Each quantity
\textsc{well}(\beta)
measures the well posedness of an inverse problem. At best,
\beta\geq 1
and
\textsc{well}(\beta)=1/2
. For severely ill posed inverse problems,
\beta\rightarrow 0
and
\textsc{well}(\beta)\rightarrow 0
. Our conditions allow the well posedness of some inverse problem to compensate the ill posedness of others.
Hence Proposition
4
clarifies multiple robustness to ill posedness.

For causal scalars, under mild regularity conditions,
\sigma\asymp 1
and hence
\gamma=0
. For causal functions, under the regularity conditions given in Appendix
\thechapter.G
,
\sigma_{\lambda}(x_{1})\asymp\lambda^{-1/2}
and hence for bandwidth
\lambda=n^{-1/5}
we have
\gamma=1/10
. Echoing the work of
Kennedy, 2023
on heterogeneous treatment effects, and many references therein, we derive product rate conditions that are
weaker
for causal functions than for causal scalars. Unlike
Kennedy, 2023
, we study causal functions in short panel data e.g. heterogeneous long term treatment effects.

Finally, we demonstrate that the set of
(\alpha,\vec{\beta},\gamma)
is non-empty. For simplicity, saturate each source condition with
\beta\geq 1
and suppose we are studying causal scalars with
\gamma=0
. Then the single sufficient condition is
\alpha>1/3
for Proposition
4
, which allows many nonparametric function classes. Without Assumption
6
, the condition is
\alpha>2/5
, i.e. less complexity; see Appendix
\thechapter.H
.

Proposition
4
is a consequence of our two technical innovations. It uses rates for nested NPIV in which the ill posedness does not compound too much (our first technique). It also uses product rate conditions involving projected mean square rates and mean square rates (our second technique). It allows non-Donsker spaces and partly ameliorates ill posedness.

6
Simulated and real data analysis

Our nested NPIV method may improve mean square error and coverage in nonlinear simulations, compared to some previous series methods. Using real data, we demonstrate that our method improves precision for the proxy direct treatment effect of the US Job Corps, compared to a previous parametric method. We also uncover heterogeneity in the long term effects of Project STAR: students with the lowest prior ability benefit the most from small class sizes.

For brevity, we focus on Algorithms
2
and
3
. Results for Algorithm
1
are similar.

Estimation with general function spaces may reduce mean square error.
Algorithm
2
outperforms some benchmarks in mean square error across several nonlinear data generating processes (DGPs) for nested NPIV. For simplicity, we focus on the equal dimensional setting:
dim(A)=dim(B)=dim(C)=dim(C^{\prime})=10
.
We fix the initial NPIV
g_{0}
as a cubic function, and let
h_{0}
be one of four different nonlinear functions, inspired by
Dikkala et al., 2020
.
Each sample has
n=2000
observations.

For each of these four variations of the DGP, we implement two versions of our estimator: an RKHS or neural network estimator with
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}^{\prime}=\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{H}
. Appendices
\thechapter.I
and
\thechapter.J
give implementation details, including a closed form for the RKHS version and principled tuning. As benchmarks, we also implement nested 2SLS, nested series, and nested series with regularization.

:
Proxy mediation analysis coverage simulations

Table

summarizes results of nested NPIV simulations. Each row corresponds to a different nonlinear function
h_{0}
. Each column corresponds to a different estimator. We report the empirical mean square error, averaged across 500 samples.
Series estimators perform relatively poorly across these DGPs.
Our proposed RKHS estimator outperforms every benchmark. In the particularly challenging exponential DGP, our proposed neural network estimator performs best.

Inference with general function spaces may improve coverage and interval length.
Algorithms
2
and
3
outperform some benchmarks in confidence interval coverage and length across several nonlinear DGPs for the proxy direct effect, i.e. Example
1
. We modify the simulation design of
Dukes et al., 2023
, introducing the same four nonlinearities studied in Table
1
. All variables are scalars except
X\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
; see Appendix
\thechapter.I
for details. As before, each sample has
n=2000
observations. We implement two versions of our estimator as well as three benchmarks.

Table
2
summarizes results of coverage experiments. Each table corresponds to a different nonlinear function
h_{0}
. Each column corresponds to a different estimator. Each row corresponds to a different performance metric,
averaged across 500 samples.

The various estimators often obtain nominal coverage, but starkly differ in their bias, variance, and interval length. The unregularized series estimator performs relatively poorly across DGPs. In the linear, piecewise linear, and sigmoid DGPs, our proposals consistently have shorter confidence intervals than the benchmarks. In the particularly challenging exponential DGP, only our proposed neural network procedure performs well.

In summary, Algorithms
2
and
3
work well across nonlinear DGPs and across machine learning function spaces. They repeatedly outperform 2SLS and series benchmarks in some nonlinear, heterogeneous causal models using short panel data and proxy variables.

Proxy mediation analysis of US Job Corps.
We turn to a motivating real world application: how to flexibly measure direct treatment effects while using proxies for unobserved confounding (Example
1
). We replicate an influential program evaluation
(
Dukes et al., 2023
)
that studies the direct effect of job training on arrests later in life, i.e. the effect that is not through the mechanism of employment. The answer sheds light on the development of worker skills. We extend previous parametric estimation to semiparametric estimation. Our flexible method documents a significant, negative direct effect. These empirical results represent a realistic use case: causal estimation in short panel data models with proxy variables.

Dukes et al., 2023
propose a parametric estimator for the proxy direct treatment effect. Continuing an extensive literature on the non-employment effects of the US Job Corps, the treatment
D
is job training in the year following randomization, the mechanism
M
is employment two years after randomization, and the outcome
Y
is arrests four years after randomization. The auxiliary variables
(Z,W)
are assumed to be relevant to unobserved motivation
U
, and also to satisfy exclusion restrictions: time spent with the Job Corps recruiter
Z
does not directly cause employment or arrests, and pre-training expectations
W
are not directly caused by training or employment. The final estimate is a scalar.

Figure 1
:
Direct effect of job training on arrests, using proxies for unobserved motivation

Figure
1
compares the previous parametric approach with our semiparametric approach. The previous parametric approach found statistically insignificant effects, possibly due to approximation error of the parametric models for the outcome confounding bridge and treatment confounding bridge. By allowing for flexible nonparametric estimation of the confounding bridges as nested NPIVs, our approach incurs a smaller approximation error. In this setting, it appears to improve statistical precision, suggesting a negative and possibly significant direct effect of job training on arrests. This empirical result is possible due to our new theoretical results for nested NPIV and its functionals.

Heterogeneous long term effects of Project STAR.
Finally, we turn to another motivating real world application: how to flexibly measure long term treatment effects by combining short term experimental data with long term observational data (Example
4
). We replicate an influential program evaluation
(
Athey et al., 2020
)
that combines Project STAR experimental data with New York City (NYC) observational data to study the average long term treatment effect of kindergarten class size on test scores later in life. We ask an additional question: is there meaningful heterogeneity in those long term effects? Our empirical results represent a realistic use case of our proposal. We document substantial heterogeneity: students who have the lowest prior ability benefit the most from small class sizes.

Athey et al., 2020
propose a parametric estimator for the average long term treatment effect and validate their results through an intuitive exercise, which we extend. Though Project STAR data include kindergarten class size
D
, elementary school test scores
M
, and middle school test scores
Y
, we suppose that the researcher sees
(D,M)
but not
Y
. The researcher combines the short term experimental
(D,M)
from Project STAR with the long term observational
(M,Y)
from NYC to estimate the average long term treatment effect. These estimates may be validated by comparing them with the “oracle” average long term treatment effect that an “oracle” who sees
(D,Y)
in the Project STAR data would obtain.

(a)

RKHS

(b)

Neural network

Figure 2
:
Average long term treatment effect over different horizons

A similar exercise may be conducted in a closely related variation of the problem. In this variation, the researcher sees
(D,M)
but not
Y
in Project STAR, and
(D,M,Y)
in NYC. Both variations of the problem
(
Athey et al., 2019
;
Athey et al., 2020
)
belong to the class of parameters we study, as well as their generalizations to causal functions.

Figure
2
demonstrates that a machine learning approach to average long term treatment effect estimation performs well in the validation exercise. Following
Athey et al., 2020
, we fix
M
as third grade test scores and take
Y
to be third, fourth, fifth, sixth, seventh, or eighth grade test scores. Across choices of
Y
, i.e. across horizons of extrapolation, the average long term treatment effect estimates recover the oracle estimates.

(a)

RKHS

(b)

Neural network

Figure 3
:
Heterogeneous long term treatment effects with respect to prior ability

Figure
3
goes deeper, from average effects to heterogeneous effects with respect to student aptitude. In particular, we examine heterogeneity with respect to prior ability
X_{1}
, measured in percentiles before the intervention.
For simplicity, we continue to fix
M
as third grade test scores and now fix
Y
to be seventh grade test scores.

The students with lowest prior ability benefit the most from enrollment in a small kindergarten class. The results may be statistically significant, with pointwise confidence intervals that exclude zero.
This empirical insight appears to be new, and it is possible due to our new theoretical results for causal functions such as heterogeneous long term treatment effects. Appendix
\thechapter.I
shows similar results across different horizons of extrapolation.

7
Discussion

A growing literature identifies parameters in nonlinear, heterogeneous causal models using short panel data and proxy variables.
These identifications motivate us to study nested nonparametric instrumental variable regression (nested NPIV), which also arises in economic models of simultaneous equations and price systems.
Our method allows researchers to conduct inference on the new causal models with machine learning, tolerating moderate ill posedness among some inverse problems.
We provide explicit mean square convergence rates for nested NPIV and introduce two techniques: relative well posedness, and multiple robustness to ill posedness.
Our new estimators detect direct effects of the US Job Corps with proxies for motivation, and long term effects Project STAR that are heterogeneous by prior ability.

Appendix
\thechapter
.A
Additional condition: Relative alignment

For faster rates, our key assumptions are relative well posedness (Assumption
6
) and relative completeness (Assumption
7
). We highlight a consequence of the latter, which allows us to articulate an additional regularity condition that we call relative alignment. Finally, we relate this regularity condition to existing regularity conditions in the NPIV literature.

\thechapter
.A.1
A consequence of relative completeness

Recall that relative completeness means that
S(g)=0
implies
T_{g}(g)=0
.

Lemma
\thechapter
.A.1
.

Under Assumption
7
, there exists a bounded linear operator
M
such that
T_{g}=MS
.

By Lemma
\thechapter.A.1
, the nested conditional expectation operator, with instrument
C
, can be expressed as a some operation
M
applied to the initial conditional expectation operator, with instrument
C^{\prime}
. In particular, the operation
M
is bounded. The intuition is as before: Assumption
7
ensures that solving the nested NPIV operator equation does not require information that cannot be learned from the initial NPIV operator equation.

\thechapter
.A.2
Relative alignment

Having shown that the operation
M
exists, we use it to articulate a technical regularity condition that rules out sign flipping, from the initial problem to the nested problem. We present a stronger sufficient condition, then a weaker sufficient condition.

Assumption
\thechapter
.A.1

(Strong relative alignment)
.

Suppose that
T
preserves alignment relative to
S
in the following pointwise sense.

1.

For all functions of the initial instrument
f,\tilde{f}\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
,
\langle f,\tilde{f}\rangle\geq 0
implies
\langle Mf,M\tilde{f}\rangle\geq 0

and
\langle M_{h}f,M_{h}\tilde{f}\rangle\geq 0
, where
M_{h}=\mu^{1/2}(T_{h}T_{h}^{*}+\mu I)^{-1/2}M
.

2.

For all functions of the nested instrument
f,\tilde{f}\in\mathcal{F}
,

\langle f,\tilde{f}\rangle\geq 0
implies
\langle f,\{(1-c)I-Q\}\tilde{f}\rangle\geq 0
. Here,
c
is a scalar,
I
is the identity operator, and
Q
is an operator defined in Appendix
\thechapter.E
. Under Assumption
6
, we later show
(1-c)I-Q
is positive semidefinite.

When
f=\tilde{f}
, each statement automatically holds. When
f\neq\tilde{f}
, Assumption
\thechapter.A.1
imposes that if the functions have a positive angle, then they also have a positive angle after certain transformations involving
S
and
T
.

As shown in Appendix
\thechapter.E
, Assumption
\thechapter.A.1
is sufficient to control a few of the terms that appear in the bias of Algorithm
2
. Simulations suggest that Assumption
\thechapter.A.1
is not necessary.

In what follows, we relax the strong, pointwise alignment condition (Assumption
\thechapter.A.1
) to a weaker, average alignment condition (Assumption
\thechapter.A.2
) that rules out sign flipping on average.
Let
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}={t\mapsto f_{t}\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}
and

\mathcal{P}={t\mapsto f_{t}\in\mathcal{F}}
be the collections of measurable, square integrable paths on
[0,1]
.

Assumption
\thechapter
.A.2

(Weak relative alignment)
.

Suppose that
T
preserves alignment relative to
S
in the following average sense.

1.

For all initial instrument paths
\{f_{(\cdot)},\tilde{f}_{(\cdot)}\}\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
satisfying

\langle f_{t},\tilde{f}_{t}\rangle\geq 0
for almost every
t\in[0,1]
, we have that

\int_{0}^{1}\left\langle Mf_{t},M\tilde{f}_{t}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}t\geq 0
and
\int_{0}^{1}\left\langle M_{h}f_{t},M_{h}\tilde{f}_{t}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}t\geq 0
, where
M_{h}
is defined in Assumption
\thechapter.A.1
.

2.

For all nested instrument paths
\{f_{(\cdot)},\tilde{f}_{(\cdot)}\}\in\mathcal{P}
satisfying
\langle f_{t},\tilde{f}_{t}\rangle\geq 0
for almost every
t\in[0,1]
, we have that

\int_{0}^{1}\left\langle f_{t},\{(1-c)I-Q\}\tilde{f}_{t}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}t\geq 0
, where the scalar
c
and operator
Q
are as in
Assumption
\thechapter.A.1
.

As before, each statement automatically holds when
f=\tilde{f}
. When
f\neq\tilde{f}
, Assumption
\thechapter.A.2
imposes that if the functions have a positive angle on a path, then they also have a positive angle after integration along the path. As shown in Appendix
\thechapter.E
, Assumption
\thechapter.A.2
is also sufficient to control a few of the terms that appear in the bias of Algorithm
2
.
13
13

13

This technical regularity condition guarantees that
\int_{0}^{t}\gamma(s)\partial s>0
in Lemma
\thechapter.E.8
.

\thechapter
.A.3
Comparison to previous conditions

Assumption
\thechapter.A.2
plays the same role as coercivity, strong curvature, and strong identification conditions in the functional analysis and NPIV literatures. Similar to those works, we use it to guarantee stability of the solution to the inverse problem.

As a first step towards the comparison, we rephrase Assumption
\thechapter.A.2
as a kind of positivity on average. Note that, for every bounded operator
B:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
,

\int_{0}^{1}\left\langle f_{t},B\tilde{f}_{t}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}t=\int\langle f,B\tilde{f}\rangle d\nu(f,\tilde{f})=\operatorname{trace}\left(B\Gamma\right),\quad\Gamma=\int\tilde{f}\otimes fd\nu(f,\tilde{f}).

In the first equality, we introduce
\nu
as the push-forward of Lebesgue measure on
(0,1)
by the path
\nu(A):=\int_{0}^{1}1_{A}\{x(t),y(t)\}\mathrm{d}t
. In the second equality, use outer product notation. Assumption
\thechapter.A.2
requires that the eigenvalues are positive on average, rather than positive everywhere.

For further interpretation, simplify the averaging measure
\nu
to be isotropic, so that
\operatorname{trace}(B\Gamma)=\kappa\operatorname{trace}(B)
with
\kappa>0
. Then Assumption
\thechapter.A.2
reduces to
\operatorname{trace}(B)\geq 0
. In this setting, any coercivity condition that is classically used to guarantee uniqueness of weak solutions
immediately implies weak relative alignment. See, e.g., the Minty-Browder, Babuška-Lax-Milgram, and Lions-Lax-Milgram theorems
(
Kress, 1989
, Theorem 13.23)
.

By similar logic, Assumption
\thechapter.A.2
resembles conditions in the NPIV literature. See, e.g., the strong curvature requirement on the weak metric
(
Ai and Chen, 2007
, Assumption 3.8(iii))
, and the functional strong identification condition
(
Bennett et al., 2023a
, Definition 1)
. Each condition guarantees solvability and stability of the associated inverse problem by imposing some form of positivity.

From a researcher’s standpoint, if Assumptions
6
,
7
, or
\thechapter.A.2
is implausible in a particular setting, then we recommend using the rates of Theorem
1
rather than Theorem
2
. Theorem
1
does not require these additional regularity conditions.

Appendix
\thechapter
.B
Projected rates without source conditions

To clarify the different roles played by Assumptions
1
and
2
, we derive projected mean square rates using only Assumption
1
. Assumption
2
is essentially a technique to translate projected mean square rates into mean square rates.

\thechapter
.B.1
Beyond ridge regularization

We study a sequential estimator with regularization besides ridge.

Algorithm
\thechapter
.B.1

(Nested NPIV with generic regularization)
.

Given observations
(A_{i},B_{i},C_{i})
, an initial estimator
\hat{g}
that may be estimated on the same data, and hyperparameter values
(\mu,\mu^{\prime\prime})>0
,

\hat{h}=\argmin_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\left[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\left\{2\cdot\textsc{loss}(f,\hat{g},h)-\textsc{penalty}(f,\mu^{\prime\prime})\right\}+\textsc{penalty}(h,\mu)\right]

where
\textsc{penalty}(f,\mu^{\prime\prime})=\mathbb{E}_{n}\{f(C)^{2}\}+\mu^{\prime\prime}\cdot\|f\|^{2}_{\mathcal{F}}
and
\textsc{penalty}(h,\mu)=\mu\cdot\|h\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}
.

Taking
\mu^{\prime\prime}=0
and
\|h\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}=\mathbb{E}_{n}\{h(B)^{2}\}
reduces Algorithm
\thechapter.B.1
to Algorithm
1
. More generally, Algorithm
\thechapter.B.1
allows for complex regularization, e.g.
\ell_{1}
norm regularization in sparse linear function spaces, and reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS) norm regularization in RKHSs. Under Assumption
1
, we will prove projected mean square error rates.
Our analysis of Algorithm
\thechapter.B.1
avoids Assumption
2
and accommodates more regularization types.

\thechapter
.B.2
Projected mean square rate

Theorem
\thechapter
.B.1

(Bound for Algorithm
\thechapter.B.1
)
.

Suppose Assumption
1
holds for
\mathcal{F}
,
\mathcal{G}
, and
\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{F}
; and Assumption
3
holds.
Further assume
h_{0}\in\mathcal{H}
and
\|T(h-h_{0})\|^{2}_{\mathcal{F}}\leq\textsc{lip}\|h-h_{0}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}
for some Lipschitz constant
\textsc{lip}<\infty
.
Then with probability
1-\zeta
, when
\mu\geq 2\mu^{\prime\prime}\cdot\textsc{lip}
and
\delta_{n}=\Omega[\{\log\log(n)+\log(1/\zeta)\}^{1/2}n^{-1/2}]
, we have
\|T(\hat{h}-h_{0})\|^{2}_{2}=O(R_{n})
, where
R_{n}=\mu\|h_{0}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}+\delta_{n}^{2}+\|\hat{g}-g_{0}\|^{2}_{2}
.

Theorem
\thechapter.B.1
is a projected mean square rate for nested NPIV. It does not require a source condition, and it allows for generic regularization. It generalizes known results for NPIV when
\hat{g}(A)=g_{0}(A)=Y

(
Dikkala et al., 2020
)
. The rate
R_{n}
has three terms: bias
\mu\|h_{0}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}
, variance
\delta_{n}^{2}
, and initial estimation error
\|\hat{g}-g_{0}\|_{2}^{2}
.
Only the ill posedness of
\hat{g}
appears in Theorem
\thechapter.B.1
, because the definition of projected mean square error sidesteps the ill posedness of
\hat{h}
.

Corollary
\thechapter
.B.1

(Rate for Algorithm
\thechapter.B.1
)
.

Suppose the conditions of Theorem
\thechapter.B.1
hold. Set
\mu=O(\delta_{n}^{2})
. Then with probability
1-\zeta
,
\|T(\hat{h}-h_{0})\|^{2}_{2}=O\left(\delta_{n}^{2}+\|\hat{g}-g_{0}\|^{2}_{2}\right)
.

Future work may strengthen Theorem
\thechapter.B.1
and Corollary
\thechapter.B.1
to mean square rates by placing further approximation assumptions, e.g. a restricted eigenvalue condition
(
Gautier and Rose, 2011
;
Gautier and Tsybakov, 2018
)
.

Appendix
\thechapter
.C
Proof of Theorem
1

Let
S:\mathcal{G}\rightarrow\mathbb{L}_{2}
be the operator
g\mapsto\mathbb{E}\{g(A)|C^{\prime}=\cdot\}
. Let
g_{0}
be the minimal
\mathbb{L}_{2}
norm solution of
S(g)-Y=0
.

Let
T:\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{G}\rightarrow\mathbb{L}_{2}
be the operator
(h,g)\mapsto\mathbb{E}\{h(B)-g(A)|C=\cdot\}
. To lighten notation, we abbreviate
Th=T(h,0)
. Let
h_{0}
be the minimal
\mathbb{L}_{2}
norm solution of
T(h,g_{0})=0
.

Let
T^{*}
be the adjoint of
T
.

Remark
\thechapter
.C.1

(AM-GM inequality)
.

If
a=O(b\cdot c)
then
a\leq\frac{b^{2}}{2}+O(c^{2})
.

\thechapter
.C.1
High probability events
