\thechapter
.E.3
Useful operator theory

Lemma
\thechapter
.E.5

(Pass through)
.

For any operators
U
and
V
and any scalar
\mu
,
(UV+\mu)^{-1}U=U(VU+\mu)^{-1}
when the products are well defined and the inverses exist.

Proof.

Write
U(VU+\mu)=(UV+\mu)U
.
∎

Lemma
\thechapter
.E.6

(Discard denominator)
.

Suppose
A
is positive definite and
B
is positive semidefinite. Then for any operator
C
,

\|C^{*}(A+B)^{-1}C\|_{\text{op}}\leq\|C^{*}A^{-1}C\|_{\text{op}}
.

Proof.

In the Loewner sense,
A+B\geq A>0
, so
0<(A+B)^{-1}\leq A^{-1}
by
Bhatia, 2013
. Therefore
A^{-1}-(A+B)^{-1}\geq 0
and in particular
C^{*}\{A^{-1}-(A+B)^{-1}\}C\geq 0
using
Bhatia, 2013
.
∎

Lemma
\thechapter
.E.7

(Relative completeness)
.

Suppose
A
and
B
are bounded linear operators between Hilbert spaces:
A\in\mathcal{L}(X,Y)
, and
B\in\mathcal{L}(X,Z)
. If
A(x)=0
implies
B(x)=0
, and
A
has closed range, then there exists a linear operator
M
such that
B=MA
and
\|M\|_{\text{op}}=O(1)
.

Proof.

Decompose
X=\textsc{null}(A)\oplus\{\textsc{null}(A)\}^{\perp}
, and consider the restricted operator
A_{0}:\{\textsc{null}(A)\}^{\perp}\rightarrow\textsc{range}(A)
by
A_{0}(x)=A(x)
. This is a bijective operator between Banach spaces, since we suppose that
\textsc{range}(A)
is closed. An application of the bounded inverse theorem states that the inverse
A_{0}^{-1}:\textsc{range}(A)\rightarrow\{\textsc{null}(A)\}^{\perp}
is bounded. In particular, there exists
C>0
such that

\|A_{0}^{-1}(y)\|\leq C\|y\|
, for all
y\in\textsc{range}(A).

Equivalently,
A
is bounded below in
\{\textsc{null}(A)\}^{\perp}
:
\|x\|\leq C\|A(x)\|
for all
x\in\{\textsc{null}(A)\}^{\perp}
. Thus we can write
\gamma:=\inf_{{x\in\{\textsc{null}(A)\}^{\perp}\;,\;\|x\|=1}}\|A(x)\|>0
.

This enables us to construct a bounded left inverse of
A
in its range. Define
L:\textsc{range}(A)\rightarrow\{\textsc{null}(A)\}^{\perp}
as
L\{A(x)\}=x
with
x\in\{\textsc{null}(A)\}^{\perp}
. This is a well-defined and bounded operator:
if
A(x_{1})=A(x_{2})
with
x_{1},x_{2}\in\{\textsc{null}(A)\}^{\perp}
, then
A\left(x_{1}-x_{2}\right)=0
, so
x_{1}-x_{2}\in\textsc{null}(A)\cap\{\textsc{null}(A)\}^{\perp}=\{0\}
; hence
x_{1}=x_{2}
.
To establish the boundedness condition, take
y=A(x)
with
x\in\{\textsc{null}(A)\}^{\perp}
, then

\|L(y)\|=\|x\|\leq\frac{1}{\gamma}\|A(x)\|=\frac{1}{\gamma}\|y\|
.

Now decompose
Y=\textsc{range}(A)\oplus\{\textsc{range}(A)\}^{\perp}
, and we extend
L
to all
Y
by letting
\tilde{L}(y_{R}+y_{R^{\perp}})=L(y_{R})
with
y_{R}\in\textsc{range}(A)
and
y_{R^{\perp}}\in\{\textsc{range}(A)\}^{\perp}
. Such an extension preserves the same operator norm:
\|\tilde{L}\|_{\text{op}}=\|L\|_{\text{op}}\leq\frac{1}{\gamma}
.

Finally, set
M:=B\tilde{L}\in\mathcal{L}(Y,Z)
. Such an
M
satisfies
\|M\|_{\text{op}}\leq\|B\|_{\text{op}}\|\tilde{L}\|_{\text{op}}\leq\|B\|_{\text{op}}\gamma^{-1}
. Moreover,
B=MA
. To prove the latter, take any
x\in X
and represent it as
x=u+v
with
u\in\textsc{null}(A)
,
v\in\{\textsc{null}(A)\}^{\perp}
. Then

MA(x)=B\tilde{L}A(u+v)=B\tilde{L}\{A(v)\}=BLA(v)=B(v)=B(v)+B(u)=B(x)

by
M=B\tilde{L}
and
x=(u+v)
;
A(u)=0
;
A(v)\in\textsc{range}(A)
;
L\{A(v)\}=v
; and
B(u)=0
by the hypothesized property that
A(u)=0
implies
B(u)=0
.
∎

The operator norm
\|M\|_{\text{op}}
can be interpreted as a relative condition number between the operators
A
and
B
: it is bounded by
\|L\|_{\text{op}}\|B\|_{\text{op}}
, where
L
is the left inverse of
A
.

Lemma
\thechapter
.E.8

(Relative alignment)
.

Suppose
(A,B,C)
are operators and
\mu>0
is a scalar, where
(A,B,A-B)
are positive semidefinite. If
\langle x,y\rangle\geq 0
implies
\langle x,(A-B)y\rangle\geq 0
then
\|C(C^{*}AC+\mu I)^{-1}\|_{\text{op}}\leq\|C(C^{*}BC+\mu I)^{-1}\|_{\text{op}}
.

Proof.

Define the function
f:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by

f(t)=\|C[C^{*}\{tA+(1-t)B\}C+\mu I]^{-1}\|_{\text{op}},

which is differentiable.
14
14

14

Since
f
is 1-Lipschitz, it is differentiable almost everywhere in the interval
[0,1]
by Rademacher’s theorem. The differential equality we obtain only needs to hold almost everywhere to establish our desired bound.
We prove that
f(1)\leq f(0)
by controlling the derivative of
f(t)
.

1.

Define the operator-valued function

W(t)=C[C^{*}\{tA+(1-t)B\}C+\mu I]^{-1}.

Then

f(t)=\|W(t)\|_{\text{op}}=\sigma_{\max}\{W(t)\},

where
\sigma_{\max}\{W(t)\}
is the largest singular value of
W(t)
.

2.

The derivative of
f(t)
is

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}f(t)=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\sigma_{\max}\{W(t)\}=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\sup_{\|u\|\leq 1,\|v\|\leq 1}\langle u,W(t)v\rangle=\langle u(t),\left\{\frac{\partial}{\partial t}W(t)\right\}v(t)\rangle

where
u(t)
and
v(t)
are the initial left and right singular functions of
W(t)
, i.e.

W(t)v(t)=\sigma_{\max}\{W(t)\}u(t),\quad u(t)^{*}W(t)=\sigma_{\max}\{W(t)\}v(t)^{*}.

The final equality appeals to
Bonnans and Shapiro, 1998
. In particular, the derivative formula applies their result on the closed unit ball endowed with the weak topology, which is compact by Banach-Alaoglu theorem. The continuity conditions are satisfied because
W(t)
and
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}W(t)
are compact operators.

3.

Next we compute the derivative of
W(t)
.
Recall that
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\{X^{-1}(t)\}=-X^{-1}(t)\frac{\partial X(t)}{\partial t}X^{-1}(t)
. Set
X(t)=C^{*}\{tA+(1-t)B\}C+\mu I
. Then
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}X(t)=C^{*}(A-B)C
and therefore

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}W(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}CX^{-1}(t)=-CX^{-1}(t)\frac{\partial X(t)}{\partial t}X^{-1}(t)

\displaystyle=-C[C^{*}\{tA+(1-t)B\}C+\mu I]^{-1}C^{*}(A-B)C[C^{*}\{tA+(1-t)B\}C+\mu I]^{-1}

\displaystyle=-W(t)C^{*}(A-B)W(t).

4.

Collecting results, the desired derivative is

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}f(t)=\langle u(t),\left\{\frac{\partial}{\partial t}W(t)\right\}v(t)\rangle=-\langle u(t),W(t)C^{*}(A-B)W(t)v(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=-\langle CW(t)^{*}u(t),(A-B)W(t)v(t)\rangle=-\langle C\sigma_{\max}\{W(t)\}v(t),(A-B)\sigma_{\max}\{W(t)\}u(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=-\sigma_{\max}\{W(t)\}^{2}\cdot\langle Cv(t),(A-B)u(t)\rangle=-f(t)^{2}\cdot\langle Cv(t),(A-B)u(t)\rangle=-f(t)^{2}\gamma(t)

with
\gamma(t):=\langle Cv(t),(A-B)u(t)\rangle
.

5.

We solve the differential equation
\frac{1}{f(s)^{2}}\frac{\partial f(s)}{\partial s}=-\gamma(s).

Integrating both sides,

-\int_{0}^{t}\gamma(s)\partial s=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{f(s)^{2}}\partial f(s)=-\frac{1}{f(t)}+\frac{1}{f(0)}.

Therefore,

f(t)=\frac{1}{\frac{1}{f(0)}+\int_{0}^{t}\gamma(s)\,\partial s}
and hence
f(1)=\frac{1}{\frac{1}{f(0)}+\int_{0}^{1}\gamma(s)\,\partial s}
.

6.

We argue that relative alignment of
A-B
implies
\gamma(s)\geq 0
. Note that

\displaystyle\langle Cv(t),u(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle u(t),Cv(t)\rangle=u(t)^{*}CX^{-1}(t)X(t)v(t)

\displaystyle=u(t)^{*}W(t)X(t)v(t)=\sigma_{\max}\{W(t)\}v(t)^{*}X(t)v(t)\geq 0

because
X(t)
is positive semidefinite as the sum of three positive semidefinite operators. Therefore, by hypothesis,
\gamma(t)=\langle Cv(t),(A-B)u(t)\rangle\geq 0
.

7.

In summary, we have shown
f(1)=\frac{1}{\frac{1}{f(0)}+\int_{0}^{1}\gamma(s)\,\partial s}\leq\frac{1}{\frac{1}{f(0)}}=f(0)
. ∎

In the final step, we use
\int_{0}^{1}\gamma(s)\,\partial s\geq 0
. Assumption
\thechapter.A.1
is a strong sufficient condition that guarantees
\gamma(s)\geq 0
. Assumption
\thechapter.A.2
is a weaker condition that guarantees
\int_{0}^{1}\gamma(s)\,\partial s\geq 0
.

\thechapter
.E.4
Verifying high level conditions

Proof of Lemma
\thechapter.A.1
.

The result is immediate from Lemma
\thechapter.E.7
.
∎

Lemma
\thechapter
.E.9
.

Assumption
\thechapter.A.1
holds if and only if

1.

\langle x,y\rangle\geq 0
implies
\langle x,M^{*}My\rangle\geq 0
;

2.

\langle x,y\rangle\geq 0
implies
\langle x,M^{*}\mu(T_{h}T_{h}^{*}+\mu)^{-1}My\rangle\geq 0
;

3.

\langle x,y\rangle\geq 0
implies
\langle x,(I-Q-cI)y\rangle\geq 0
.

A similar statement holds for Assumption
\thechapter.A.2
.

Proof.

The first and second results use the definition of the adjoint. The third uses
\langle x,(I-Q-cI)y\rangle=(1-c)\langle x,y\rangle-\langle x,Qy\rangle
.
∎

Lemma
\thechapter
.E.10

(Verifying high level conditions)
.

Assumptions
6
,
7
, and
\thechapter.A.1
imply Assumption
\thechapter.E.1
. The same is true replacing Assumption
\thechapter.A.1
with Assumption
\thechapter.A.2
.

Proof.

We state the argument under Assumption
\thechapter.A.1
; the argument under Assumption
\thechapter.A.2
is identical.
Again we abbreviate
\|\cdot\|=\|\cdot\|_{\text{op}}
.

By Lemma
\thechapter.E.3
and
Bhatia, 2013
,

\displaystyle c^{-1}

\displaystyle=\|(S^{*}S+T_{g}^{*}T_{g}+\mu^{\prime})^{1/2}(S^{*}S+\mu^{\prime})^{-1}(S^{*}S+T_{g}^{*}T_{g}+\mu^{\prime})^{1/2}\|

\displaystyle\leq\|(S^{*}S+T_{g}^{*}T_{g}+\mu^{\prime})^{1/2}(S^{*}S+\mu^{\prime})^{-1/2}\|\|(S^{*}S+\mu^{\prime})^{-1/2}(S^{*}S+T_{g}^{*}T_{g}+\mu^{\prime})^{1/2}\|

\displaystyle=\|(S^{*}S+\mu^{\prime})^{-1/2}(S^{*}S+T_{g}^{*}T_{g}+\mu^{\prime})^{1/2}\|^{2}\leq\|(S^{*}S+\mu^{\prime})^{-1}(S^{*}S+T_{g}^{*}T_{g}+\mu^{\prime})\|

\displaystyle=\|I+(S^{*}S+\mu^{\prime})^{-1}T_{g}^{*}T_{g}\|\leq 1+\|(S^{*}S+\mu^{\prime})^{-1}T_{g}^{*}T_{g}\|=O(1)

where in the last line we use Assumption
6
.

1.

By Lemma
\thechapter.E.6
,
\|T_{g}(S^{*}S+T_{g}^{*}T_{g}+\mu^{\prime})^{-1}T_{g}^{*}\|\leq\|T_{g}(T_{g}^{*}T_{g}+\mu^{\prime})^{-1}T_{g}^{*}\|\leq 1
.

2.

Under Assumption
7
, Lemma
\thechapter.A.1
implies

\displaystyle\|T_{g}(S^{*}S+T_{g}^{*}T_{g}+\mu)^{-1}\|

\displaystyle=\|MS\{S^{*}(I+M^{*}M)S+\mu\}^{-1}\|\leq\|M\|\|S\{S^{*}(I+M^{*}M)S+\mu\}^{-1}\|.

Focusing on the latter factor, Assumption
\thechapter.A.1
.1 and Lemmas
\thechapter.E.8
and
\thechapter.E.9
imply

\|S\{S^{*}(I+M^{*}M)S+\mu\}^{-1}\|\leq\|S(S^{*}S+\mu)^{-1}\|=O(\mu^{-1/2}).

3.

Since
T_{h}^{*}(I-Q)T_{h}\geq cT_{h}^{*}T_{h}
by Lemma
\thechapter.E.3
, an argument similar to Lemma
\thechapter.E.6
gives

\|T_{h}\{T_{h}^{*}(I-Q)T_{h}+\mu\}^{-1}T_{h}^{*}\|\leq\|T_{h}(cT_{h}^{*}T_{h}+\mu)^{-1}T_{h}^{*}\|=c^{-1}\|T_{h}(T_{h}^{*}T_{h}+\mu/c)^{-1}T_{h}^{*}\|\leq c^{-1}.

4.

Assumption
\thechapter.A.1
.2 and Lemmas
\thechapter.E.8
and
\thechapter.E.9
imply

\displaystyle\|T_{h}\{T_{h}^{*}(I-Q)T_{h}+\mu\}^{-1}\|

\displaystyle\leq\|T_{h}(cT_{h}^{*}T_{h}+\mu)^{-1}\|=c^{-1}\|T_{h}(T_{h}^{*}T_{h}+\mu/c)^{-1}\|=O(\mu^{-1/2}).

5.

See result 4.

6.

Since
T_{h}^{*}(I-Q)T_{h}\geq 0
by Lemma
\thechapter.E.3
,
\|\{T_{h}^{*}(I-Q)T_{h}+\mu\}^{-1}\|\leq\mu^{-1}
.

7.

Since
R\geq 0
by Lemma
\thechapter.E.3
, Lemma
\thechapter.E.6
gives
\|S(S^{*}S+R+\mu^{\prime})^{-1}S^{*}\|\leq\|S(S^{*}S+\mu^{\prime})^{-1}S^{*}\|\leq 1.

8.

Under Assumption
7
, Lemma
\thechapter.A.1
implies

\|T_{g}(S^{*}S+R+\mu^{\prime})^{-1}T_{g}^{*}\|=\|MS(S^{*}S+R+\mu^{\prime})^{-1}S^{*}M^{*}\|\leq\|M\|\|S(S^{*}S+R+\mu^{\prime})^{-1}S^{*}\|\|M^{*}\|.

Then appeal to result 7.

9.

Under Assumption
7
, Lemmas
\thechapter.E.3
and
\thechapter.A.1
imply that

R=T_{g}^{*}\mu(T_{h}T_{h}^{*}+\mu)^{-1}T_{g}=S^{*}M^{*}\mu(T_{h}T_{h}^{*}+\mu)^{-1}MS.

Assumption
\thechapter.A.1
.1 and Lemmas
\thechapter.E.8
and
\thechapter.E.9
imply

\displaystyle\|S(S^{*}S+R+\mu^{\prime})^{-1}\|

\displaystyle=\|S[S^{*}\left\{I+M^{*}\mu(T_{h}T_{h}^{*}+\mu)^{-1}M\right\}S+\mu^{\prime}]^{-1}\|

\displaystyle\leq\|S(S^{*}S+\mu^{\prime})^{-1}\|=O\{(\mu^{\prime})^{-1/2}\}.

10.

See result 9.

11.

Under Assumption
7
, Lemma
\thechapter.A.1
implies
\|T_{g}(S^{*}S+R+\mu^{\prime})^{-1}\|=\|MS(S^{*}S+R+\mu^{\prime})^{-1}\|\leq\|M\|\|S(S^{*}S+R+\mu^{\prime})^{-1}\|
. Then appeal to result 9.

12.

See result 11.

13.

Under Assumption
7
, Lemma
\thechapter.A.1
implies

\displaystyle\|S(S^{*}S+R+\mu^{\prime})^{-1}T_{g}^{*}\|=\|S(S^{*}S+R+\mu^{\prime})^{-1}S^{*}M^{*}\|\leq\|S(S^{*}S+R+\mu^{\prime})^{-1}S^{*}\|\|M^{*}\|.

Then appeal to result 7.

14.

Since
S^{*}S+R\geq 0
by Lemma
\thechapter.E.3
,
\|(S^{*}S+R+\mu^{\prime})^{-1}\|\leq(\mu^{\prime})^{-1}
. ∎

Appendix
\thechapter
.F
Proof of Theorem
3

Theorem
3
summarizes nonasymptotic Gaussian approximation and variance estimation results, which we now state and prove. Our nonasymptotic results apply to casual scalars and causal functions, though we defer details on the latter to Appendix
\thechapter.G
.

\thechapter
.F.1
Nonasymptotic refinements

We pointwise approximate causal functions such as Example
4
, taking the limit where the bandwidth
\lambda
of the weighting
\ell_{\lambda}
vanishes. Formally, we write
\theta_{0}(v)=\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow 0}\theta_{\lambda}(v)
, where
v=x_{1}
in Example
4
. For each
\theta_{\lambda}(v)
, we write the influence function as

\displaystyle\psi_{\lambda}(B_{1},B_{2},B_{3},B_{4})

\displaystyle=\ell_{\lambda}(V)[h_{1}(B_{1})+h_{3}(B_{3})\{Y-h_{2}(B_{2})\}+h_{4}(B_{4})\{h_{2}(B_{2})-h_{1}(B_{1})\}]-\theta_{\lambda}(v)

\displaystyle=h_{1,\lambda}(B_{1})+h_{3}(B_{3})\{Y_{\lambda}-h_{2,\lambda}(B_{2})\}+h_{4}(B_{4})\{h_{2,\lambda}(B_{2})-h_{1,\lambda}(B_{1})\}-\theta_{\lambda}(v).

By construction, the first moment of
\psi_{\lambda}
is zero, and the second, third, and fourth moments
(\sigma_{\lambda}^{2},\kappa_{\lambda}^{3},\chi_{\lambda}^{4})
diverge as
\lambda\downarrow 0
. Finally, we write the pointwise approximation error as
\Delta_{\lambda}(v)=n^{1/2}\sigma_{\lambda}^{-1}|\theta_{\lambda}(v)-\theta_{0}(v)|
.

For causal scalars,
\ell_{\lambda}(V)=1
,
(\sigma^{2},\kappa^{3},\chi^{4})
are fixed constants, and there is no pointwise approximation error.

Theorem
\thechapter
.F.1

(Finite sample Gaussian approximation for Algorithm
3
)
.

Suppose Assumptions
9
,
10
(i), and
10
(ii) hold. Then with probability
1-\epsilon
,
\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}\left\{\frac{n^{1/2}}{\sigma}(\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0})\leq z\right\}-\Phi(z)\right|\leq 0.4748\left(\frac{\kappa}{\sigma}\right)^{3}n^{-1/2}+\frac{\Delta}{(2\pi)^{1/2}}+\epsilon,
where
\Phi
is the standard Gaussian distribution and

\displaystyle\Delta

\displaystyle=\frac{7L}{2\epsilon\sigma}\bigg\{(1+\bar{h}_{4})\|\hat{h}_{1}-h_{1}\|_{2}+(\bar{h}_{3}+\bar{h}_{4})\|\hat{h}_{2}-h_{2}\|_{2}+\bar{\sigma}_{y}\|\hat{h}_{3}-h_{3}\|_{2}+\bar{\sigma}_{2}\|\hat{h}_{4}-h_{4}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+n^{1/2}\|\hat{h}_{1}-h_{1}\|_{2}\|\hat{h}_{4}-h_{4}\|_{2}+n^{1/2}\|\hat{h}_{2}-h_{2}\|_{2}\|\hat{h}_{3}-h_{3}\|_{2}+n^{1/2}\|\hat{h}_{2}-h_{2}\|_{2}\|\hat{h}_{4}-h_{4}\|_{2}\bigg\}.

If in addition Assumption
10
(iii) holds, then the same holds updating
\Delta
to be

\displaystyle\Delta

\displaystyle=\frac{4L}{\epsilon^{1/2}\sigma}\bigg\{(1+\bar{h}_{4}+\bar{h}_{4}^{\prime})\|\hat{h}_{1}-h_{1}\|_{2}+(\bar{h}_{3}+\bar{h}_{3}^{\prime}+\bar{h}_{4}+\bar{h}_{4}^{\prime})\|\hat{h}_{2}-h_{2}\|_{2}+\bar{\sigma}_{y}\|\hat{h}_{3}-h_{3}\|_{2}+\bar{\sigma}_{2}\|\hat{h}_{4}-h_{4}\|_{2}\bigg\}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\frac{1}{2\sigma}\bigg\{n^{1/2}\|T_{1}(\hat{h}_{1}-h_{1})\|_{2}\|\hat{h}_{4}-h_{4}\|_{2}\wedge n^{1/2}\|\hat{h}_{1}-h_{1}\|_{2}\|T_{4}(\hat{h}_{4}-h_{4})\|_{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt+n^{1/2}\|T_{2}(\hat{h}_{2}-h_{2})\|_{2}\|\hat{h}_{3}-h_{3}\|_{2}\wedge n^{1/2}\|\hat{h}_{2}-h_{2}\|_{2}\|T_{3}(\hat{h}_{3}-h_{3})\|_{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt+n^{1/2}\|T_{2}(\hat{h}_{2}-h_{2})\|_{2}\|\hat{h}_{4}-h_{4}\|_{2}\wedge n^{1/2}\|\hat{h}_{2}-h_{2}\|_{2}\|T_{4}(\hat{h}_{4}-h_{4})\|_{2}\bigg\}.

For causal functions, the same holds replacing
(\hat{\theta},\theta_{0},\Delta)
with
\{\hat{\theta}_{\lambda}(v),\theta_{0}(v),\Delta+\Delta_{\lambda}(v)\}
and indexing several quantities by
\lambda
; see Appendix
\thechapter.G
.

Theorem
\thechapter
.F.2

(Finite sample variance estimation for Algorithm
3
)
.

Suppose Assumptions
10
(i) and (iii) hold. Then with probability
1-\epsilon^{\prime}
,
|\hat{\sigma}^{2}-\sigma^{2}|\leq\Delta^{\prime}+2(\Delta^{\prime})^{1/2}\{(\Delta^{\prime\prime})^{1/2}+\sigma\}+\Delta^{\prime\prime},
where
\Delta^{\prime\prime}=\left(\frac{2}{\epsilon^{\prime}}\right)^{1/2}\chi^{2}n^{-1/2}
and

\Delta^{\prime}=7(\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0})^{2}+\frac{84L}{\epsilon^{\prime}}\left[\|\hat{h}_{1}-h_{1}\|_{2}^{2}+\{(\bar{h}_{3}^{\prime})^{2}+(\bar{h}_{4}^{\prime})^{2}\}\|\hat{h}_{2}-h_{2}\|_{2}^{2}+\{(\bar{h}_{4}^{\prime})^{2}+\bar{\sigma}_{y}^{2}\}\|\hat{h}_{3}-h_{3}\|_{2}^{2}+\bar{\sigma}_{2}^{2}\|\hat{h}_{4}-h_{4}\|_{2}^{2}\right].

For causal functions, the same holds, indexing several quantities by
\lambda
; see Appendix
\thechapter.G
.

Theorems
\thechapter.F.1
and
\thechapter.F.2
give nonasymptotic Gaussian approximation and variance estimation for mediated, time varying, and long term treatment effects with generic machine learning. The results hold with or without proxy variables, and apply to nonparametric causal functions.

Proof of Theorem
3
.

By Theorem
\thechapter.F.1
,
\hat{\theta}\overset{p}{\rightarrow}\theta_{0}
and

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left\{\theta_{0}\in\left(\hat{\theta}\pm 1.96\frac{\sigma}{n^{1/2}}\right)\right\}=0.95.

For the desired result, it suffices that
\hat{\sigma}^{2}\overset{p}{\rightarrow}\sigma^{2}
, which follows from Theorem
\thechapter.F.2
.
∎

\thechapter
.F.2
Neyman orthogonality

In this appendix, we expand the notation to eliminate some subscripts. We denote the norm
\mathcal{R}(h)=\|h-h_{0}\|_{2}^{2}
and
\mathcal{P}(h)=\|T(h-h_{0})\|_{2}^{2}
, where the operator
T
is relative to the definition of
h_{0}
. We write the nuisances as
(\nu_{0},\delta_{0},\alpha_{0},\eta_{0})=(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4})
. Let
W
concatenate all of the random variables in an observation. Let
\psi_{0}(w)=\psi(w,\theta_{0},\nu_{0},\delta_{0},\alpha_{0},\eta_{0})
where

\psi(w,\theta,\nu,\delta,\alpha,\eta)=\nu(w)+\alpha(w)\{y-\delta(w)\}+\eta(w)\{\delta(w)-\nu(w)\}-\theta

and we suppress the indexing by
\lambda
for causal functions.

Let
s(w),t(w),u(w),v(w)
be functions and let
\tau,\zeta\in\mathbb{R}
be scalars. The Gateaux derivative of
\psi(w,\theta,\nu,\delta,\alpha,\eta)
with respect to its argument
\nu
in the direction
s
is

\{\partial_{\nu}\psi(w,\theta,\nu,\delta,\alpha,\eta)\}(s)=\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}\psi(w,\theta,\nu+\tau s,\delta,\alpha,\eta)|_{\tau=0}.

The cross derivative of
\psi(w,\theta,\nu,\delta,\alpha,\eta)
with respect to its arguments
(\nu,\delta)
in the directions
(s,t)
is

\{\partial^{2}_{\nu,\delta}\psi(w,\theta,\nu,\delta,\alpha,\eta)\}(s,t)=\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\tau\partial\zeta}\psi(w,\theta,\nu+\tau s,\delta+\zeta t,\alpha,\eta)|_{\tau=0,\zeta=0}.

Lemma
\thechapter
.F.1

(Calculation of derivatives)
.

The first derivatives are

\{\partial_{\nu}\psi(w,\theta,\nu,\delta,\alpha,\eta)\}(s)=s(w)\{1-\eta(w)\}
,

\{\partial_{\delta}\psi(w,\theta,\nu,\delta,\alpha,\eta)\}(t)=t(w)\{\eta(w)-\alpha(w)\}
,

\{\partial_{\alpha}\psi(w,\theta,\nu,\delta,\alpha,\eta)\}(u)=u(w)\{y-\delta(w)\}
, and

\{\partial_{\eta}\psi(w,\theta,\nu,\delta,\alpha,\eta)\}(v)=v(w)\{\delta(w)-\nu(w)\}
.
The second derivatives are
\{\partial^{2}_{\nu,\delta}\psi(w,\theta,\nu,\delta,\alpha,\eta)\}(s,t)=0
,
\{\partial^{2}_{\nu,\alpha}\psi(w,\theta,\nu,\delta,\alpha,\eta)\}(s,u)=0
,
\{\partial^{2}_{\nu,\eta}\psi(w,\theta,\nu,\delta,\alpha,\eta)\}(s,v)=-v(w)s(w)
,
\{\partial^{2}_{\delta,\alpha}\psi(w,\theta,\nu,\delta,\alpha,\eta)\}(t,u)=-u(w)t(w)
,
\{\partial^{2}_{\delta,\eta}\psi(w,\theta,\nu,\delta,\alpha,\eta)\}(t,v)=v(w)t(w)
, and
\{\partial^{2}_{\alpha,\eta}\psi(w,\theta,\nu,\delta,\alpha,\eta)\}(u,v)=0
.

Proof.

The result is immediate from the definition of Gateaux differentiation.
∎

Lemma
\thechapter
.F.2

(Neyman orthogonality)
.

If Assumption
9
holds then
\psi
is Neyman orthogonal with respect to
(\nu,\delta,\alpha,\eta)
.

Proof.

The result is immediate from the first derivatives in Lemma
\thechapter.F.1
.
∎

Lemma
\thechapter
.F.3

(Verifying Neyman orthogonality)
.

Examples
1
and
4
are Neyman orthogonal. So are the other examples, using the efficient influence functions derived in other works.

Proof.

By the law of iterated expectations, it is straightforward to verify Assumption
9
for each example. By Lemma
\thechapter.F.2
, this suffices for Neyman orthogonality.
∎

\thechapter
.F.3
Gaussian approximation

Partition the observations into
L
folds. Denote the
\ell
th fold by
I_{\ell}
. Train
(\hat{\nu}_{\ell},\hat{\delta}_{\ell},\hat{\alpha}_{\ell},\hat{\eta}_{\ell})
on observations in the complement of
I_{\ell}
, i.e.
I_{\ell}^{c}
. Let
n_{\ell}=|I_{\ell}|=n/L
be the number of observations in
I_{\ell}
. Denote by
\mathbb{E}_{\ell}(\cdot)=n_{\ell}^{-1}\sum_{i\in I_{\ell}}(\cdot)
the average over observations in
I_{\ell}
. Denote by
\mathbb{E}_{n}(\cdot)=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\cdot)
the average over all observations in the sample.

We define the foldwise target as
\hat{\theta}_{\ell}=\mathbb{E}_{\ell}[\hat{\nu}_{\ell}(W)+\hat{\alpha}_{\ell}(W)\{Y-\hat{\delta}_{\ell}(W)\}+\hat{\eta}_{\ell}(W)\{\hat{\delta}_{\ell}(W)-\hat{\nu}_{\ell}(W)\}]
. We define the foldwise oracle as
\bar{\theta}_{\ell}=\mathbb{E}_{\ell}[\nu_{0}(W)+\alpha_{0}(W)\{Y-\delta_{0}(W)\}+\eta_{0}(W)\{\delta_{0}(W)-\nu_{0}(W)\}]
. We define the overall target as
\hat{\theta}=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\hat{\theta}_{\ell}
. We define the overall oracle as

\bar{\theta}=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\bar{\theta}_{\ell}.

Finally, let
(\bar{\alpha},\bar{\eta},\bar{\alpha}^{\prime},\bar{\eta}^{\prime},\bar{\sigma}_{1},\bar{\sigma}_{2})=(\bar{h}_{3},\bar{h}_{4},\bar{h}_{3}^{\prime},\bar{h}_{4}^{\prime},\bar{\sigma}_{y},\bar{\sigma}_{2})
.

Lemma
\thechapter
.F.4

(Taylor expansion)
.

Let
s=\hat{\nu}_{\ell}-\nu_{0}
,
t=\hat{\delta}_{\ell}-\delta_{0}
,
u=\hat{\alpha}-\alpha_{0}
, and
v=\hat{\eta}-\eta_{0}
. Then
n_{\ell}^{1/2}(\hat{\theta}_{\ell}-\bar{\theta}_{\ell})=\sum_{j=1}^{7}\Delta_{j{\ell}}
where the first derivative terms are
\Delta_{1{\ell}}=n_{\ell}^{1/2}\mathbb{E}_{\ell}[s(W)\{1-\eta_{0}(W)\}]
,
\Delta_{2{\ell}}=n_{\ell}^{1/2}\mathbb{E}_{\ell}[t(W)\{\eta_{0}(W)-\alpha_{0}(W)\}]
,
\Delta_{3{\ell}}=n_{\ell}^{1/2}\mathbb{E}_{\ell}[u(W)\{Y-\delta_{0}(W)\}]
,
\Delta_{4{\ell}}=n_{\ell}^{1/2}\mathbb{E}_{\ell}[v(W)\{\delta_{0}(W)-\nu_{0}(W)\}]
,
and the second derivative terms are
\Delta_{5{\ell}}=\frac{n_{\ell}^{1/2}}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\ell}\{-s(W)v(W)\}
,
\Delta_{6{\ell}}=\frac{n_{\ell}^{1/2}}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\ell}\{-t(W)u(W)\}
,
\Delta_{7{\ell}}=\frac{n_{\ell}^{1/2}}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\ell}\{t(W)v(W)\}
.

Proof.

An exact Taylor expansion gives
\psi(w,\theta_{0},\hat{\nu}_{\ell},\hat{\delta}_{\ell},\hat{\alpha}_{\ell},\hat{\eta}_{\ell})-\psi_{0}(w)
equal to

\displaystyle\{\partial_{\nu}\psi_{0}(w)\}(s)+\{\partial_{\delta}\psi_{0}(w)\}(t)+\{\partial_{\alpha}\psi_{0}(w)\}(u)+\{\partial_{\eta}\psi_{0}(w)\}(v)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\{\partial^{2}_{\nu,\delta}\psi_{0}(w)\}(s,t)+\frac{1}{2}\{\partial^{2}_{\nu,\alpha}\psi_{0}(w)\}(s,u)+\frac{1}{2}\{\partial^{2}_{\nu,\eta}\psi_{0}(w)\}(s,v)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\{\partial^{2}_{\delta,\alpha}\psi_{0}(w)\}(t,u)+\frac{1}{2}\{\partial^{2}_{\delta,\eta}\psi_{0}(w)\}(t,v)+\frac{1}{2}\{\partial^{2}_{\alpha,\eta}\psi_{0}(w)\}(u,v).

Averaging over observations in
I_{\ell}
,
\hat{\theta}_{\ell}-\bar{\theta}_{\ell}=\mathbb{E}_{\ell}\{\psi(W,\theta_{0},\hat{\nu}_{\ell},\hat{\delta}_{\ell},\hat{\alpha}_{\ell},\hat{\eta}_{\ell})\}-\mathbb{E}_{\ell}\{\psi_{0}(W)\}
. Finally appeal to Lemma
\thechapter.F.1
.
∎

Lemma
\thechapter
.F.5

(Residuals)
.

Suppose the conditions of Theorem
\thechapter.F.1
hold. Then with probability
1-\epsilon/L
, the first derivative terms have the bounds
|\Delta_{1\ell}|\leq t_{1}=\left(\frac{7L}{\epsilon}\right)^{1/2}(1+\bar{\eta})\{\mathcal{R}(\hat{\nu}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}
,
|\Delta_{2\ell}|\leq t_{2}=\left(\frac{7L}{\epsilon}\right)^{1/2}(\bar{\alpha}+\bar{\eta})\{\mathcal{R}(\hat{\delta}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}
,
|\Delta_{3\ell}|\leq t_{3}=\left(\frac{7L}{\epsilon}\right)^{1/2}\bar{\sigma}_{1}\{\mathcal{R}(\hat{\alpha}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}
,
|\Delta_{4\ell}|\leq t_{4}=\left(\frac{7L}{\epsilon}\right)^{1/2}\bar{\sigma}_{2}\{\mathcal{R}(\hat{\eta}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}
while the second derivative terms have the bounds
|\Delta_{5\ell}|\leq t_{5}=\frac{7L^{1/2}}{2\epsilon}\{n\mathcal{R}(\hat{\nu}_{\ell})\mathcal{R}(\hat{\eta}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}
,
|\Delta_{6\ell}|\leq t_{6}=\frac{7L^{1/2}}{2\epsilon}\{n\mathcal{R}(\hat{\delta}_{\ell})\mathcal{R}(\hat{\alpha}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}
,
|\Delta_{7\ell}|\leq t_{7}=\frac{7L^{1/2}}{2\epsilon}\{n\mathcal{R}(\hat{\delta}_{\ell})\mathcal{R}(\hat{\eta}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}
.

Proof.

For simplicity, we focus on one first derivative term and one second derivative term; the rest are similar.

1.

Markov inequality implies
\mathbb{P}(|\Delta_{1\ell}|>t_{1})\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}(\Delta^{2}_{1\ell})}{t_{1}^{2}}
and
\mathbb{P}(|\Delta_{5\ell}|>t_{5})\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}(|\Delta_{5\ell}|)}{t_{5}}
.

2.

The law of iterated expectations implies
\mathbb{E}(\Delta^{2}_{1\ell})=\mathbb{E}\{\mathbb{E}(\Delta^{2}_{1\ell}\mid I^{c}_{\ell})\}
and
\mathbb{E}(|\Delta_{5\ell}|)=\mathbb{E}\{\mathbb{E}(|\Delta_{5\ell}|\mid I^{c}_{\ell})\}
.

3.

Conditional on
I_{\ell}^{c}
,
(s,t,u,v)
are nonrandom. Moreover, observations within fold
I_{\ell}
are independent and identically distributed. Hence by Assumption
9
,
\mathbb{E}(\Delta^{2}_{1\ell}\mid I^{c}_{\ell})
equals

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left([n_{\ell}^{1/2}\mathbb{E}_{\ell}\{s(W)-s(W)\eta_{0}(W)\}\}]^{2}\mid I^{c}_{\ell}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{n_{\ell}}{n^{2}_{\ell}}\sum_{i,j\in I_{\ell}}\{s(W_{i})-s(W_{i})\eta_{0}(W_{i})\}\{s(W_{j})-s(W_{j})\eta_{0}(W_{j})\}\mid I^{c}_{\ell}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{n_{\ell}}{n^{2}_{\ell}}\sum_{i,j\in I_{\ell}}\mathbb{E}\left[\{s(W_{i})-s(W_{i})\eta_{0}(W_{i})\}\{s(W_{j})-s(W_{j})\eta_{0}(W_{j})\}\mid I^{c}_{\ell}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{n_{\ell}}{n^{2}_{\ell}}\sum_{i\in I_{\ell}}\mathbb{E}\left[\{s(W_{i})-s(W_{i})\eta_{0}(W_{i})\}^{2}\mid I^{c}_{\ell}\right]=\mathbb{E}[s(W)^{2}\{1-\eta_{0}(W)\}^{2}\mid I^{c}_{\ell}]\leq(1+\bar{\eta})^{2}\mathcal{R}(\hat{\nu}_{\ell}).

By Cauchy Schwarz,
\mathbb{E}(|\Delta_{5\ell}|\mid I^{c}_{\ell})=\frac{n_{\ell}^{1/2}}{2}\mathbb{E}\{|-s(W)v(W)|\mid I^{c}_{\ell}\}
is bounded by

\displaystyle\frac{n_{\ell}^{1/2}}{2}[\mathbb{E}\{s(W)^{2}\mid I^{c}_{\ell}\}]^{1/2}[\mathbb{E}\{v(W)^{2}\mid I^{c}_{\ell}\}]^{1/2}=\frac{n_{\ell}^{1/2}}{2}\{\mathcal{R}(\hat{\nu}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}\{\mathcal{R}(\hat{\eta}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}.

4.

Collecting results gives
\mathbb{P}(|\Delta_{1\ell}|>t_{1})\leq\frac{(1+\bar{\eta})^{2}\mathcal{R}(\hat{\nu}_{\ell})}{t_{1}^{2}}=\frac{\epsilon}{7L}
and
\mathbb{P}(|\Delta_{5\ell}|>t_{5})\leq\frac{n_{\ell}^{1/2}\{\mathcal{R}(\hat{\nu}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}\{\mathcal{R}(\hat{\eta}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}}{2t_{5}}=\frac{\epsilon}{7L}
. Therefore with probability
1-\epsilon/L
,
|\Delta_{1\ell}|\leq t_{1}=\left(\frac{7L}{\epsilon}\right)^{1/2}(1+\bar{\eta})\{\mathcal{R}(\hat{\nu}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}
and
|\Delta_{5\ell}|\leq t_{5}=\frac{7L}{2\epsilon}n_{\ell}^{1/2}\{\mathcal{R}(\hat{\nu}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}\{\mathcal{R}(\hat{\eta}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}
and similarly for all
j\in\{1,...,7\}
. ∎

Lemma
\thechapter
.F.6

(Residuals: Alternative path)
.

Suppose the conditions of Theorem
\thechapter.F.1
hold.
Then with probability
1-\epsilon/L
, the first derivative terms have the bounds of Lemma
\thechapter.F.5
, while the second derivative terms have the bounds

\displaystyle|\Delta_{5}|

\displaystyle\leq t_{5}=\left(\frac{7L}{4\epsilon}\right)^{1/2}(\bar{\eta}+\bar{\eta}^{\prime})\{\mathcal{R}(\hat{\nu}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}+(4L)^{-1/2}[\{n\mathcal{P}(\hat{\nu}_{\ell})\mathcal{R}(\hat{\eta}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}\wedge\{n\mathcal{R}(\hat{\nu}_{\ell})\mathcal{P}(\hat{\eta}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}],

\displaystyle|\Delta_{6}|

\displaystyle\leq t_{6}=\left(\frac{7L}{4\epsilon}\right)^{1/2}(\bar{\alpha}+\bar{\alpha}^{\prime})\{\mathcal{R}(\hat{\delta}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}+(4L)^{-1/2}[\{n\mathcal{P}(\hat{\delta}_{\ell})\mathcal{R}(\hat{\alpha}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}\wedge\{n\mathcal{R}(\hat{\delta}_{\ell})\mathcal{P}(\hat{\alpha}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}],

\displaystyle|\Delta_{7}|

\displaystyle\leq t_{7}=\left(\frac{7L}{4\epsilon}\right)^{1/2}(\bar{\eta}+\bar{\eta}^{\prime})\{\mathcal{R}(\hat{\delta}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}+(4L)^{-1/2}[\{n\mathcal{P}(\hat{\delta}_{\ell})\mathcal{R}(\hat{\eta}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}\wedge\{n\mathcal{R}(\hat{\delta}_{\ell})\mathcal{P}(\hat{\eta}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}].

Proof.

See Lemma
\thechapter.F.5
for
(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3},t_{4})
. We focus on
t_{5}
;
(t_{6},t_{7})
are similar.

1.

Write
2\Delta_{5\ell}=n_{\ell}^{1/2}\mathbb{E}_{\ell}\{-s(W)v(W)\}=\Delta_{5^{\prime}\ell}+\Delta_{5^{\prime\prime}\ell}
where
\Delta_{5^{\prime}\ell}=n_{\ell}^{1/2}\mathbb{E}_{\ell}[-s(W)v(W)+\mathbb{E}\{s(W)v(W)\mid I^{c}_{\ell}\}]
and
\Delta_{5^{\prime\prime}\ell}=n_{\ell}^{1/2}\mathbb{E}\{-s(W)v(W)\mid I^{c}_{\ell}\}
.

2.

Consider the former term. By Markov inequality,
\mathbb{P}(|\Delta_{5^{\prime}\ell}|>t)\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}(\Delta^{2}_{5^{\prime}\ell})}{t^{2}}
. By the law of iterated expectations,

\mathbb{E}(\Delta^{2}_{5^{\prime}\ell})=\mathbb{E}\{\mathbb{E}(\Delta^{2}_{5^{\prime}\ell}\mid I^{c}_{\ell})\}.

We bound the conditional moment. Conditional on
I_{\ell}^{c}
,
(s,t,u,v)
are nonrandom. Moreover, observations within fold
I_{\ell}
are independent and identically distributed. Since
\Delta_{5^{\prime}\ell}
has conditional mean zero by construction,
\mathbb{E}(\Delta^{2}_{5^{\prime}\ell}\mid I^{c}_{\ell})
equals

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\{\left(n_{\ell}^{1/2}\mathbb{E}_{\ell}[-s(W)v(W)+\mathbb{E}\{s(W)v(W)\mid I^{c}_{\ell}\}]\right)^{2}\mid I^{c}_{\ell}\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{n_{\ell}}{n^{2}_{\ell}}\sum_{i,j\in I_{\ell}}[-s(W_{i})v(W_{i})+\mathbb{E}\{s(W_{i})v(W_{i})\mid I^{c}_{\ell}\}][-s(W_{j})v(W_{j})+\mathbb{E}\{s(W_{j})v(W_{j})\mid I^{c}_{\ell}\}]\mid I^{c}_{\ell}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{n_{\ell}}{n^{2}_{\ell}}\sum_{i,j\in I_{\ell}}\mathbb{E}\left([-s(W_{i})v(W_{i})+\mathbb{E}\{s(W_{i})v(W_{i})\mid I^{c}_{\ell}\}][-s(W_{j})v(W_{j})+\mathbb{E}\{s(W_{j})v(W_{j})\mid I^{c}_{\ell}\}]\mid I^{c}_{\ell}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{n_{\ell}}{n^{2}_{\ell}}\sum_{i\in I_{\ell}}\mathbb{E}\left([-s(W_{i})v(W_{i})+\mathbb{E}\{s(W_{i})v(W_{i})\mid I^{c}_{\ell}\}]^{2}\mid I^{c}_{\ell}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}([s(W)v(W)-\mathbb{E}\{s(W)v(W)\mid I^{c}_{\ell}\}]^{2}\mid I^{c}_{\ell})\leq\mathbb{E}\{s(W)^{2}v(W)^{2}\mid I^{c}_{\ell}\}\leq(\bar{\eta}+\bar{\eta}^{\prime})^{2}\mathcal{R}(\hat{\nu}_{\ell}).

Collecting results gives
\mathbb{P}(|\Delta_{5^{\prime}\ell}|>t)\leq\frac{(\bar{\eta}+\bar{\eta}^{\prime})^{2}\mathcal{R}(\hat{\nu}_{\ell})}{t^{2}}=\frac{\epsilon}{7L}
.
Therefore with probability
1-3\epsilon/(7L)
,
|\Delta_{5^{\prime}\ell}|\leq t=\left(\frac{7L}{\epsilon}\right)^{1/2}(\bar{\eta}+\bar{\eta}^{\prime})\{\mathcal{R}(\hat{\nu}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}
and similarly for
j\in\{5,6,7\}
.

3.

Consider the latter term. Specializing to nonparametric confounding bridges, if
\mathbb{E}\{h_{0}(B)|C\}=\mathbb{E}\{g_{0}(A)|C\}
and
\mathbb{E}\{g_{0}(A)|C^{\prime}\}=\mathbb{E}(Y|C^{\prime})
, then the arguments of
(\nu,\delta,\alpha,\eta)
, and hence
(s,t,u,v)
, are
(B,A,C^{\prime},C)
, respectively. Therefore
\mathbb{E}\{-s(W)v(W)\mid I^{c}_{\ell}\}=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}\{-s(B)\mid C,I^{c}_{\ell}\}v(C)\mid I^{c}_{\ell}]
is bounded by

\displaystyle\{\mathbb{E}([\mathbb{E}\{s(B)\mid C,I^{c}_{\ell}\}]^{2}\mid I^{c}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}[\mathbb{E}\{v(C)^{2}\mid I^{c}_{\ell}\}]^{1/2}=\{\mathcal{P}(\hat{\nu}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}\{\mathcal{R}(\hat{\eta}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}.

Hence

\Delta_{5^{\prime\prime}\ell}\leq n_{\ell}^{1/2}\{\mathcal{P}(\hat{\nu}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}\{\mathcal{R}(\hat{\eta}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}=L^{-1/2}\{n\mathcal{P}(\hat{\nu}_{\ell})\mathcal{R}(\hat{\eta}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}.

Likewise

\Delta_{5^{\prime\prime}\ell}\leq n_{\ell}^{1/2}\{\mathcal{R}(\hat{\nu}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}\{\mathcal{P}(\hat{\eta}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}=L^{-1/2}\{n\mathcal{R}(\hat{\nu}_{\ell})\mathcal{P}(\hat{\eta}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}.

4.

Combining terms yields the desired result. ∎

Lemma
\thechapter
.F.7

(Oracle approximation)
.

Suppose the conditions of Theorem
\thechapter.F.1
hold. Then with probability
1-\epsilon
,
\frac{n^{1/2}}{\sigma}|\hat{\theta}-\bar{\theta}|\leq\Delta
where
\Delta
equals

\displaystyle\frac{7L}{2\epsilon\sigma}\bigg[(1+\bar{\eta})\{\mathcal{R}(\hat{\nu}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}+(\bar{\alpha}+\bar{\eta})\{\mathcal{R}(\hat{\delta}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}+\bar{\sigma}_{1}\{\mathcal{R}(\hat{\alpha}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}+\bar{\sigma}_{2}\{\mathcal{R}(\hat{\eta}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\quad+\{n\mathcal{R}(\hat{\nu}_{\ell})\mathcal{R}(\hat{\eta}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}+\{n\mathcal{R}(\hat{\delta}_{\ell})\mathcal{R}(\hat{\alpha}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}+\{n\mathcal{R}(\hat{\delta}_{\ell})\mathcal{R}(\hat{\eta}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}\bigg].

Proof.

We proceed in steps.

1.

By Lemma
\thechapter.F.4
, write

n^{1/2}(\hat{\theta}-\bar{\theta})=\frac{n^{1/2}}{n_{\ell}^{1/2}}\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}n_{\ell}^{1/2}(\hat{\theta}_{\ell}-\bar{\theta}_{\ell})=L^{1/2}\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\sum_{j=1}^{3}\Delta_{j\ell}.

2.

Define the events

\mathcal{E}_{\ell}=\{\text{for all }j\in\{1,...,7\},\;|\Delta_{j\ell}|\leq t_{j}\}
,
\mathcal{E}=\cap_{\ell=1}^{L}\mathcal{E}_{\ell}
, and
\mathcal{E}^{c}=\cup_{\ell=1}^{L}\mathcal{E}^{c}_{\ell}.

Hence by the union bound and Lemma
\thechapter.F.5
,

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}^{c})\leq\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}^{c}_{\ell})\leq L\frac{\epsilon}{L}=\epsilon.

3.

Therefore with probability
1-\epsilon
,

\displaystyle n^{1/2}|\hat{\theta}-\bar{\theta}|

\displaystyle\leq L^{1/2}\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\sum_{j=1}^{7}|\Delta_{jk}|\leq L^{1/2}\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\sum_{j=1}^{7}t_{j}=L^{1/2}\sum_{j=1}^{7}t_{j}.

Finally, we simplify
(t_{j})
. Note that
7^{1/2}<7/2
and that for
\epsilon\leq 1
,
\epsilon^{-1/2}\leq\epsilon^{-1}
. ∎

Lemma
\thechapter
.F.8

(Oracle approximation: Alternative path)
.

Suppose the conditions of Theorem
\thechapter.F.1
hold. Then with probability
1-\epsilon
,
\frac{n^{1/2}}{\sigma}|\hat{\theta}-\bar{\theta}|\leq\Delta
where
\Delta
equals

\displaystyle\frac{4L}{\epsilon^{1/2}\sigma}\bigg[(1+\bar{\eta}+\bar{\eta}^{\prime})\{\mathcal{R}(\hat{\nu}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}+(\bar{\alpha}+\bar{\alpha}^{\prime}+\bar{\eta}+\bar{\eta}^{\prime})\{\mathcal{R}(\hat{\delta}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}+\bar{\sigma}_{1}\{\mathcal{R}(\hat{\alpha}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}+\bar{\sigma}_{2}\{\mathcal{R}(\hat{\eta}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}\bigg]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2\sigma}\bigg[\{n\mathcal{P}(\hat{\nu}_{\ell})\mathcal{R}(\hat{\eta}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}\wedge\{n\mathcal{R}(\hat{\nu}_{\ell})\mathcal{P}(\hat{\eta}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}+\{n\mathcal{P}(\hat{\delta}_{\ell})\mathcal{R}(\hat{\alpha}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}\wedge\{n\mathcal{R}(\hat{\delta}_{\ell})\mathcal{P}(\hat{\alpha}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\{n\mathcal{P}(\hat{\delta}_{\ell})\mathcal{R}(\hat{\eta}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}\wedge\{n\mathcal{R}(\hat{\delta}_{\ell})\mathcal{P}(\hat{\eta}_{\ell})\}^{1/2}\bigg].

Proof.

As in Lemma
\thechapter.F.7
, Lemmas
\thechapter.F.4
and
\thechapter.F.6
imply that with probability
1-\epsilon
,
n^{1/2}|\hat{\theta}-\bar{\theta}|\leq L^{1/2}\sum_{j=1}^{3}t_{j}.

Note
7^{1/2}+(7/4)^{1/2}<4
when combining terms.
∎

Proof of Theorem
\thechapter.F.1
.

The steps of
Chernozhukov et al., 2023
generalize to our setting, using our new
\Delta
defined in Lemmas
\thechapter.F.7
and
\thechapter.F.8
.
∎

\thechapter
.F.4
Variance estimation

Recall that
\mathbb{E}_{\ell}(\cdot)=n_{\ell}^{-1}\sum_{i\in I_{\ell}}(\cdot)
means the average over observations in
I_{\ell}
and
\mathbb{E}_{n}(\cdot)=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\cdot)
means the average over all observations in the sample. For
i\in I_{\ell}
, define
\psi_{0}(W_{i})=\psi(W_{i},\theta_{0},\nu_{0},\delta_{0},\alpha_{0},\eta_{0})
and
\hat{\psi}(W_{i})=\psi(W_{i},\hat{\theta},\hat{\nu}_{\ell},\hat{\delta}_{\ell},\hat{\alpha}_{\ell},\hat{\eta}_{\ell})
.

Lemma
\thechapter
.F.9

(Foldwise second moment)
.

\mathbb{E}_{\ell}[\{\hat{\psi}(W)-\psi_{0}(W)\}^{2}]\leq 7\left\{(\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0})^{2}+\sum_{j=8}^{13}\Delta_{j\ell}\right\},

where

\Delta_{8\ell}=\mathbb{E}_{\ell}\{s(W_{i})^{2}\}
,
\Delta_{9\ell}=\mathbb{E}_{\ell}[u(W_{i})^{2}\{Y-\delta_{0}(W_{i})\}^{2}]
,
\Delta_{10\ell}=\mathbb{E}_{\ell}[v(W_{i})^{2}\{\delta_{0}(W_{i})-\nu_{0}(W_{i})\}^{2}]
,
\Delta_{11\ell}=\mathbb{E}_{\ell}\{\hat{\alpha}_{\ell}(W_{i})^{2}t(W_{i})^{2}\}
,
\Delta_{12\ell}=\mathbb{E}_{\ell}\{\hat{\eta}_{\ell}(W_{i})^{2}t(W_{i})^{2}\}
,
\Delta_{13\ell}=\mathbb{E}_{\ell}\{\hat{\eta}_{\ell}(W_{i})^{2}u(W_{i})^{2}\}
.

Proof.

Write
\hat{\psi}(W_{i})-\psi_{0}(W_{i})
equal to

\displaystyle\hat{\nu}_{\ell}(W_{i})+\hat{\alpha}_{\ell}(W_{i})\{Y_{i}-\hat{\delta}_{\ell}(W_{i})\}+\hat{\eta}_{\ell}(W_{i})\{\hat{\delta}_{\ell}(W_{i})-\hat{\nu}_{\ell}(W_{i})\}-\hat{\theta}

\displaystyle\quad-\left[\nu_{0}(W)+\alpha_{0}(W_{i})\{Y_{i}-\delta_{0}(W_{i})\}+\eta_{0}(W_{i})\{\delta_{0}(W_{i})-\nu_{0}(W_{i})\}-\theta_{0}\right]

\displaystyle\quad\pm\hat{\alpha}_{\ell}\{Y-\delta_{0}(W_{i})\}\pm\hat{\eta}_{\ell}\{\delta_{0}(W_{i})-\nu_{0}(W_{i})\}

\displaystyle=(\theta_{0}-\hat{\theta})+s(W_{i})+u(W_{i})\{Y-\delta_{0}(W_{i})\}+v(W_{i})\{\delta_{0}(W_{i})-\nu_{0}(W_{i})\}

\displaystyle\quad-\hat{\alpha}_{\ell}(W_{i})t(W_{i})+\hat{\eta}_{\ell}(W_{i})t(W_{i})-\hat{\eta}_{\ell}(W_{i})u(W_{i}).

Apply parallelogram law across the seven terms, and take
\mathbb{E}_{\ell}(\cdot)
of both sides.
∎

Lemma
\thechapter
.F.10

(Residuals)
.

Suppose the conditions of Theorem
\thechapter.F.2
hold. Then with probability
1-\epsilon^{\prime}/(2L)
,

|\Delta_{8\ell}|\leq t_{8}=\frac{12L}{\epsilon^{\prime}}\mathcal{R}(\hat{\nu}_{\ell})
,
|\Delta_{9\ell}|\leq t_{9}=\frac{12L}{\epsilon^{\prime}}\bar{\sigma}_{1}^{2}\mathcal{R}(\hat{\alpha}_{\ell})
,
|\Delta_{10\ell}|\leq t_{10}=\frac{12L}{\epsilon^{\prime}}\bar{\sigma}_{2}^{2}\mathcal{R}(\hat{\eta}_{\ell})
,
|\Delta_{11\ell}|\leq t_{11}=\frac{12L}{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\bar{\alpha}^{\prime})^{2}\mathcal{R}(\hat{\delta}_{\ell})
,
|\Delta_{12\ell}|\leq t_{12}=\frac{12L}{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\bar{\eta}^{\prime})^{2}\mathcal{R}(\hat{\delta}_{\ell})
,
|\Delta_{13\ell}|\leq t_{13}=\frac{12L}{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\bar{\eta}^{\prime})^{2}\mathcal{R}(\hat{\alpha}_{\ell}).

Proof.

The steps are analogous to Lemma
\thechapter.F.5
.
∎

Lemma
\thechapter
.F.11

(Oracle approximation)
.

Suppose the conditions of Lemma
\thechapter.F.10
hold. Then with probability
1-\epsilon^{\prime}/2
,
\mathbb{E}_{n}[\{\hat{\psi}(W)-\psi_{0}(W)\}^{2}]\leq\Delta^{\prime}
where

\displaystyle\Delta^{\prime}=7(\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0})^{2}+\frac{84L}{\epsilon^{\prime}}\left[\mathcal{R}(\hat{\nu}_{\ell})+\{(\bar{\alpha}^{\prime})^{2}+(\bar{\eta}^{\prime})^{2}\}\mathcal{R}(\hat{\delta}_{\ell})+\{(\bar{\eta}^{\prime})^{2}+\bar{\sigma}_{1}^{2}\}\mathcal{R}(\hat{\alpha}_{\ell})+\bar{\sigma}_{2}^{2}\mathcal{R}(\hat{\eta}_{\ell})\right].

Proof.

The steps are analogous to Lemma
\thechapter.F.7
, appealing to Lemmas
\thechapter.F.9
and
\thechapter.F.10
.
∎

Lemma
\thechapter
.F.12

(Markov inequality)
.

If
\chi<\infty
, then with probability
1-\epsilon^{\prime}/2

|\mathbb{E}_{n}\{\psi_{0}(W)^{2}\}-\sigma^{2}|\leq\Delta^{\prime\prime}=\left(\frac{2}{\epsilon^{\prime}}\right)^{1/2}\frac{\chi^{2}}{n^{1/2}}.

Proof.

The steps of
Chernozhukov et al., 2023
generalize to our setting, using our new moments.
∎

Proof of Theorem
\thechapter.F.2
.

The steps of
Chernozhukov et al., 2023
generalize to our setting, using our new
(\Delta^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime\prime})
defined in Lemmas
\thechapter.F.11
and
\thechapter.F.12
, respectively.
∎

Appendix
\thechapter
.G
Extension to causal functions

We revisit causal functions to clarify how the main inference result encompasses them. We pointwise approximate the causal function
\theta_{0}(v)
, e.g. the heterogeneous long term effect for the subpopulation with
V=v
, by the local functional
\theta_{\lambda}(v)
, e.g. the heterogeneous long term effect for the subpopulation with
V
close to
v
. We emphasize which quantities depend on the bandwidth by indexing by
\lambda
, e.g.
\sigma_{\lambda}^{2}
is the variance of the approximating Gaussian when
\lambda>0
. Moreover, we write
h_{j,\lambda}=\ell_{\lambda}h_{j}
where
h_{j,\lambda}
is the nuisance of the local functional and
h_{j}
is the nuisance of the corresponding global functional. For example,
h_{j,\lambda}
is the nuisance for the (approximate) heterogeneous long term effect, while
h_{j}
is the nuisance for the average long term effect.

\thechapter
.G.1
Main result

Theorem
\thechapter
.G.1

(Key quantities for causal functions in Algorithm
3
)
.

Suppose that Assumption
9
holds. Suppose that bounded balancing weight, residual variance, density, derivative, and kernel conditions hold, which are defined below. Then for the local functional
\theta_{\lambda}
, suppressing the index
v
,

\kappa_{\lambda}/\sigma_{\lambda}\lesssim\lambda^{-1/6}
,
\sigma_{\lambda}\asymp\lambda^{-1/2}
,
\kappa_{\lambda}\lesssim\lambda^{-2/3}
,
\chi_{\lambda}\lesssim\lambda^{-3/4},

and

\bar{\sigma}_{1,\lambda}\lesssim\lambda^{-1}\bar{\sigma}_{1}
,
\bar{\sigma}_{2,\lambda}\lesssim\lambda^{-1}\bar{\sigma}_{2}
,
\Delta_{\lambda}\lesssim n^{1/2}\lambda^{s+1/2},

where
s
is the order of differentiability defined below. Moreover,

\|\hat{h}_{1,\lambda}-h_{1,\lambda}\|_{2}\lesssim\lambda^{-1}\|\hat{h}_{1}-h_{1}\|_{2}
,
\|T_{1}(\hat{h}_{1,\lambda}-h_{1,\lambda})\|_{2}\lesssim\lambda^{-1}\|T_{1}(\hat{h}_{1}-h_{1})\|_{2}
,
\|\hat{h}_{2,\lambda}-h_{2,\lambda}\|_{2}\lesssim\lambda^{-1}\|\hat{h}_{2}-h_{2}\|_{2}
,
\|T_{2}(\hat{h}_{2,\lambda}-h_{2,\lambda})\|_{2}\lesssim\lambda^{-1}\|T_{2}(\hat{h}_{2}-h_{2})\|_{2}.

Corollary
\thechapter
.G.1

(Multiple robustness to ill posedness: Causal functions)
.

Suppose Assumptions
9
and
10
hold as well as the regularity conditions of Theorem
\thechapter.G.1
.
Finally assume the following are
o_{p}(1)
: the bandwidth rates
n^{-1/2}\lambda^{-3/2}
and
n^{1/2}\lambda^{s+1/2}
;
the individual rates
(\lambda^{-1}+\lambda^{-1/2}\bar{h}_{4}+\lambda^{-1/2}\bar{h}_{4}^{\prime})\|\hat{h}_{1}-h_{1}\|_{2}
,

(\lambda^{-1/2}\bar{h}_{3}+\lambda^{-1}\bar{h}_{3}^{\prime}+\lambda^{-1/2}\bar{h}_{4}+\lambda^{-1}\bar{h}_{4}^{\prime})\|\hat{h}_{2}-h_{2}\|_{2}
,

(\bar{h}_{4}^{\prime}+\lambda^{-1}\bar{\sigma}_{y}\|\hat{h}_{3}-h_{3}\|_{2}
,

\lambda^{-1}\bar{\sigma}_{2}\|\hat{h}_{4}-h_{4}\|_{2}
; and the product rates

1.

\lambda^{-1/2}n^{1/2}\{\|\hat{h}_{1}-h_{1}\|_{2}\|\hat{h}_{4}-h_{4}\|_{2}\wedge\|T_{1}(\hat{h}_{1}-h_{1})\|_{2}\|\hat{h}_{4}-h_{4}\|_{2}\wedge\|\hat{h}_{1}-h_{1}\|_{2}\|T_{4}(\hat{h}_{4}-h_{4})\|_{2}\}
;

2.

\lambda^{-1/2}n^{1/2}\{\|\hat{h}_{2}-h_{2}\|_{2}\|\hat{h}_{3}-h_{3}\|_{2}\wedge\|T_{2}(\hat{h}_{2}-h_{2})\|_{2}\|\hat{h}_{3}-h_{3}\|_{2}\wedge\|\hat{h}_{2}-h_{2}\|_{2}\|T_{3}(\hat{h}_{3}-h_{3})\|_{2}\}
;

3.

\lambda^{-1/2}n^{1/2}\{\|\hat{h}_{2}-h_{2}\|_{2}\|\hat{h}_{4}-h_{4}\|_{2}\wedge\|T_{2}(\hat{h}_{2}-h_{2})\|_{2}\|\hat{h}_{4}-h_{4}\|_{2}\wedge\|\hat{h}_{2}-h_{2}\|_{2}\|T_{4}(\hat{h}_{4}-h_{4})\|_{2}\}
.

Then

\hat{\theta}_{\lambda}\overset{p}{\rightarrow}\theta_{0}
,
\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\sigma_{\lambda}}(\hat{\theta}_{\lambda}-\theta_{0})\overset{d}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, and
\mathbb{P}\{\theta_{0}\in(\hat{\theta}_{\lambda}\pm 1.96\hat{\sigma}_{\lambda}n^{-1/2})\}\rightarrow 0.95.

\thechapter
.G.2
Extended notation

While proving this result, we use the notation of Appendix
\thechapter.F.2
to eliminate some subscripts. We emphasize which quantities are diverging sequences for local functionals by indexing with the bandwidth
\lambda
. We study a function of the variable
V\subset W
, i.e.
\theta_{0}(v)
, which we approximate with
\theta_{0,\lambda}(v)=\mathbb{E}\{\ell_{\lambda}(V)\nu_{0}(W)\}=\mathbb{E}\{\nu_{0,\lambda}(W)\}
. Denote the localized moment function

\psi_{\lambda}(W,\theta_{\lambda},\nu_{\lambda},\delta_{\lambda},\alpha,\eta)=\nu_{\lambda}(W)+\alpha(W)\{Y_{\lambda}-\delta_{\lambda}(W)\}+\eta(W)\{\delta_{\lambda}(W)-\nu_{\lambda}(W)\}-\theta_{\lambda}

where
\nu_{\lambda}(W)=\ell_{\lambda}(V)\nu(W)
,
\delta_{\lambda}(W)=\ell_{\lambda}(V)\delta(W)
, and
Y_{\lambda}=\ell_{\lambda}(V)Y
. To use Theorem
\thechapter.F.1
, we reduce the rates for the localized nuisances
(\hat{\nu}_{\lambda},\hat{\delta}_{\lambda})
to the rates for the global nuisances
(\hat{\nu},\hat{\delta})
. Intuitively, we expect the former to be slower than the latter.

The moments of the localized moment function are

0=\mathbb{E}\{\psi_{0,\lambda}(W)\}
,
\sigma_{\lambda}^{2}=\mathbb{E}\{\psi_{0,\lambda}(W)^{2}\}
,
\kappa_{\lambda}^{3}=\mathbb{E}\{|\psi_{0,\lambda}(W)|^{3}\}
, and
\chi_{\lambda}^{4}=\mathbb{E}\{\psi_{0,\lambda}(W)^{4}\}.

The moments
(\sigma_{\lambda},\kappa_{\lambda},\chi_{\lambda})
are indexed by
\lambda
, so a complete analysis must also characterize how these parameters diverge as the bandwidth
\lambda
vanishes. Doing so will verify the regularity condition on moments and also pin down the nonparametric rate of Gaussian approximation
\sigma_{\lambda}n^{-1/2}
.

Finally, the residual variances must also be updated. With localization, they become

\mathbb{E}[\{Y_{\lambda}-\delta_{0,\lambda}(W)\}^{2}\mid W]\leq\bar{\sigma}_{1,\lambda}^{2}
and
\mathbb{E}[\{\delta_{0,\lambda}(W)-\nu_{0,\lambda}(W_{1})\}^{2}\mid W_{1}]\leq\bar{\sigma}_{2,\lambda}^{2}.

A complete analysis must also characterize how these parameters diverge as bandwidth
\lambda
vanishes.

We restate the conclusions of Theorem
\thechapter.G.1
that we wish to prove in this alternative notation. Suppose that the global residual variances are finite. Suppose bounded balancing weight, residual, density, derivative, and kernel conditions hold. Then for local functionals,

\kappa_{\lambda}/\sigma_{\lambda}\lesssim\lambda^{-1/6}
,
\sigma_{\lambda}\asymp\lambda^{-1/2}
,
\kappa_{\lambda}\lesssim\lambda^{-2/3}
,
\chi_{\lambda}\lesssim\lambda^{-3/4}

and

\bar{\sigma}_{1,\lambda}\lesssim\lambda^{-1}\bar{\sigma}_{1}
,
\bar{\sigma}_{2,\lambda}\lesssim\lambda^{-1}\bar{\sigma}_{2}
,
\Delta_{\lambda}\lesssim n^{1/2}\lambda^{s+1/2}

where
s
is the order of differentiability. Moreover,

\mathcal{R}(\hat{\nu}_{\ell,\lambda})\lesssim\lambda^{-2}\mathcal{R}(\hat{\nu}_{\ell})
,
\mathcal{P}(\hat{\nu}_{\ell,\lambda})\lesssim\lambda^{-2}\mathcal{P}(\hat{\nu}_{\ell})
,
\mathcal{R}(\hat{\delta}_{\ell,\lambda})\lesssim\lambda^{-2}\mathcal{R}(\hat{\delta}_{\ell})
,
\mathcal{P}(\hat{\delta}_{\ell,\lambda})\lesssim\lambda^{-2}\mathcal{P}(\hat{\delta}_{\ell}).

\thechapter
.G.3
Oracle moments

To lighten notation, we write the local weighting as
\ell=\ell_{\lambda}
. We also suppress the arguments of functions and define
U_{0}=\nu_{0}-\mathbb{E}(\nu_{0})
,
U_{1}=Y-\delta_{0}
,
U_{2}=\delta_{0}-\nu_{0}

so that

\psi_{0,\lambda}=\ell\cdot(U_{0}+\alpha_{0}U_{1}+\eta_{0}U_{2}).

Finally, we lighten notation by defining

\|W\|_{\mathbb{P},q}=\{\mathbb{E}(W^{q})\}^{1/q}.

Lemma
\thechapter
.G.1

(Oracle moments for local functionals)
.

Suppose there exist

(\underline{\alpha},\bar{\alpha},\underline{\eta},\bar{\eta},\underline{\sigma}_{0},\bar{\sigma}_{0},\underline{\sigma}_{1},\bar{\sigma}_{1},\underline{\sigma}_{2},\bar{\sigma}_{2},\underline{f},\bar{f},\bar{f}^{\prime},\lambda_{0})

bounded away from zero and above such that the following conditions hold.

1.

Control of balancing weights:
\underline{\alpha}\leq\|\alpha_{0}\|_{\infty}\leq\bar{\alpha}
,
\underline{\eta}\leq\|\eta_{0}\|_{\infty}\leq\bar{\eta}
.

2.

Control of residual moments: for
q\in\{2,3,4\}
,
\underline{\sigma}_{0}\leq\|U_{0}|V\|_{\mathbb{P},q}\leq\bar{\sigma}_{0}
,
\underline{\sigma}_{1}\leq\|U_{1}|W\|_{\mathbb{P},q}\leq\bar{\sigma}_{1}
,
\underline{\sigma}_{2}\leq\|U_{2}|W_{1}\|_{\mathbb{P},q}\leq\bar{\sigma}_{2}.

3.

Bounded density: the density
f_{V}
obeys, for all
v^{\prime}\in N_{\lambda_{0}}(v)=(v^{\prime}:|v^{\prime}-v|\leq\lambda_{0})
,

\underline{f}\leq f_{V}(v^{\prime})\leq\bar{f}
and
|\partial f_{V}(v^{\prime})|\leq\bar{f}^{\prime}.

Then

\frac{\kappa_{\lambda}}{\sigma_{\lambda}}\lesssim\lambda^{-1/6}
,
\sigma_{\lambda}\asymp\lambda^{-1/2}
,
\kappa_{\lambda}\lesssim\lambda^{-2/3}
,
\chi_{\lambda}\lesssim\lambda^{-3/4}.

Proof.

We extend
Chernozhukov et al., 2022b
. We proceed in steps.

1.

Observe that
\sigma_{\lambda}^{2}=\mathbb{E}\{\ell^{2}\cdot(U_{0}^{2}+\alpha_{0}^{2}U_{1}^{2}+\eta_{0}^{2}U_{2}^{2}+2\alpha_{0}U_{0}U_{1}+2\eta_{0}U_{0}U_{2}+2\alpha_{0}\eta_{0}U_{1}U_{2})\}
equals
\mathbb{E}\{\ell^{2}\cdot(U_{0}^{2}+\alpha_{0}^{2}U_{1}^{2}+\eta_{0}^{2}U_{2}^{2})\}
by Assumption
9
. In particular, we use

\mathbb{E}[\{\alpha(W)-\alpha_{0}(W)\}U_{1}]=0
and
\mathbb{E}[\{\eta(W)-\eta_{0}(W)\}U_{2}]=0.

Hence

(\underline{\sigma}_{0}^{2}+\underline{\alpha}^{2}\underline{\sigma}_{1}^{2}+\underline{\eta}^{2}\underline{\sigma}_{2}^{2})\|\ell\|_{\mathbb{P},2}\leq\sigma_{\lambda}^{2}\leq(\bar{\sigma}_{0}^{2}+\bar{\alpha}^{2}\bar{\sigma}_{1}^{2}+\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\sigma}_{2}^{2})\|\ell\|_{\mathbb{P},2}.

Moreover,
\|\psi_{0,\lambda}\|_{\mathbb{P},q}\leq(\bar{\sigma}_{0}+\bar{\alpha}\bar{\sigma}_{1}+\bar{\eta}\bar{\sigma}_{2})\|\ell\|_{\mathbb{P},q}.

In summary,

\|\ell\|_{\mathbb{P},2}\lesssim\sigma_{\lambda}\lesssim\|\ell\|_{\mathbb{P},2}
and
\|\psi_{0,\lambda}\|_{\mathbb{P},q}\lesssim\|\ell\|_{\mathbb{P},q}.

2.

Consider the change of variables
u=(v^{\prime}-v)/\lambda
so that
\mathrm{d}u=\lambda^{-1}\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}
. Hence

\displaystyle\|\ell\|^{q}_{\mathbb{P},q}\omega^{q}

\displaystyle=\|\ell\omega\|^{q}_{\mathbb{P},q}=\int\lambda^{-q}\left|K\left(\frac{v^{\prime}-v}{\lambda}\right)\right|^{q}f_{V}(v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}=\int\lambda^{-(q-1)}|K(u)|^{q}f_{V}(v-u\lambda)\mathrm{d}u.

It follows that

\lambda^{-(q-1)/q}\underline{f}^{1/q}\left(\int|K|^{q}\right)^{1/q}\leq\|\ell\|_{\mathbb{P},q}\omega\leq\lambda^{-(q-1)/q}\bar{f}^{1/q}\left(\int|K|^{q}\right)^{1/q}.

Further, we have that

\omega=\int\lambda^{-1}K\left(\frac{v^{\prime}-v}{\lambda}\right)f_{V}(v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}=\int K(u)f_{V}(v-u\lambda)\mathrm{d}u

and

\int K(u)f_{V}(v-0u)\mathrm{d}u=\int K(u)f_{V}(v)\mathrm{d}u=f_{V}(v).

Using the Taylor expansion in
\lambda
around
\lambda=0
and the Holder inequality, there exist some
\tilde{\lambda}\in[0,\lambda]
such that

|\omega-f_{V}(v)|=\left|\lambda\int K(u)\partial_{v}f_{V}(v-u\tilde{\lambda})u\mathrm{d}u\right|\leq\lambda\bar{f}^{\prime}\int|u||K(u)|du.

Hence there exists some
\lambda_{1}\in(\lambda,\lambda_{0})
depending only on
(K,\bar{f}^{\prime},\underline{f},\bar{f})
such that

\underline{f}/2\leq\omega\leq 2\bar{f}.

In summary,

\lambda^{-(q-1)/q}\underline{f}^{1/q}\left(\int|K|^{q}\right)^{1/q}\frac{1}{2\bar{f}}\leq\|\ell\|_{\mathbb{P},q}\leq\lambda^{-(q-1)/q}\bar{f}^{1/q}\left(\int|K|^{q}\right)^{1/q}\frac{2}{\underline{f}}

which implies

\lambda^{-(q-1)/q}\lesssim\|\ell\|_{\mathbb{P},q}\lesssim\lambda^{-(q-1)/q}.

3.

For all
\lambda<\lambda_{1}
,

\sigma_{\lambda}\asymp\|\ell\|_{\mathbb{P},2}
,
\|\psi_{0,\lambda}\|_{\mathbb{P},2}\lesssim\|\ell\|_{\mathbb{P},q}
,
\|\ell\|_{\mathbb{P},q}\asymp\lambda^{-(q-1)/q}.
∎

\thechapter
.G.4
Residual variances and mean square rates

Lemma
\thechapter
.G.2

(Residual variance for local functionals)
.

Suppose there exist
(\bar{\sigma}_{1},\bar{\sigma}_{2},\underline{f},\bar{f},\bar{f}^{\prime},\lambda_{0},\bar{K})
bounded away from zero and above such that the following conditions hold.

1.

Bounded residual variance:

\|U_{1}|W\|_{\mathbb{P},2}\leq\bar{\sigma}_{1}
,
\|U_{2}|W_{1}\|_{\mathbb{P},2}\leq\bar{\sigma}_{2}.

2.

Bounded density: the density
f_{V}
obeys, for all
v^{\prime}\in N_{\lambda_{0}}(v)
,

0<\underline{f}\leq f_{V}(v^{\prime})\leq\bar{f}
,
|\partial f_{V}(v^{\prime})|\leq\bar{f}^{\prime}.

3.

Bounded kernel:
|K(u)|\leq\bar{K}
.

Then

\bar{\sigma}_{1,\lambda}\lesssim\lambda^{-1}\bar{\sigma}_{1}
and
\bar{\sigma}_{2,\lambda}\lesssim\lambda^{-1}\bar{\sigma}_{2}.

Proof.

Write

\bar{\sigma}_{1,\lambda}=\|\ell\cdot U_{1}|W\|_{\mathbb{P},2}\leq\|\ell\|_{\infty}\|U_{1}|W\|_{\mathbb{P},2}.

By the proof of Lemma
\thechapter.G.1
,

\|\ell\|_{\infty}=\left\|\frac{1}{\lambda\omega}K\left(\frac{v^{\prime}-v}{\lambda}\right)\right\|_{\infty}\leq\bar{K}\frac{1}{\lambda\omega}\leq\bar{K}\frac{2}{\lambda\underline{f}}.

Therefore

\bar{\sigma}_{1,\lambda}\leq\bar{K}\frac{2}{\lambda\underline{f}}\cdot\bar{\sigma}_{\lambda}\lesssim\lambda^{-1}\bar{\sigma}_{1}.

The argument for
\bar{\sigma}_{2,\lambda}
is identical.
∎

A natural choice of estimator
\hat{\nu}_{\lambda}
for
\nu_{0,\lambda}
is the localization
\ell_{\lambda}
times an estimator
\hat{\nu}
for
\nu_{0}
. We prove that this choice translates global nuisance parameter rates into local nuisance parameter rates under mild regularity conditions.

Lemma
\thechapter
.G.3

(Translating global rates to local rates)
.

Suppose the conditions of Lemma
\thechapter.G.2
hold. Then

\mathcal{R}(\hat{\nu}_{\ell,\lambda})\lesssim\lambda^{-2}\mathcal{R}(\hat{\nu}_{\ell})
,
\mathcal{P}(\hat{\nu}_{\ell,\lambda})\lesssim\lambda^{-2}\mathcal{P}(\hat{\nu}_{\ell})
,
\mathcal{R}(\hat{\delta}_{\ell,\lambda})\lesssim\lambda^{-2}\mathcal{R}(\hat{\delta}_{\ell})
,
\mathcal{P}(\hat{\delta}_{\ell,\lambda})\lesssim\lambda^{-2}\mathcal{P}(\hat{\delta}_{\ell}).

Proof.

We generalize
Chernozhukov et al., 2023
.
Write

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\hat{\nu}_{\ell,\lambda})=\mathbb{E}[\{\ell_{\lambda}(V)\hat{\nu}_{\ell}(W)-\ell_{\lambda}(V)\nu_{0}(W)\}^{2}\mid I^{c}_{\ell}]\leq\|\ell_{\lambda}\|^{2}_{\infty}\mathcal{R}(\hat{\nu}_{\ell}).

From the proof of Lemma
\thechapter.G.2
,
\|\ell_{\lambda}\|_{\infty}\lesssim\lambda^{-1}
. The remaining results are identical.
∎

\thechapter
.G.5
Approximation error

Finally, we characterize the finite sample approximation error
\Delta_{\lambda}=n^{1/2}\sigma^{-1}|\theta_{0,\lambda}-\theta_{0}|
where

\theta_{0}=\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow 0}\theta_{0,\lambda}.

Here,
\Delta_{\lambda}
is bias from approximating a causal function using sequence of local functionals.
We define
m(v)=\mathbb{E}\left\{\nu_{0}(W)\mid V=v\right\}

to lighten notation.

Lemma
\thechapter
.G.4

(Approximation error from localization
(
Chernozhukov et al., 2022b
)
)
.

Suppose there exist constants
(\lambda_{0},s,\bar{g}_{s},\bar{f}_{s},\underline{f},\bar{g})
bounded away from zero and above such that the following conditions hold.

1.

Differentiability: on
N_{\lambda_{0}}(v)=\{v^{\prime}:|v^{\prime}-v|\leq\lambda_{0}\}
,
m(v^{\prime})
and
f_{V}(v^{\prime})
are differentiable to the integer order
d
.

2.

Bounded derivatives: let
s=d\wedge o
where
o
is the order of the kernel
K
. Let
\partial^{s}_{v}
denote the
s
order derivative
\partial^{s}/(\partial v)^{s}
. Assume

\sup_{v^{\prime}\in N_{\lambda_{0}}(v)}\|\partial^{s}_{v}(m(v^{\prime})f_{V}(v^{\prime}))\|_{op}\leq\bar{g}_{s}
,
\sup_{v^{\prime}\in N_{\lambda_{0}}(v)}\|\partial^{s}_{v}f_{V}(v^{\prime})\|_{op}\leq\bar{f}_{s}
,
\inf_{v^{\prime}\in N_{\lambda_{0}}(v)}f_{V}(v^{\prime})\geq\underline{f}.

3.

Bounded conditional formula:

m(v)f_{V}(v)\leq\bar{g}.

Then there exist constants
(C,\lambda_{1})
depending only on
(\lambda_{0},K,s,\bar{g}_{s}
,
\bar{f}_{s}
,
\underline{f},\bar{g})
such that for all
\lambda_{1}\in(\lambda,\lambda_{0})
,
|\theta_{0,\lambda}-\theta_{0}|\leq C\lambda^{s}.

In summary,

\Delta_{\lambda}\lesssim n^{1/2}\lambda^{s+1/2}.

Appendix
\thechapter
.H
Proofs of propositions and corollaries

\thechapter
.H.1
Analytical examples of relative well posedness

Proof of Proposition
1
.

Here,
(g_{0},h_{0})
are real numbers solving
\mathbb{E}(YC^{\prime})=g\mathbb{E}(AC^{\prime})
and
h\mathbb{E}(BC)=g\mathbb{E}(AC)
. Clearly
S:g\mapsto\mathbb{E}(AC^{\prime})g
has operator norm
|\mathbb{E}(AC^{\prime})|
and
T:(0,g)\mapsto\mathbb{E}(BC)\cdot 0-\mathbb{E}(AC)g
has operator norm
|\mathbb{E}(AC)|
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2
.

By
Hoderlein and Holzmann, 2011
,

\sigma_{j}(T_{g})=|\rho_{A}|^{j}
and
\sigma_{j}(S)=|\rho^{\prime}|^{j}
. By Mehler’s formula, the right singular functions coincide as Hermite polynomials of
A
. Therefore,

\|(S^{*}S+\mu^{\prime}I)^{-1}T_{g}^{*}T_{g}\|_{\text{op}}=\frac{\sigma_{j}^{2}(T)}{\sigma_{j}^{2}(S)+\mu^{\prime}}=\frac{|\rho_{A}|^{2j}}{|\rho^{\prime}|^{2j}+\mu^{\prime}}\leq\frac{|\rho_{A}|^{2j}}{|\rho^{\prime}|^{2j}}
.
∎

Proposition
\thechapter
.H.1

(Nonlinear models with multivariate Gaussian data)
.

Suppose that
(A,B,C,C^{\prime})
are each multivariate normal vectors with mean zero and identity covariance, possibly of differing dimensions. Assumption
6
holds when
\max_{k\leq r\wedge r^{\prime}}\frac{|\rho_{k}|}{|\rho_{k}^{\prime}|}=O(1)
, where
\rho_{k}
and
\rho_{k}^{\prime}

are the
k
th canonical correlations between
(A,C)
and
(A,C^{\prime})
, respectively. In the maximization,
r
and
r^{\prime}
are the ranks of
\textsc{cov}(A,C)
and
\textsc{cov}(A,C^{\prime})
, respectively.

Proof.

The result is a straightforward generalization of Proposition
2
, using the tensorized Mehler’s formula.
∎

The quantity
\max_{k\leq r\wedge r^{\prime}}\frac{|\rho_{k}|}{|\rho_{k}^{\prime}|}
uniformly compares the relevance of the two instruments
C
and
C^{\prime}
for
A
, in each canonical direction. We require that
C^{\prime}
is at least as strong an instrument as
C
in every such direction. Here,
k\leq r\wedge r^{\prime}
means that we only compare the nondegenerate directions.

The result naturally generalizes to random vectors that are not mean zero and that do not have identity covariances, using heavier notation.

\thechapter
.H.2
Corollaries with compounding ill posedness

Proof of Corollary
1
.

The result is immediate from Lemma
\thechapter.C.5
.
∎

Proof of Corollary
3
.

To lighten notation, let
\bar{r}=\max\{\delta_{n},\|\hat{g}-g_{0}\|_{2}\}
. We minimize the mean square bound in Theorem
1
:
\|\hat{h}-h_{0}\|_{2}^{2}=O\{\mu^{\min(\beta,1)}\|w_{h}\|^{2}_{2}+\mu^{-1}\bar{r}^{2}\}
.

In the case
\beta\geq 1
, the first order condition is
\|w_{h}\|^{2}_{2}-\mu^{-2}\bar{r}^{2}=0
, suggesting
\mu\asymp\bar{r}
,
R_{n}\asymp\mu^{2}+\bar{r}^{2}\asymp\bar{r}^{2}
, and
\mu^{-1}R_{n}\asymp\bar{r}
.

In the case
\beta<1
, the first order condition is
\mu^{\beta-1}\beta\|w_{h}\|^{2}_{2}-\mu^{-2}\bar{r}^{2}=0
, suggesting
\mu\asymp\bar{r}^{\frac{2}{\beta+1}}
,
R_{n}\asymp\mu^{\beta+1}+\bar{r}^{2}\asymp\bar{r}^{2}
, and
\mu^{-1}R_{n}\asymp\bar{r}^{2-\frac{2}{\beta+1}}=\bar{r}^{\frac{2\beta}{\beta+1}}
.
∎

Proof of Corollary
4
.

By Corollary
3
, we set
\mu_{g}=\delta_{n}^{\frac{2}{\min(\beta_{g}^{\prime},1)+1}}
to obtain
\|\hat{g}-g_{0}\|_{2}^{2}=O\left\{\delta_{n}^{2\textsc{well}(\beta_{g}^{\prime})}\right\}
, which dominates
\delta_{n}^{2}
. Hence
\bar{r}^{2}=\delta_{n}^{2\textsc{well}(\beta_{g}^{\prime})}
. Thus by Corollary
3
, we set
\mu_{h}=\bar{r}^{\frac{2}{\min(\beta_{h},1)+1}}
to obtain
\|T(\hat{h}-h_{0})\|_{2}^{2}=O\left(\bar{r}^{2}\right)
and
\|\hat{h}-h_{0}\|_{2}^{2}=O\left\{\bar{r}^{2\textsc{well}(\beta_{h})}\right\}
.
∎

Proposition
\thechapter
.H.2

(Less robustness to ill posedness)
.

Suppose the conditions of Corollary
4
hold for
(\hat{h}_{1},\hat{h}_{2},\hat{h}_{3},\hat{h}_{4})
. Write the largest critical radius as
\bar{\delta}_{n}=\tilde{O}(n^{-\alpha})
, and the source conditions as
(\vec{\beta}_{1},\beta_{2},\vec{\beta}_{3},\beta_{4})
.
15
15

15

Recall that for several leading examples,
(h_{1},h_{3})
are nested NPIVs while
(h_{2},h_{4})
are NPIVs.
Set the regularizations as in Corollary
4
. Suppose
\sigma\asymp n^{\gamma}
. Then product rate condition of Theorem
3
are satisfied when
(i)

\gamma+\alpha\left\{\textsc{well}(\beta_{1g}^{\prime})\textsc{well}(\beta_{1h})+1\right\}>1/2;

(ii)
\gamma+\alpha\left\{\textsc{well}(\beta_{3g}^{\prime})\textsc{well}(\beta_{3h})+1\right\}>1/2;

(iii)
\gamma+\alpha\left\{\textsc{well}(\beta_{2})\vee\textsc{well}(\beta_{4})+1\right\}>1/2.

Proof.

To begin, recall the rates we have derived. By Corollary
3
,

\|S(\hat{g}-g_{0})\|_{2}=O(\bar{\delta}_{n})
and
\|\hat{g}-g_{0}\|_{2}=O\left\{\bar{\delta}_{n}^{\textsc{well}(\beta_{g}^{\prime})}\right\}
. By Corollary
4
,

\|T(\hat{h}-h_{0})\|_{2}=O\left\{\bar{\delta}_{n}^{\textsc{well}(\beta_{g}^{\prime})}\right\}
and
\|\hat{h}-h_{0}\|_{2}=O\left\{\bar{\delta}_{n}^{\textsc{well}(\beta_{h})\textsc{well}(\beta_{g}^{\prime})}\right\}
.

1.

The first product rate condition of Theorem
3
is satisfied when

n^{1/2}\sigma^{-1}\{\|T_{1}(\hat{h}_{1}-h_{1})\|_{2}\|\hat{h}_{4}-h_{4}\|_{2}\wedge\|\hat{h}_{1}-h_{1}\|_{2}\|T_{4}(\hat{h}_{4}-h_{4})\|_{2}\}=o_{p}(1).

Since
h_{1}
is a nested NPIV and
h_{4}
is an NPIV, the former term is

n^{1/2}\sigma^{-1}\|T_{1}(\hat{h}_{1}-h_{1})\|_{2}\|\hat{h}_{4}-h_{4}\|_{2}=O\left[n^{\frac{1}{2}-\gamma-\alpha\left\{\textsc{well}(\beta_{1g}^{\prime})+\textsc{well}(\beta_{4})\right\}}\right].

Meanwhile the latter term is

n^{1/2}\sigma^{-1}\|\hat{h}_{1}-h_{1}\|_{2}\|T_{4}(\hat{h}_{4}-h_{4})\|_{2}=O\left[n^{\frac{1}{2}-\gamma-\alpha\left\{\textsc{well}(\beta_{1h})\textsc{well}(\beta_{1g}^{\prime})+1\right\}}\right].

In summary, the first product rate condition requires

\left[1/2-\gamma-\alpha\left\{\textsc{well}(\beta_{1g}^{\prime})+\textsc{well}(\beta_{4})\right\}\right]\wedge\left[1/2-\gamma-\alpha\left\{\textsc{well}(\beta_{1h})\textsc{well}(\beta_{1g}^{\prime})+1\right\}\right]<0.

Rearranging,

\frac{1}{2}<\gamma+\alpha\left[\left\{\textsc{well}(\beta_{1g}^{\prime})+\textsc{well}(\beta_{4})\right\}\vee\left\{\textsc{well}(\beta_{1h})\textsc{well}(\beta_{1g}^{\prime})+1\right\}\right].

The latter branch of the maximum weakly dominates the former since
\textsc{well}(\beta)\in[0,1/2]
and
\textsc{well}(\beta_{1g}^{\prime})
appears in both branches.

2.

The second product rate condition is similar.
Again, the latter branch weakly dominates the former.

3.

For the third product rate condition, we require

n^{1/2}\sigma^{-1}\{\|T_{2}(\hat{h}_{2}-h_{2})\|_{2}\|\hat{h}_{4}-h_{4}\|_{2}\wedge\|\hat{h}_{2}-h_{2}\|_{2}\|T_{4}(\hat{h}_{4}-h_{4})\|_{2}\}.

Since
(h_{2},h_{4})
are NPIVs, the former term is

n^{1/2}\sigma^{-1}\|T_{2}(\hat{h}_{2}-h_{2})\|_{2}\|\hat{h}_{4}-h_{4}\|_{2}=O\left[n^{\frac{1}{2}-\gamma-\alpha\left\{1+\textsc{well}(\beta_{4})\right\}}\right].

Meanwhile the latter term is

n^{1/2}\sigma^{-1}\|\hat{h}_{2}-h_{2}\|_{2}\|T_{4}(\hat{h}_{4}-h_{4})\|_{2}=O\left[n^{\frac{1}{2}-\gamma-\alpha\left\{\textsc{well}(\beta_{2})+1\right\}}\right].

In summary, the third product rate condition requires

\left[1/2-\gamma-\alpha\left\{1+\textsc{well}(\beta_{4})\right\}\right]\wedge\left[1/2-\gamma-\alpha\left\{\textsc{well}(\beta_{2})+1\right\}\right]<0.

Rearranging,

\frac{1}{2}<\gamma+\alpha\left[\left\{1+\textsc{well}(\beta_{4})\right\}\vee\left\{\textsc{well}(\beta_{2})+1\right\}\right].
∎

Proof of Corollary
\thechapter.B.1
.

The result is immediate from Theorem
\thechapter.B.1
.
∎

\thechapter
.H.3
Corollaries without compounding ill posedness

Proof of Corollary
2
.

The result is immediate from Lemmas
\thechapter.C.5
and
\thechapter.D.3
.
∎

Proof of Corollary
5
.

To lighten notation, let
\underline{\beta}=\min(\beta_{h},\beta_{g}^{\prime},\beta_{g})
and
\|\bar{w}\|_{2}^{2}=\max(\|w_{h}\|_{2}^{2},\|w_{g}^{\prime}\|^{2}_{2},\|w_{g}\|^{2}_{2})
. We minimize the mean square bound in Theorem
2
:
\|\hat{h}-h_{0}\|_{2}^{2}=O\{\mu^{\min(\underline{\beta},1)}\|\bar{w}\|_{2}^{2}+\mu^{-1}\delta_{n}^{2}\}
.

In the case
\underline{\beta}\geq 1
, the first order condition is
\|\bar{w}\|^{2}_{2}-\mu^{-2}\delta_{n}^{2}=0
, suggesting
\mu\asymp\delta_{n}
,
R_{n}\asymp\mu^{2}+\delta_{n}^{2}\asymp\delta_{n}^{2}
, and
\mu^{-1}R_{n}\asymp\delta_{n}
.

In the case
\underline{\beta}<1
, the first order condition is
\mu^{\underline{\beta}-1}\underline{\beta}\|\bar{w}\|^{2}_{2}-\mu^{-2}\delta_{n}^{2}=0
, suggesting
\mu\asymp\delta_{n}^{\frac{2}{\underline{\beta}+1}}
,
R_{n}\asymp\mu^{\underline{\beta}+1}+\delta_{n}^{2}\asymp\delta_{n}^{2}
, and
\mu^{-1}R_{n}\asymp\delta_{n}^{2-\frac{2}{\underline{\beta}+1}}=\delta_{n}^{\frac{2\underline{\beta}}{\underline{\beta}+1}}
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4
.

By Corollary
5
, the projected rates are
O(\bar{\delta}_{n})
and the mean square rates are
O\left\{\bar{\delta}_{n}^{\textsc{well}(\underline{\beta}_{j})}\right\}
where
\underline{\beta}_{j}=\min(\vec{\beta}_{j})
.
The first product rate condition of Theorem
3
is satisfied when

n^{1/2}\sigma^{-1}\{\|T_{1}(\hat{h}_{1}-h_{1})\|_{2}\|\hat{h}_{4}-h_{4}\|_{2}\wedge\|\hat{h}_{1}-h_{1}\|_{2}\|T_{4}(\hat{h}_{4}-h_{4})\|_{2}\}=o_{p}(1).

The former term is

n^{1/2}\sigma^{-1}\|T_{1}(\hat{h}_{1}-h_{1})\|_{2}\|\hat{h}_{4}-h_{4}\|_{2}=O\left[n^{\frac{1}{2}-\gamma-\alpha\left\{1+\textsc{well}(\underline{\beta}_{4})\right\}}\right].

Meanwhile the latter term is

n^{1/2}\sigma^{-1}\|\hat{h}_{1}-h_{1}\|_{2}\|T_{4}(\hat{h}_{4}-h_{4})\|_{2}=O\left[n^{\frac{1}{2}-\gamma-\alpha\left\{\textsc{well}(\underline{\beta}_{1})+1\right\}}\right].

In summary, the first product rate condition requires

\left[1/2-\gamma-\alpha\left\{1+\textsc{well}(\underline{\beta}_{4})\right\}\right]\wedge\left[1/2-\gamma-\alpha\left\{\textsc{well}(\underline{\beta}_{1})+1\right\}\right]<0.

Rearranging,

\frac{1}{2}<\gamma+\alpha\left[\left\{\textsc{well}(\underline{\beta}_{1})+1\right\}\vee\left\{\textsc{well}(\underline{\beta}_{4})+1\right\}\right].

The other product rate conditions are similar.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3
.

The argument is similar to Proposition
4
, however product rate conditions are of the form
n^{1/2}\sigma^{-1}\|\hat{h}_{1}-h_{1}\|_{2}\|\hat{h}_{4}-h_{4}\|_{2}=o_{p}(1)
, leading to
\frac{1}{2}<\gamma+\alpha\left\{\textsc{well}(\underline{\beta}_{1})+\textsc{well}(\underline{\beta}_{4})\right\}
. For causal scalars,
\gamma=0
. At best,
\alpha=\textsc{well}(\underline{\beta}_{1})=\textsc{well}(\underline{\beta}_{4})=1/2
, so the inequality fails.
∎

Appendix
\thechapter
.I
Simulation and application details

\thechapter
.I.1
Nested NPIV design

Let
g_{0}(A)=A_{1}^{3}
and
h_{0}(B)=f(B_{1})
, where
f:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is one of four possible functions from
Dikkala et al., 2020
.
For simplicity, we write
p=dim(A)=dim(B)=dim(C)=dim(C^{\prime})
. We define
F:(x_{1},...,x_{p})\mapsto(x_{1}^{1/3},...,x_{p}^{1/3})
and
1_{p}=(1,...,1)^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
.

Each observation is generated as follows. Independently draw the instruments
C\sim\mathcal{N}(0,...I_{p})
and
C^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,...I_{p})
. Next draw the noise terms
U\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
,
U_{A}\sim\mathcal{N}\{0,\min(1,|C_{1}|)\}
,
U_{B}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,0.1)
, and
U_{Y}\sim\mathcal{N}\{0,\min(1,|C_{1}^{\prime}|)\}
.
Finally, set
B=C+1_{p}\cdot U+1_{p}\cdot U_{B}
,
A=F\{h_{0}(B)\cdot 1_{p}+U\cdot 1_{p}+C^{\prime}+U_{A}\cdot 1_{p}\}
, and
Y=g_{0}(A)+U+U_{Y}
.

Proposition
\thechapter
.I.1

(Nested NPIV simulation)
.

The nested NPIV simulation design satisfies
\mathbb{E}\{h_{0}(B)|C\}=\mathbb{E}\{g_{0}(A)|C\}
and
\mathbb{E}\{g_{0}(A)|C^{\prime}\}=\mathbb{E}(Y|C^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Clearly
\mathbb{E}\{Y-g_{0}(A)|C^{\prime}\}=\mathbb{E}\{U+U_{Y}|C^{\prime}\}=0
. Moreover,
\mathbb{E}\{g_{0}(A)-h_{0}(B)|C\}=\mathbb{E}\{A_{1}^{3}-h_{0}(B)|C^{\prime}\}=\mathbb{E}\{U+C_{1}^{\prime}+U_{A}|C\}=0
.
∎

\thechapter
.I.2
Coverage design

To begin, we recap the linear design of
Dukes et al., 2023
in our notation. Each variable is a scalar except for
X\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Each observation is generated as follows:

1.

(X_{1},X_{2},U)^{\top}\sim\mathcal{N}\{(0.25,0.25,0)^{\top},\Sigma\}
where
\Sigma=\begin{pmatrix}0.25&0.00&0.05\\
0.00&0.25&0.05\\
0.05&0.05&1.00\end{pmatrix}
;

2.

D|X,U\sim\textsc{Bernoulli}[1+\exp\{(0.5,0.5)^{\top}X+0.4U\}]^{-1}
;

3.

Z|X,D,U\sim\mathcal{N}\{0.2-0.52D+(0.2,0.2)^{\top}X-U,1\}
;

4.

W|X,U\sim\mathcal{N}\{0.3+(0.2,0.2)^{\top}X-0.6U,1\})
;

5.

M|X,D,U\sim\mathcal{N}\{-0.3D-(0.5,0.5)^{\top}X+0.4U,1\}
;

6.

Y|X,D,M,W,U=2+2D+M+2W-(1,1)^{\top}X-U+2\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.

Recall that
h_{0}
is an outcome confounding bridge that solves
\mathbb{E}\{h(X,D,W)|X,Z,D=0\}=\mathbb{E}\{g_{0}(X,1,M,W)|X,Z,D=0\}
, where
g_{0}
solves

\mathbb{E}\{g(X,D,M,W)|X,Z,D=1,M\}=\mathbb{E}(Y|X,Z,D=1,M)
.

Recall that
h_{0}^{\prime}
is a treatment confounding bridge that solves

\mathbb{E}\{h^{\prime}(X,Z,D,M)|X,D=1,M,W\}=\mathbb{E}\left\{g_{0}^{\prime}(X,Z,0)\frac{\mathbb{P}(D=0|X,M,W)}{\mathbb{P}(D=1|X,M,W)}|X,D=1,M,W\right\}
,
where
g_{0}^{\prime}
solves

\mathbb{E}\{g^{\prime}(X,Z,D)|X,D=0,W\}=\mathbb{E}\left\{\frac{1}{\mathbb{P}(D=0|X,W)}|X,D=0,W\right\}
.

We write the nuisances as

h_{1}(X,W)=h_{0}(X,0,W)
,

h_{2}(X,M,W)=g_{0}(X,1,M,W)
,

h_{3}(X,Z,D,M)=1_{D=1}h_{0}^{\prime}(X,Z,1,M)
, and

h_{4}(X,Z,D)=1_{D=0}g_{0}^{\prime}(X,Z,0)
.

Consider the notation
\mathbb{E}(A|B,C)=\beta_{A0}+\beta_{AB}B+\beta_{AC}C
and
\mathbb{V}(A|B,C)=v^{2}_{A|B,C}
. Also define
\mathbb{P}(D=0|X,U)=[1+\exp\{-(\pi_{0}+\pi_{X}^{\top}X+\pi_{U}U)\}]^{-1}
and
\log\left\{\frac{\mathbb{P}(D=0|X,M,U)}{\mathbb{P}(D=1|X,M,U)}\right\}=\rho_{0}+\rho_{X}^{\top}X+\rho_{M}M+\rho_{U}U.

Proposition
\thechapter
.I.2

(Linear coverage simulation; c.f. Supplementary Material of
Dukes et al., 2023
)
.

The linear design for coverage simulations satisfies

\displaystyle h_{1}(X,W)

\displaystyle=\nu_{0}+\nu_{X}^{\top}X+\nu_{W}W,

\displaystyle h_{2}(X,M,W)

\displaystyle=\delta_{0}+\delta_{X}^{\top}X+\delta_{M}M+\delta_{W}W,

\displaystyle h_{3}(X,Z,D,M)

\displaystyle=1_{D=1}\left[1+\exp\left\{-(\eta_{0}+\eta_{X}^{\top}X+\eta_{Z}Z)\right\}\right]\exp\left(\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{X}^{\top}X+\alpha_{Z}Z+\alpha_{M}M\right),

\displaystyle h_{4}(X,Z,D)

\displaystyle=1_{D=0}\left[1+\exp\left\{-(\eta_{0}+\eta_{X}^{\top}X+\eta_{Z}Z)\right\}\right],

where
(\nu,\delta,\alpha,\eta)
can be expressed in terms of
(\beta,v,\pi,\rho)
as follows:

\displaystyle(\nu_{0},\nu_{X}^{\top},\nu_{W})

\displaystyle=\left(\delta_{0}+\delta_{M}\beta_{M0}+\delta_{W}\beta_{W0}-\nu_{W}\beta_{W0},\delta_{M}\beta_{MX}^{\top}+\delta_{W}\beta_{WX}^{\top}+\delta_{X}^{\top}-\nu_{W}\beta_{WX}^{\top},\frac{\delta_{M}\beta_{MU}+\delta_{W}\beta_{WU}}{\beta_{WU}}\right);

\displaystyle(\delta_{0},\delta_{X}^{\top},\delta_{M},\delta_{W})

\displaystyle=\left(\beta_{Y0}+\beta_{YD}+\beta_{YW}\beta_{W0}-\delta_{W}\beta_{W0},\beta_{YW}\beta_{WX}^{\top}+\beta_{YX}^{\top}-\delta_{W}\beta_{WX}^{\top},\beta_{YM},\frac{\beta_{YW}\beta_{WU}+\beta_{YU}}{\beta_{WU}}\right);

\displaystyle(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{X}^{\top},\alpha_{Z},\alpha_{M})

\displaystyle=\left\{\rho_{0}-\alpha_{Z}(\beta_{Z0}+\beta_{ZD})-\frac{\alpha_{Z}^{2}v^{2}_{Z|D,X,U}}{2},\rho_{X}^{\top}-\alpha_{Z}\beta_{ZX}^{\top},\frac{\rho_{U}}{\beta_{ZU}},\rho_{M}\right\};

\displaystyle(\eta_{0},\eta_{X}^{\top},\eta_{Z})

\displaystyle=\left(\pi_{0}-\eta_{Z}\beta_{Z0}+\frac{\eta_{Z}^{2}v^{2}_{Z|D,X,U}}{2},\pi_{X}^{\top}-\eta_{Z}\beta_{ZX}^{\top},\frac{\pi_{U}}{\beta_{ZU}}\right).

Moreover,
\rho
can be expressed in terms of
(\beta,v,\pi)
as

\displaystyle(\rho_{0},\rho_{X}^{\top},\rho_{M},\rho_{U})

\displaystyle=\left\{\frac{\beta_{MD}}{v^{2}_{M|D,X,U}}(\beta_{M0}+\beta_{MD}/2)+\pi_{0},\frac{\beta_{MD}}{v^{2}_{M|D,X,U}}\beta_{MX}^{\top}+\pi_{X}^{\top},\frac{-\beta_{MD}}{v^{2}_{M|D,X,U}},\frac{\beta_{MD}}{v^{2}_{M|D,X,U}}\beta_{MU}+\pi_{U})\right\}.

Finally, in this design,
\theta_{0}=4.05
.

Next, we extend the linear design to a nonlinear design. Instead of observing
(X_{1},X_{2},Z,D,M,Y,W)
, we observe
(\tilde{X}_{1},\tilde{X}_{2},\tilde{Z},\tilde{D},\tilde{M},\tilde{Y},\tilde{W})=\{f(X_{1}),f(X_{2}),f(Z),D,f(M),Y,f(W)\}
where
f:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is one of four possible functions.
Let
\tilde{\theta}_{0}
be the causal parameter in the nonlinear design, and let
(\tilde{h}_{1},\tilde{h}_{2},\tilde{h}_{3},\tilde{h}_{4})
be the nuisances.

Proposition
\thechapter
.I.3

(Nonlinear coverage simulation)
.

The nonlinear design for coverage simulations satisfies
\tilde{h}_{1}(\tilde{X}_{1},\tilde{X}_{2},\tilde{W})=h_{1}\{f^{-1}(\tilde{X}_{1}),f^{-1}(\tilde{X}_{2}),f^{-1}(\tilde{W})\}
,

\displaystyle\tilde{h}_{2}(\tilde{X}_{1},\tilde{X}_{2},\tilde{M},\tilde{W})

\displaystyle=h_{2}\{f^{-1}(\tilde{X}_{1}),f^{-1}(\tilde{X}_{2}),f^{-1}(\tilde{M}),f^{-1}(\tilde{W})\},

\displaystyle\tilde{h}_{3}(\tilde{X}_{1},\tilde{X}_{2},\tilde{Z},\tilde{D},\tilde{M})

\displaystyle=h_{3}\{f^{-1}(\tilde{X}_{1}),f^{-1}(\tilde{X}_{2}),f^{-1}(\tilde{Z}),\tilde{D},f^{-1}(\tilde{M})\},

\displaystyle\tilde{h}_{4}(\tilde{X}_{1},\tilde{X}_{2},\tilde{Z},\tilde{D})

\displaystyle=h_{4}\{f^{-1}(\tilde{X}_{1}),f^{-1}(\tilde{X}_{2}),f^{-1}(\tilde{Z}),\tilde{D}\},

where
(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4})
are characterized in Proposition
\thechapter.I.2
. Hence
\tilde{h}_{1}
is linear in
f^{-1}(\tilde{X})
yet nonlinear in
\tilde{X}
, and so on. In this design,
\tilde{\theta}_{0}=4.05
.

Proof.

By Proposition
\thechapter.I.2
,
0=\mathbb{E}\{Y-h_{2}(X,M,W)|X,Z,D=1,M\}
, which equals

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Y-h_{2}\{f^{-1}(\tilde{X}_{1}),f^{-1}(\tilde{X}_{2}),f^{-1}(\tilde{M}),f^{-1}(\tilde{W})\}|f^{-1}(\tilde{X}_{1}),f^{-1}(\tilde{X}_{2}),f^{-1}(\tilde{Z}),\tilde{D}=1,f^{-1}(\tilde{M})]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\{Y-\tilde{h}_{2}(\tilde{X}_{1},\tilde{X}_{2},\tilde{Z},\tilde{D})|\tilde{X}_{1},\tilde{X}_{2},\tilde{Z},\tilde{D}=1,\tilde{M}\}

and similarly for the other bridge functions.
∎

\thechapter
.I.3
Implementation details

All estimators are implemented in a
python
package called
nnpiv
that accompanies the paper as supplementary material. In general, we follow the standard tuning procedures of previous work on NPIV
(
Dikkala et al., 2020
;
Bennett et al., 2020
)
.

Benchmark: 2SLS.
We employ an iterated two stage least squares (2SLS) method using the command
tsls.tsls()
. Sequential and simultaneous estimation coincide.

Bechmark: Series.
We implement the iterated 2SLS method after applying a cubic transformation to the data. Again, sequential and simultaneous estimation coincide.

Benchmark: Regularized series.
In addition to the cubic transformation, we also introduce regularization via
ElasticNetCV
with cross-validation (
cv=3
). This method blends both the
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
penalties of the lasso and ridge. We implement it in the command
tsls.regtsls()
.

Proposal: RKHS.
Our estimator has a closed form solution, derived in Appendix
\thechapter.J.1
and implemented via the command
rkhsiv.RKHSIVL2
. We use the Gaussian kernel, which has a hyperparameter called the lengthscale controlling the width of the kernel, and hence its sensitivity to local variations. We set the lengthscale by cross validation, which gives the value
0.0013
. We set the ridge regularization parameters
(\mu,\mu^{\prime})=n^{-1.6}
, following the choice of
Dikkala et al., 2020
.

Proposal: Neural network.
We optimize the objective using the optimistic Adam algorithm of
Daskalakis et al., 2017
, as described in Appendix
\thechapter.J.2
and implemented via the command
agmm2.AGMM2L2(learnerh, learnerg, adversary1, adversary2)
.
Following
Dikkala et al., 2020
;
Bennett et al., 2020
, the architecture consists of fully connected layers with dropout layers throughout to prevent overfitting, with a dropout probability of
0.1
, hidden layer width set to
100
, and
LeakyReLU
activation for both the learner and the adversary models. Following
Dikkala et al., 2020
;
Bennett et al., 2020
, the learning rates for both the learner and adversary are set to
1\times 10^{-4}
, with a weight-decay parameter of
1\times 10^{-3}
for the learner and
1\times 10^{-4}
for the adversary. The ridge regularization parameters are
(\mu,\mu^{\prime})=1\times 10^{-12}
, similar to those works. The model is trained with a batch size of
100
samples.
Since we consider joint estimation of
(\hat{h},\hat{g})
, we double the number of epochs of
Dikkala et al., 2020
to
600
.
The final neural network model is computed by averaging models, burning in the initial
200
epochs to allow the model to stabilize.

Standard practice: Debiased machine learning.
In Algorithm
3
, we use five folds. When using neural networks, we reduce the number of epochs to
500
because the inference process is computationally expensive. We increase the burn-in period to
400
epochs to enhance model stability, particularly for accurate estimation of the treatment bridge function.

Computational resources.
The simulations were run on a cluster partition with
2
nodes, each equipped with
110
Intel "Sapphire Rapids" CPUs and
600
GB of memory. For the neural network, we utilized a separate cluster partition with
64
Intel "Ice Lake" CPUs,
4
NVIDIA A100 GPUs per node, and
600
GB of memory.

\thechapter
.I.4
Proxy mediation analysis of US Job Corps

We directly extend the real world application of
Dukes et al., 2023
, from parametric estimation to semiparametric estimation. In the Job Corps study, participants were randomized to receive eligibility for the Job Corps job training program. The treatment is whether an individual received job training in the first year after randomization. The mediator is the fraction of weeks employed in the second year after randomization. The outcome is the number of arrests in the fourth year after randomization.
The sample consists of
10,775
participants, with
257
participants removed from the analysis due to missing mediator information.

The parametric estimate is derived from
Dukes et al., 2023
.

For the RKHS estimate, we use the
RKHS2IVL2
command with a Gaussian kernel and lengthscale
0.1
, chosen by cross validation. The ridge regularization is tuned as in the simulations.

For the neural network estimate, the learning and regularization parameters are set as in the simulations. Since the application is less computationally intensive than the coverage simulations, we increase the hidden layer width to
400
. The final model is computed using
500
epochs, with a burn-in period of
350
epochs.

\thechapter
.I.5
Heterogeneous long term effects of Project STAR

We directly extend the real world application of
Athey et al., 2020
, from average long term effects to heterogeneous long term effects. We use data from the Tennessee Student Teacher
Achievement Ratio experiment (Project STAR), where each student was randomly assigned to either a small or regular kindergarten class. The short term outcome is the third grade test score. Observational data come from the New York City (NYC) public school system, and include the same variables, along with fourth to eighth grade test scores.

The full sample consists of
11,600
students:
3,497
from STAR, and
8,103
from NYC. Due to missing values in the prior ability variable, analysis of heterogeneous long term effects was conducted on
1,954
students from STAR, and
8,103
students from NYC. We refer to the subpopulation with non-missing prior ability as the selected sample. Figure
4
illustrates the average long term effects for this subpopulation, which are lower than the average long term effects for the full population reported in Section
6
.

We compute localization weights to assess heterogeneous effects with respect to prior ability
X_{1}
. For the localization, we use a Gaussian kernel with bandwidth chosen according to Silverman’s rule:
\lambda=0.9\min\left(\hat{\sigma}_{X_{1}},\frac{\textsc{IQR}_{X_{1}}}{1.34}\right)n^{-\frac{1}{5}}
, where
\hat{\sigma}_{X_{1}}
is the standard deviation and
\textsc{IQR}_{X_{1}}
is the interquantile range of
X_{1}
.

Oracle average long term effects are estimated as in
Athey et al., 2020
. Oracle heterogeneous long term effects are localized averages thereof.

(a)

RKHS

(b)

Neural network

Figure 4
:
Average long term treatment effect over different horizons for selected sample

For our RKHS proposal, we use the Gaussian kernel. The lengthscale is chosen in a data driven way, following the median distance heuristic. For our neural network proposal, we choose the same hyperparameters as in the simulations.

Section
6
reports heterogeneous long term effects of small kindergarten class size on seventh grade test scores. Figure
5
reports heterogeneous long term effects of small kindergarten class size over different horizons: third through eight grade test scores. Across horizons and function spaces, our proposals recover the oracle well.
